Outline.

The remainder of this work systematically analyses GW signatures from realistic PBH populations in the early Universe. Sec.
2.1
presents the background dynamics of PBH-dominated scenarios. Sec.
2.2
introduces the PBH gas formed from super-horizon collapse and derives the resulting extended mass distribution using Press–Schechter statistics and peak theory. Sec.
3
derives the transfer functions and suppression factors of the cosmological perturbations that source the GWs. Sec.
4
defines the framework for SIGW production and computes the signal from PBH evaporation in Sec.
5
. Sec.
4.4
addresses the sensitivity of the SIGW signal to the UV cut-off of the scalar power spectrum. We then turn to complementary sources of gravitational waves, namely SIGWs sourced during the early matter-dominated phase (Sec.
6
), during the early radiation era (Sec.
7
), from direct enhancement of the curvature spectrum at PBH formation (Sec.
8
), and high-frequency GWs from PBH mergers and Hawking emission (Sec.
9
). Finally, we conclude. A list of the symbols used throughout this work is provided in Tab.
2
.

2
Background cosmology of evaporating PBHs

We briefly review the background cosmology relevant for primordial black
holes (PBHs).
On sufficiently large scales, the Universe is well described by a spatially flat, homogeneous, and isotropic Friedmann–Lemaître–Robertson–Walker (FLRW) spacetime. In conformal time
\eta
, the line element takes the form

\mathrm{d}s^{2}=a^{2}(\eta)\left(-\mathrm{d}\eta^{2}+\delta_{ij}\,\mathrm{d}x^{i}\mathrm{d}x^{j}\right),

(4)

where
a(\eta)
is the scale factor. The conformal time
\eta
is related to the cosmic time
t
by

\mathrm{d}\eta\equiv\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{a(t)}.

(5)

Derivatives with respect to
\eta
are denoted by a prime.
The expansion rate is characterized by the Hubble parameter
H
and the
conformal Hubble parameter
\mathcal{H}
, defined respectively by

H\equiv\frac{\dot{a}}{a},\qquad\mathcal{H}\equiv\frac{a^{\prime}}{a}=aH.

(6)

The comoving Hubble horizon is then defined as the inverse conformal
Hubble parameter,
\mathcal{H}^{-1}
, which sets the characteristic scale
of causal connectivity in comoving coordinates.
For a homogeneous and isotropic Universe filled with a perfect fluid of
equation-of-state parameter
\omega\equiv\overline{p}/\overline{\rho}
,
the Einstein equations reduce to the standard Friedmann and continuity
relations,

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{2}=\frac{8\pi G}{3}\overline{\rho}a^{2},\qquad\mathcal{H}^{\prime}=-\frac{1+3\omega}{2}\mathcal{H}^{2},\qquad\overline{\rho}^{\prime}+3(1+\omega)\mathcal{H}\overline{\rho}=0.

(7)

2.1
From formation to evaporation

Black Hole Properties and Evaporation

Once formed, primordial black holes act as pressureless matter on large
scales and can be described as a non-relativistic fluid with average
energy density
\rho_{\rm{PBH}}
. As such, they redshift like ordinary
matter. However, unlike stable cold dark matter, PBHs lose mass through Hawking
evaporation
[
191
]
, eventually disappearing altogether.
Expressed in conformal time, the associated mass loss is described by the
logarithmic rate
2
2

2

Here, we adopt Hawking’s
standard evaporation formula, disregarding potential quantum gravity
effects such as the memory burden mechanism

[
121
,
119
,
120
]
, which has been proposed
as a means to avoid information loss. This effect can stabilize the
black hole once it has lost approximately half of its initial mass,
thereby significantly extending its lifetime. As a consequence, the
range of PBH parameters consistent with evaporation before BBN would be
substantially modified.

\Gamma(\eta;M_{\rm{PBH}})\equiv-\,\frac{{\rm d}\ln M_{\rm{PBH}}}{{\rm d}\eta}=a(\eta)\,\frac{A\,M_{\rm pl}^{4}}{M_{\rm{PBH}}^{3}(\eta)},\qquad A=\frac{\pi\,\mathcal{G}}{480}\,g_{{\rm H}\star}\simeq 2.7,

(8)

where the greybody factor
\mathcal{G}\simeq 3.8
captures the partial
back-scattering of Hawking radiation by gravitational and centrifugal
potentials, as computed in

[
290
,
289
,
291
,
262
,
263
]

and revisited in
[
34
,
77
]
. The quantity

g_{{\rm H}\star}(T_{\rm{PBH}})
accounts for the number of spin-weighted
degrees of freedom accessible at the Hawking–Bekenstein temperature

[
47
,
192
]

T_{\rm{PBH}}\equiv\frac{M_{\rm pl}^{2}}{M_{\rm{PBH}}}\simeq 100~{\rm GeV}\left(\frac{10^{11}~\rm g}{M_{\rm{PBH}}}\right).

(9)

In the regime of interest in which
M_{\rm{PBH}}\lesssim 10^{11}~\rm g

and for the particle spectrum predicted in the Standard Model, we have

g_{{\rm H}\star}(T_{\rm{PBH}})\simeq 108

[
202
]
.
Moreover, we assume the absence of heavier BSM particles that would
increase this number close to final evaporation, when the PBH
temperature increases rapidly. Throughout this work we use the reduced Planck mass

M_{\rm pl}\simeq 2.44\times 10^{18}~{\rm GeV}
.
For simplicity, PBHs are assumed to be non-rotating.
This approximation is well justified for PBHs formed via superhorizon
collapse, for which the resulting spin distribution peaks well below
the percent level,
\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{R}}<10^{-2}

[
92
]
. Integrating the mass-loss equation yields the time evolution of the PBH mass
3
3

3

This is an idealized scenario in which mass growth from accretion is neglected. Recent studies
[
181
,
225
]
indicate that accretion can lead to substantial PBH mass growth for sufficiently large collapse fractions
\gamma_{H}
. The two analyses, however, find different critical values,
\gamma_{H,c}=0.395
and
0.495
, at which the absorption rate enters a runaway regime that formally leads to divergent mass growth. As a result, the precise onset of absorption dominance remains uncertain. In Sec.
2.2
we find
\gamma_{H}\simeq[0.2,4]
depending on the width of the primordial power spectrum and on the time at which we evaluate
\gamma_{\rm H}
, see Tab.
5
. Unlike the analyses of Refs.
[
181
,
225
]
, which treat the radiation bath as an external reservoir with fixed temperature, realistic scenarios—particularly those involving ultra-light PBHs that temporarily dominate the cosmic energy budget—do not admit an infinite heat source: the radiation component becomes subdominant due to differential redshifting and partial absorption. We therefore expect absorption effects to modify the initial PBH masses by an
\mathcal{O}(1)
factor, without qualitatively altering the overall picture, which may be captured by the mapping
M_{{\rm PBH},f}\rightarrow M_{{\rm PBH},f}+M_{{\rm PBH,abs}}
.

M_{\rm{PBH}}(t)=M_{\rm PBH,f}\left(1-\frac{t}{t_{\rm eva}}\right)^{1/3},

(10)

where
M_{\rm PBH,f}
denotes the PBH mass at formation and
t_{\rm eva}

is the total PBH lifetime,

t_{\rm eva}=\frac{M_{\rm PBH,f}^{3}}{3AM_{\rm pl}^{4}}\simeq 0.41~{\rm s}\left(\frac{108}{g_{{\rm H}\star}}\right)\left(\frac{M_{\rm PBH,f}}{10^{9}~{\rm g}}\right)^{3}.

(11)

Using the conformal-time definition in Eq. (
8
), the
evaporation rate can equivalently be expressed as

\Gamma(\eta;M_{\rm{PBH}})=\frac{a(\eta)}{3\,[t_{\rm eva}-t(\eta)]},

(12)

where
t(\eta)
is the cosmic time corresponding to conformal time

\eta
, and
\eta_{\rm eva}
is defined by
t(\eta_{\rm eva})=t_{\rm eva}
.
For notational simplicity, we will denote the formation mass

M_{\rm PBH,f}
by
M
in the following.

Figure 3:
Evolution of the PBH and radiation energy fractions,
\Omega_{\rm{PBH}}
and
\Omega_{\rm r}
. PBHs redshift more slowly than radiation, leading to the onset of PBH domination at
\eta_{\rm eq}
, defined by
\Omega_{\rm{PBH}}=\Omega_{\rm r}
. This is followed by an early matter-dominated era, which ends at
\eta_{\rm eva}
when PBHs evaporate and reheat the Universe.

Cosmological History

We focus on ultra-light PBHs that evaporate prior to Big-Bang Nucleosynthesis and assume a monochromatic mass function characterized by a single mass scale
M_{\rm{PBH}}
, which we identify with the average PBH mass at formation. PBHs form during radiation domination when overdensities re-enter the horizon, with a mass set by the horizon mass at that time and controlled by an
\mathcal{O}(1)
collapse efficiency factor
\gamma_{\rm H}
. Their subsequent evolution is governed by Hawking evaporation. Consistency with the maximal temperature attained after inflation imposes a lower bound on the PBH mass, while the requirement that evaporation occur before BBN selects the ultra-light window relevant for this work,

1~{\rm g}\;\lesssim\;M_{\rm{PBH}}\;\lesssim\;10^{9}~{\rm g}.

(13)

If the initial PBH energy fraction at formation,
\beta_{f}
, exceeds a critical value
\beta_{c}
, PBHs come to dominate the energy density before evaporating, giving rise to an early matter-dominated epoch
4
4

4

For the sake of simplicity, we omit in this equation and the ones below the factors involving ratios of relativistic degrees of freedom, which are reported in App.
A
.

\beta_{f}>\beta_{c}\simeq 2.5\times 10^{-14}\gamma_{\rm H}^{-1/2}\left(\frac{10^{8}{\rm g}}{M_{{\rm PBH}}}\right)\qquad\Rightarrow\qquad{\rm\textbf{eMD}}

(14)

This phase begins at conformal time
\eta_{\rm eq}
, when the PBH and radiation energy densities become equal, and ends at
\eta_{\rm eva}
, when PBHs evaporate. We show the evolution of relative matter and density energy fractions in Fig.
3
and characterize the duration of the eMD between equality and evaporation by the number of e-folds,

N_{\rm MD}\;\equiv\;\ln\!\left(\frac{a(\eta_{\rm eva})}{a(\eta_{\rm eq})}\right),

(15)

which depends primarily on
\beta_{f}
and
M_{{\rm PBH}}
.

PBH evaporation reheats the Universe and injects entropy into the thermal bath, diluting any pre-existing relic abundances. The associated entropy injection can be summarized by a dilution factor

D\equiv\frac{S(T_{\rm eva})}{S(T_{\rm eva}^{-})}\simeq\frac{\beta_{f}}{\beta_{\rm c}}\simeq\exp\left(\frac{3}{4}N_{\rm MD}\right).

(16)

In the opposite regime,
\beta_{f}<\beta_{c}
, PBHs never dominate the energy density, the eMD phase is absent, and one recovers
D=1
, corresponding to standard adiabatic expansion.

Figure 4:
Schematic illustration of the characteristic comoving scales during a PBH-dominated epoch in the early Universe. The comoving volume is populated by an ensemble of randomly distributed PBHs, shown as black dots. Indicated are the PBH separation cutoff
k_{\rm{PBH}}^{-1}
, together with the formation scale
k_{f}^{-1}
, the equality scale
k_{\rm eq}^{-1}
, and the evaporation scale
k_{\rm eva}^{-1}
, corresponding to the Hubble horizon at PBH formation, early matter–radiation equality, and reheating from Hawking evaporation, respectively.
k_{f}^{-1}
is therefore associated with the horizon crossing of a sufficiently large overdensity for PBH formation and hence the comoving size of the collapsing region (denoted by inward arrows). As the comoving Hubble radius grows (denoted by outward arrows), these characteristic scales increase accordingly.

Characteristic comoving scales

Here, we summarize the physical interpretation of the characteristic scales associated with the formation, evolution, and evaporation of PBHs. The goal is to provide an intuitive overview of their meaning and mutual relations, while retaining only the final expressions required for phenomenological applications. All technical derivations including degree-of-freedom factors can be found in App.
A
.

Formation scale

\mathbf{k_{f}}
The formation scale corresponds to the comoving size of the Hubble horizon,
k_{f}=\mathcal{H}_{f}
, at the time when a sufficiently large overdensity re-enters the horizon and collapses to form a PBH. It therefore also represents the comoving size of the collapsing region itself. This scale is directly tied to the PBH mass through the horizon mass
M_{{\rm H},f}
and the scale factor
a_{f}\equiv a(\eta_{f})
at formation,

M_{{\rm PBH},f}\equiv\gamma_{\rm H}M_{{\rm H},f},\qquad\textrm{with}\quad M_{\rm H,f}=a_{f}\frac{4\pi M_{\rm pl}^{2}}{\mathcal{H}_{f}}.

(17)

PBH scale

\mathbf{k_{PBH}}

This scale is set by the mean comoving separation between PBHs at formation. On scales smaller than
k_{\rm{PBH}}^{-1}
the discrete nature of the PBH distribution becomes important, and a fluid description of the PBH ensemble breaks down. Up to mild dependence on relativistic degrees of freedom, the corresponding comoving wavenumber is given by

k_{\rm{PBH}}\simeq 4.9\times 10^{14}~{\rm Mpc}^{-1}\left(\frac{10^{8}~{\rm g}}{M_{\rm{PBH}}}\right)^{5/6}.

(18)

Equality scale

\mathbf{k_{eq}}

If the initial PBH energy fraction at formation
\beta_{f}
exceeds a critical value, PBHs come to dominate the energy density of the Universe prior to their evaporation, leading to an early matter-dominated phase. The scale
k_{\rm eq}^{-1}
corresponds to the Hubble horizon at the onset of this PBH-dominated era. Up to mild corrections (
c_{1}=\mathcal{O}(1)
from relativistic dofs), one finds relative to the PBH scale

\frac{k_{\rm eq}}{k_{\rm{PBH}}}\simeq\sqrt{2}\gamma_{\rm H}^{1/3}\beta_{f}^{2/3}.

(19)

Evaporation scale

\mathbf{k_{eva}}

The evaporation scale is defined as the comoving Hubble radius at the time when PBHs complete their Hawking evaporation and reheat the Universe. The evaporation scale is set by the PBH mass as

k_{\rm eva}\simeq 4.2\times 10^{5}~{\rm Mpc}^{-1}\left(\frac{10^{8}~{\rm g}}{M_{\rm{PBH}}}\right)^{3/2}.

(20)

Since the comoving Hubble radius grows with time, the relative ordering of these scales obeys the following hierarchy:

k_{\rm eva}\ll k_{\rm eq}\ll k_{\rm{PBH}}\ll k_{f}.

(21)

Fig.
4
provides a schematic overview of the physical
origin of the characteristic comoving scales introduced above, and
illustrates their relative hierarchy in cosmic time.

2.2
PBH mass distribution

This subsection summarizes the ingredients of the PBH mass distribution underlying our analysis. A complete derivation, the explicit fitting formula for the geometric factor
\kappa
defined below, and a detailed comparison of analytic approaches are presented in App.
B
.

Figure 5:
Schematic comparison of the Press–Schechter and peak-theory prescriptions for primordial black hole formation. In both panels,
\delta_{R}
denotes the density contrast smoothed on a fixed scale
R=\mathcal{H}^{-1}
.
Left:
Press–Schechter estimates the PBH abundance from the volume fraction of regions in which
\delta_{R}
exceeds the collapse threshold
\delta_{c}
, treating all such regions on the same footing.
Right:
peak theory identifies PBH formation sites with local maxima of
\delta_{R}
above threshold.

PBHs are assumed to form during radiation domination from the collapse of large overdensities as they re-enter the Hubble horizon
[
69
]
. Their abundance is characterized by the energy fraction
\beta
, defined as the fraction of the total energy density converted into PBHs. To track the distribution over masses we use the differential quantity

\frac{d\beta(M_{\rm{PBH}})}{d\ln M_{\rm{PBH}}},

(22)

which gives how the PBH energy density is distributed over masses at formation. The normalized PBH mass function is then

\psi_{f}(M_{\rm{PBH}})\equiv\frac{1}{\beta_{f}}\frac{d\beta(M_{\rm{PBH}},T_{f})}{d\ln M_{\rm{PBH}}},\qquad\int d\ln M_{\rm{PBH}}\,\psi_{f}(M_{\rm{PBH}})=1,

(23)

where
\beta_{f}
is the total PBH energy fraction at formation.
The PBH mass spectrum is set by the statistics of the primordial curvature perturbations, encoded in
\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{R}}(k)
. Throughout this work we adopt the log-normal parametrization

\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{R}}(k)=\frac{\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{R}}}{\sqrt{2\pi}\,\Delta}\exp\!\left[-\frac{\ln^{2}(k/k_{f})}{2\Delta^{2}}\right],

(24)

peaked at the scale
k_{f}
with width
\Delta
, and we assume Gaussian statistics. The horizon mass evaluated when this peak scale crosses the horizon is

M_{f}\equiv M_{H}(\mathcal{H}=k_{f}).

(25)

Near the collapse threshold, the PBH mass is not fixed only by the horizon mass. Numerical simulations of gravitational collapse show that, for a one-parameter family of initial data labeled by an amplitude
p
, there exists a critical value
p_{c}
separating dispersion from black-hole formation
[
81
,
132
,
287
,
288
]
. For
p>p_{c}
and close to threshold, the solution first approaches a universal self-similar critical solution. This critical solution has one unstable mode. The time spent near it is therefore controlled by the distance to threshold, and the final black-hole mass obeys the universal scaling law
[
81
,
132
,
267
,
177
]

{\it Choptuik^{\prime}s~law:}\qquad\qquad M_{\rm{PBH}}=\mathcal{K}\,M_{H}\,(\delta_{m}-\delta_{c})^{\gamma_{\rm M}}.

(26)

Here
\delta_{m}
is the smoothed density contrast at horizon crossing,
\delta_{c}
is the collapse threshold,
\mathcal{K}\sim 4
is a coefficient, and
\gamma_{\rm M}
is the critical exponent. The scaling in Eq. (
26
) relies on three assumptions: the collapsing region is approximately spherically symmetric, the perturbation is close to threshold,
\delta_{m}-\delta_{c}\ll\delta_{c}
, and the equation of state is fixed during collapse. The exponent
\gamma_{\rm M}
is universal for a given matter content, while
\delta_{c}
and
\mathcal{K}
depend on the perturbation profile. For radiation domination one finds
\gamma_{\rm M}\simeq 0.36

[
287
,
288
,
340
]
, while typical values of the threshold lie in the range
\delta_{c}\in[0.40,0.67]

[
284
,
126
]
.
An important subtlety is that the horizon mass entering Eq. (
26
) should be associated with the real-space scale controlling collapse, namely the radius
r_{m}
at which the compaction function is maximal, rather than directly with the Fourier scale
k_{f}^{-1}

[
318
,
284
]
. We therefore define

M_{H}\equiv M_{H}(r_{m})\equiv M_{H}(\mathcal{H}=1/r_{m}).

(27)

where
r_{m}
is the radius at which the compaction function is maximal. The compaction function
\mathcal{C}=2\delta M/R
measures the excess mass inside a spherical region normalized by its areal radius, and it is this scale rather than the wavelength
k_{f}^{-1}
that controls the collapse
[
318
,
186
,
283
,
284
,
341
,
128
,
129
,
282
]
.
The ratio between the two reference scales is parametrized by
[
282
,
148
,
135
,
150
]

\kappa\equiv k_{f}r_{m},

(28)

which for the log-normal spectrum of Eq. (
24
) depends only on the width
\Delta
. An explicit fitting formula is given in Eq. (
294
) of App.
B
, and representative values for the widths used in this work are reported in Tab.
5
. Since PBHs form during radiation domination,
M_{H}\propto\mathcal{H}^{-2}
, and therefore

M_{H}(r_{m})=M_{H}(\mathcal{H}=k_{f}/\kappa)=\kappa^{2}M_{f}.

(29)

With these geometric factors in hand, we turn to the statistical computation of the mass fraction. Two analytic frameworks are commonly used to compute the PBH abundance from
\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{R}}(k)
, namely the Press–Schechter (PS) formalism
[
303
,
58
]
and peak theory
[
39
]
, schematically compared in Fig.
5
. In the PS framework, the PBH abundance is estimated from the volume fraction of regions in which the smoothed density contrast exceeds
\delta_{c}
, while peak theory instead counts local maxima above threshold. Despite these different counting prescriptions, both approaches yield the same infrared tail of the logarithmic mass function
[
288
,
173
,
341
,
171
]
,

\psi_{f}(M_{\rm{PBH}})\propto M_{\rm{PBH}}^{1+1/\gamma_{\rm M}}.

(30)

This scaling refers to
d\beta/d\ln M_{\rm{PBH}}
, whereas a mass function defined per unit mass,
d\beta/dM_{\rm{PBH}}
, scales instead as
M_{\rm{PBH}}^{1/\gamma_{\rm M}}
. The ultraviolet part of the distribution, including the precise position and shape of the high-mass cutoff, is model dependent
[
287
,
340
,
73
,
227
]
.
This behavior is illustrated in Fig.
6
, which shows
\psi_{f}(M_{\rm{PBH}})
for several widths
\Delta
and amplitudes
\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{R}}
of the log-normal spectrum. To retain only the universal IR behavior and discard the model-dependent UV tail, we adopt the conservative sharp-cutoff form

Figure 6:
PBH mass distribution
\psi_{f}(M_{\rm{PBH}})
for different widths
\Delta
and amplitudes
\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{R}}
of the log-normal curvature power spectrum in Eq. (
24
). To remain agnostic about the model-dependent UV part, we conservatively model the mass distribution as a power law
\psi_{f}(M_{\rm{PBH}})\propto M_{\rm{PBH}}^{1+1/\gamma_{\rm M}}\propto M_{\rm{PBH}}^{3.78}
with a sharp cutoff at
M_{\rm cut}
, see Eq. (
31
). The reference mass
M_{f}
is the horizon mass at
k_{f}=\mathcal{H}
, while the horizon mass entering Choptuik scaling is
M_{H}(r_{m})=\kappa^{2}M_{f}
, with
\kappa
for the log-normal spectra given in Eq. (
294
) of App.
B
.

{\it Choptuik^{\prime}s~mass~dist:}\quad\quad\psi_{f}^{{\rm sharp}}(M_{\rm{PBH}})\!=\!\frac{1+\gamma_{\rm M}}{\gamma_{\rm M}}\!\left(\frac{M_{\rm{PBH}}}{M_{\rm cut}}\right)^{1+1/\gamma_{\rm M}}\!\!\!\!\Theta(M_{\rm cut}-M_{\rm{PBH}}),

(31)

with
M_{\rm cut}
the upper cutoff of the distribution.
From this mass function we extract a characteristic PBH mass. The PBH energy density contained in an interval
d\ln M_{\rm{PBH}}
is
d\rho_{\rm PBH}=\rho_{\rm PBH}\,\psi_{f}(M_{\rm{PBH}})\,d\ln M_{\rm{PBH}}
, so the corresponding contribution to the number density is
dn_{\rm PBH}=d\rho_{\rm PBH}/M_{\rm{PBH}}
. The average PBH mass, defined as the mass density divided by the number density, then reads

\langle M_{\rm{PBH}}\rangle\equiv\frac{\rho_{\rm PBH}}{n_{\rm PBH}}=\left[\int d\ln M_{\rm{PBH}}\,\psi_{f}(M_{\rm{PBH}})\,M_{\rm{PBH}}^{-1}\right]^{-1}.

(32)

It is convenient to express this average in units of
M_{f}
, defining

\gamma_{\rm H}^{(k_{f})}\equiv\frac{\langle M_{\rm{PBH}}\rangle}{M_{f}}\simeq\begin{cases}1.2,&\text{Press--Schechter},\\[4.0pt]
2.7,&\text{peak theory},\end{cases}

(33)

where the numerical values are computed from the PBH mass distribution shown in Fig.
6
for
\Delta=0.1
and
\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{R}}=0.01
. The larger peak-theory value reflects the heavier high-mass tail produced when counting rare density maxima. Equivalently, if the average mass is normalized to the horizon mass at the compaction scale,
M_{H}(r_{m})
, we have

\gamma_{\rm H}^{(r_{m})}\equiv\frac{\langle M_{\rm{PBH}}\rangle}{M_{H}(r_{m})}=\frac{\gamma_{\rm H}^{(k_{f})}}{\kappa^{2}}\simeq\begin{cases}0.16,&\text{Press--Schechter},\\[4.0pt]
0.37,&\text{peak theory},\end{cases}

(34)

in agreement with the commonly quoted estimate
\gamma_{\rm H}^{(r_{m})}\sim 0.2

[
71
]
. The full dependence of
\kappa
,
\gamma_{\rm H}^{(k_{f})}
, and
\gamma_{\rm H}^{(r_{m})}
on
\Delta
and
\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{R}}
is collected in Tab.
5
of App.
B
. In what follows we treat

\gamma_{\rm H}\sim\gamma_{\rm H}^{(k_{f})}\sim\gamma_{\rm H}^{(r_{m})},

(35)

as an
\mathcal{O}(1)
parameter and identify
M_{\rm{PBH}}\equiv\langle M_{\rm{PBH}}\rangle
as the characteristic PBH mass.

3
Perturbations in a PBH-dominated universe

3.1
The averaged two-fluid background

Before turning to cosmological perturbations and the generation of
gravitational waves from scalar metric fluctuations, we briefly recall
the evolution of the homogeneous background.
The background dynamics are governed by a PBH-induced matter component
and a radiation bath, with energy densities
\rho_{\rm{PBH}}
and

\rho_{\rm r}
, respectively, and scale factor
a
.
Following Ref.
[
211
]
, the standard fluid equations are
supplemented by a source term that accounts for PBH evaporation.
It is convenient to express the background evolution in terms of
comoving energy densities.
The resulting system of equations reads

\left(\rho_{\rm{PBH}}a^{3}\right)^{\prime}=-\,\Gamma\,\rho_{\rm{PBH}}a^{3},\qquad\left(\rho_{\rm r}a^{4}\right)^{\prime}=\;\;\Gamma\,\rho_{\rm{PBH}}a^{4},\qquad\mathcal{H}=a\,\sqrt{\frac{\rho_{\rm{PBH}}+\rho_{\rm r}}{3M_{\rm pl}^{2}}},

(36)

where primes denote derivatives with respect to conformal time
\eta
,
and
\Gamma
is the PBH evaporation rate defined in
Eq. (
8
).
Solving the background evolution equations (
36
)
yields the
following expressions for the energy densities:

\displaystyle\rho_{\rm{PBH}}(\eta;M_{\rm{PBH}})

\displaystyle=\rho_{\rm{PBH},0}\left(\frac{a_{0}}{a}\right)^{3}\exp\!\left[-\int_{0}^{\eta}\!{\rm d}\tilde{\eta}\,\Gamma(\tilde{\eta})\right]=\rho_{\rm{PBH},0}\left(\frac{a_{0}}{a}\right)^{3}\left(1-\frac{t(\eta)}{t_{\rm eva}(M_{\rm{PBH}})}\right)^{1/3},

(37)

\displaystyle\rho_{\rm r}(\eta;M_{\rm{PBH}})

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{a_{0}}{a}\right)^{4}\left[\rho_{\rm r,0}+\rho_{\rm{PBH},0}\int_{0}^{\eta}\!{\rm d}\tilde{\eta}\,\frac{a^{2}(\tilde{\eta})}{a_{0}}\,\frac{1}{3\,t_{\rm eva}(M_{\rm{PBH}})}\left(1-\frac{t(\tilde{\eta})}{t_{\rm eva}(M_{\rm{PBH}})}\right)^{-2/3}\right].

(38)

Relaxing the idealized assumption of a monochromatic PBH mass spectrum and instead allowing for an extended mass distribution
\psi(M_{\rm{PBH}})
implies that different PBHs evaporate at different times, thereby prolonging the reheating transition. To obtain a conservative lower bound on the duration of this modified reheating period, we model the mass function using Choptuik’s distribution with a sharp cutoff,
\psi_{\rm f,sharp}(M_{\rm{PBH}})
, defined in Eq.
31
. The use of a sharp cutoff further allows us to remain largely model agnostic while capturing the minimal physical broadening implied by critical collapse.
We therefore define averaged background quantities on causally connected regions of the Universe, taken to be Hubble-sized patches. Concretely, for any function
\mathcal{F}(M_{\rm{PBH}})
we perform the replacement

\mathcal{F}(M_{\rm{PBH}})\rightarrow\langle\mathcal{F}(M_{\rm{PBH}})\rangle_{\mathcal{H}^{-1}},

(39)

where the average is taken over the PBH mass distribution within a given Hubble volume. We further assume that, at the times of interest, the number of PBHs contained in a Hubble patch is sufficiently large for this ensemble average to be well defined and given by
5
5

5

Variance of statistical fluctuations due to the finite number of PBHs within a Hubble volume scale as
1/N
and vanish in the large-
N
limit. Residual effects of such fluctuations are captured by isocurvature perturbations.

\langle\mathcal{F}(M_{\rm{PBH}})\rangle_{\mathcal{H}^{-1}}\simeq\int\mathcal{F}(M_{\rm{PBH}})\,\psi(M_{\rm{PBH}}){\rm d}\log M_{\rm{PBH}}.

(40)

We define the average time at which the universe is reheated as

\langle\eta_{\rm eva}\rangle=\int\eta_{\rm eva}(M_{\rm{PBH}})\,\psi(M_{\rm{PBH}}){\rm d}\log M_{\rm{PBH}}.

(41)

Figure 7:
Schematic representation of the averaged background quantities

(\rho_{\rm{PBH}},\rho_{\rm r},\Gamma\rho_{\rm{PBH}})
and the associated
long-wavelength perturbations

(\delta\rho_{\rm{PBH}},\delta\rho_{\rm r},\theta_{\rm r},\epsilon,h)
.
The figure depicts the comoving universe in the presence of a
long-wavelength metric perturbation (blue), whose scale is much larger than the mean separation between PBHs.
Such perturbations therefore evolve on an ensemble-averaged background, represented by the red Hubble sized patch containing many PBHs with different masses. On sufficiently small scales, of order the PBH separation
d_{\rm{PBH}}
, local regions are instead dominated by individual PBHs, whose properties determine the local energy densities
\rho_{\rm r}(M_{\rm{PBH}})
and
\rho_{\rm{PBH}}(M_{\rm{PBH}})
. The ensemble is characterized by the PBH mass distribution

\psi(M_{\rm{PBH}})
, over which single-PBH quantities are averaged, yielding
\left(\rho_{\rm{PBH}},\rho_{\rm r},\Gamma\rho_{\rm{PBH}}\right)\xrightarrow{\psi(M_{\rm{PBH}})}\left(\langle\rho_{\rm{PBH}}\rangle,\langle\rho_{\rm r}\rangle,\langle\Gamma\rho_{\rm{PBH}}\rangle\right)
.

Accordingly, the space-averaged matter and radiation energy densities are obtained by

\displaystyle\langle\rho_{\rm{PBH}}\rangle(\eta)=\int\rho_{\rm{PBH}}(\eta;M_{\rm{PBH}})\psi(M_{\rm{PBH}}){\rm d}\log M_{\rm{PBH}},

(42)

\displaystyle\langle\rho_{\rm r}\rangle(\eta)=\int\rho_{\rm r}(\eta;M_{\rm{PBH}})\psi(M_{\rm{PBH}}){\rm d}\log M_{\rm{PBH}},

(43)

where
\rho_{\rm{PBH}}(\eta;M_{\rm{PBH}})
and
\rho_{\rm r}(\eta;M_{\rm{PBH}})
are given by Eqs. (
37
) and (
38
) with
a(\eta)
given by Eq. (
45
) below.
Those background quantities,
\left<\rho_{m}\right>
and
\left<\rho_{r}\right>
, will be needed when solving the linear perturbation evolution equations in Sec.
3.3
. Another useful quantity is

\langle\Gamma\rho_{\rm{PBH}}\rangle(\eta)=\int\Gamma(\eta;M_{\rm{PBH}})\rho_{\rm{PBH}}(\eta;M_{\rm{PBH}})\psi(M_{\rm{PBH}}){\rm d}\log M_{\rm{PBH}},

(44)

where
\Gamma(\eta;M_{\rm{PBH}})
is given by Eq. (
8
).

In order to disentangle the coupled differential equations governing the scale factor
a(\eta)
and the background quantities, we compute
a(\eta)
under the simplifying assumption of instantaneous PBH reheating
6
6

6

We have verified numerically that this approximation affects our results only at the percent level.
which occurs at
\langle\eta_{\rm eva}\rangle
.
For
\eta<\left<\eta_{\rm eva}\right>
, the universe is a matter-radiation mixture and
a(\eta)
is given by Eq. (
355
) of App.
C
(see also Ref.
[
46
]
).
For
\eta>\left<\eta_{\rm eva}\right>
, the universe is radiation dominated so
a(\eta)=c_{1}\eta+c_{2}
. Fixing the constant
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
by imposing the continuity of the scale factor and its derivative at
\eta=\left<\eta_{\rm eva}\right>
leads to

a(\eta)=a_{\mathrm{eq}}\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\left(\frac{\eta}{\eta_{\star}}\right)^{2}+2\left(\frac{\eta}{\eta_{\star}}\right)&\left(\eta\leq\left<\eta_{\mathrm{eva}}\right>\right),\\
\dfrac{2\eta\left(\left<\eta_{\mathrm{eva}}\right>+\eta_{\star}\right)-{\left<\eta_{\mathrm{eva}}\right>^{2}}}{\eta_{\star}^{2}}&\left(\eta>\left<\eta_{\mathrm{eva}}\right>\right),\end{array}\right.

(45)

with
a_{\rm eq}=a_{f}\rho_{{\rm r},f}/\rho_{{\rm{PBH}},f}
and
\eta_{\star}=\eta_{\rm eq}/(\sqrt{2}-1)
. Consequently, the Hubble factor reads
7
7

7

It is also customary
[
106
]
to introduce the shifted time variable
\bar{\eta}\equiv\eta-\left<\eta_{\rm eva}\right>/2
such that the scale factor after evaporation,
\eta>\left<\eta_{\mathrm{eva}}\right>
, becomes
a/a_{\rm eq}=\left(2\bar{\eta}(\left<\eta_{\rm eva}\right>+\eta_{\star})+\left<\eta_{\rm eva}\right>\eta_{\star}\right)/\eta_{\star}^{2}\xrightarrow[\eta_{\rm eq}\to 0]{}2\bar{\eta}\left<\eta_{\rm eva}\right>/\eta_{\star}^{2}
and the Hubble rate simplifies to
\mathcal{H}=2(\left<\eta_{\mathrm{eva}}\right>+\eta_{\star})/[2\bar{\eta}(\left<\eta_{\mathrm{eva}}\right>+\eta_{\star})+\left<\eta_{\mathrm{eva}}\right>\eta_{\star}]\xrightarrow[\eta_{\rm eq}\to 0]{}\bar{\eta}^{-1}
.

\mathcal{H}(\eta)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\dfrac{2(\eta+\eta_{\star})}{\eta(\eta+2\eta_{\star})}&\left(\eta\leq\left<\eta_{\mathrm{eva}}\right>\right),\\[8.0pt]
\dfrac{2(\left<\eta_{\mathrm{eva}}\right>+\eta_{\star})}{2\eta(\left<\eta_{\mathrm{eva}}\right>+\eta_{\star})-\left<\eta_{\mathrm{eva}}\right>^{2}}&\left(\eta>\left<\eta_{\mathrm{eva}}\right>\right).\end{array}\right.

(46)

Note that we have

\displaystyle k_{\rm eq}\equiv\mathcal{H}(\eta_{\rm eq})=\frac{4-2\sqrt{2}}{\eta_{\rm eq}}\simeq\frac{1.17}{\eta_{\rm eq}},

(47)

\displaystyle k_{\rm eva}\equiv\mathcal{H}(\eta_{\rm eva})=\dfrac{2(\left<\eta_{\mathrm{eva}}\right>+\eta_{\star})}{\left<\eta_{\mathrm{eva}}\right>(\left<\eta_{\mathrm{eva}}\right>+2\eta_{\star})}\xrightarrow[\left<\eta_{\mathrm{eva}}\right>/\eta_{\rm eq}\gg 1]{}\frac{2}{\left<\eta_{\mathrm{eva}}\right>}.

(48)

Figure 8:
Background energy-density fractions during PBH reheating for monochromatic and extended mass functions following the Choptuik scaling. We show the evolution of the PBH and radiation components,
\Omega_{\rm{PBH}}
and
\Omega_{r}
, as functions of conformal time normalized to the evaporation time,
\eta/\eta_{\rm eva}
.
\langle\eta_{\rm eva}\rangle
and
\eta_{\rm eva}^{\rm mono}
coincide, while the cutoff evaporation time
\eta_{\rm cut}
associated with the cutoff mass of the extended distribution is larger by a factor
\eta_{\rm cut}\simeq 1.26\langle\eta_{\rm eva}\rangle
.

3.2
Transfer function before evaporation

We now study the linear perturbations in the presence of a PBH gas on scales much smaller than the formation scale,
k\ll k_{f}
. In the Newtonian gauge, the perturbed FLRW metric reads

{\rm d}s^{2}=a(\eta)^{2}\left[-(1+2\Phi)\,{\rm d}\eta^{2}+\left((1-2\Psi)\delta_{ij}+h_{ij}\right){\rm d}x^{i}{\rm d}x^{j}\right],

(49)

where
\Phi
and
\Psi
denote the Newtonian potentials, and
\eta
is the conformal time. Newtonian gauge is particularly convenient as
\Phi
coincides with the Newtonian potential in the non-relativistic limit. In the absence of anisotropic stress, these potentials coincide
\Phi=\Psi

[
46
]
. We work in Fourier space

\Phi(\mathbf{k},\eta)=\int d^{3}\mathbf{x}\,e^{-i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{x}}\Phi(\mathbf{x},\eta).

(50)

Using the perturbed Einstein and conservation equations in the Newton gauge and neglecting anisotropic stress (
\Phi=\Psi
), one finds the following evolution equation for
\Phi

\Phi^{\prime\prime}+3\mathcal{H}(1+c_{s}^{2})\Phi^{\prime}+\left[3(c_{s}^{2}-\omega)\mathcal{H}^{2}+c_{s}^{2}k^{2}\right]\Phi=\frac{3}{2}\mathcal{H}^{2}\,\delta_{p,{\rm nad}},

(51)

where
\delta_{p,{\rm nad}}\equiv\delta p_{\rm nad}/\overline{\rho}
and
\delta p_{\rm nad}\equiv\delta p-c_{s}^{2}\delta\rho
denote the non-adiabatic density contrast and pressure perturbation, respectively.
8
8

8

In perturbation theory, energy densities and pressure contributions of the different components are split into a homogeneous background part and a small fluctuation,

\rho_{i}(t,\mathbf{x})=\bar{\rho}_{i}(t)+\delta\rho_{i}(t,\mathbf{x})
and
p_{i}(t,\mathbf{x})=\bar{p}_{i}(t)+\delta p_{i}(t,\mathbf{x})
.
These matter perturbations enter the Einstein equations through the perturbed energy-momentum tensor and are discussed in more detail in App.
C.1
.

In case of a mixture of perfect fluids, PBH + radiation, the non-adiabatic density contrast reads

\delta_{p,{\rm nad}}=\frac{\left<\Omega_{\rm{PBH}}\right>\left<\Omega_{\rm r}\right>(1+\omega_{\rm{PBH}})(1+\omega_{\rm r})}{1+\omega}(c_{\rm{PBH}}^{2}-c_{\rm r}^{2})S\,,

(52)

where we have introduced the relative background energy fractions

\langle\Omega_{\rm{PBH}}\rangle\equiv\frac{\langle\rho_{\rm{PBH}}\rangle}{\langle\rho_{\rm{PBH}}\rangle+\langle\rho_{\rm r}\rangle}\,,\qquad{\rm and}\qquad\langle\Omega_{\rm r}\rangle\equiv\frac{\langle\rho_{\rm r}\rangle}{\langle\rho_{\rm{PBH}}\rangle+\langle\rho_{\rm r}\rangle}\,.

(53)

as well as the effective equation-of-state parameter
\omega\equiv\bar{p}/\bar{\rho}

and the adiabatic sound speed
c_{s}^{2}\equiv\bar{p}^{\prime}/\bar{\rho}^{\prime}
.The latter take the simple forms
\omega_{\rm r}=c_{\rm r}^{2}=1/3
and
\omega_{\rm{PBH}}=c_{\rm{PBH}}^{2}=0
during pure radiation and matter eras, respectively.
The quantity
S
is the PBH–radiation isocurvature perturbation

S\;\equiv\;\delta_{\rm{PBH}}-\frac{3}{4}\delta_{\rm r}.

(54)

The latter obeys the following evolution equation in Fourier space

\displaystyle S^{\prime\prime}+\mathcal{H}S^{\prime}-\frac{c_{s}^{2}k^{2}}{1+\omega}\,S

\displaystyle=\frac{2(c_{\rm r}^{2}-c_{\rm{PBH}}^{2})}{3(1+\omega)}\frac{k^{4}}{\mathcal{H}^{2}}\Phi\,.

(55)

We refer to App.
C
for a derivation of Eqs. (
51
) and (
55
). Such derivations can also be found in Refs.
[
112
,
347
]
, or in the textbook
[
298
]
.
Numerically solving the system of Eqs. (
51
) and (
55
) through the radiation–matter transition shows that
\Phi
freezes to a constant in the matter era,

Figure 9:
Transfer function of the Newtonian potential
\Phi
assuming adiabatic (
blue
) and isocurvature (
red
) initial conditions. The solid lines are obtained from numerically integrating Eqs. (
51
) and (
55
) with the dashed lines correspond to
Eq. (
56
).

\Phi(k,y\!\gg\!1)=T_{\Phi}^{\rm(eMD)}(\kappa)\Phi(k,0)+T_{S}^{\rm(eMD)}(\kappa)S(k,0),\qquad\kappa\equiv k/k_{\rm eq},\qquad y\equiv a/a_{\rm eq},

(56)

where the superscript
“eMD”
indicates that the transfer functions account for effects from the PBH-dominated era but not for effects from the PBH evaporation which are discussed in the next section, Sec.
3.3
.
Fig.
9
shows that the numerical solution for the transfer functions can be very well approximated by the semi-analytical formulae (see App.
C
and Refs.
[
240
,
204
]
for derivations)
9
9

9

The analytical expressions in Eq. (
57
) cease to be valid for
k\ll k_{\rm eva}
, as the corresponding modes enter the horizon during the radiation-dominated era following PBH evaporation. This regime is nevertheless captured by the numerical computation, which explains the discrepancy between the blue solid and dashed curves at
k\lesssim k_{\rm eva}
in Fig.
13
.

T_{\Phi}^{\rm(eMD)}(\kappa)\simeq\left(\frac{10}{9}+\frac{0.09\kappa^{2}}{\ln\left(1+0.13\kappa\right)}\right)^{-1},\qquad T_{S}^{\rm(eMD)}(\kappa)\simeq\left(5^{1/c}+\kappa^{2/c}\right)^{-c},\qquad c=2.

(57)

The initial values
\Phi(k,0)
and
S(\mathbf{k},0)
in Eq. (
56
) are treated as Gaussian random fields
10
10

10

For a statistically homogeneous field
S
, the power spectrum, bispectrum, and trispectrum are defined through the Fourier-space correlators

\displaystyle\langle S(\mathbf{k}_{1})S(\mathbf{k}_{2})\rangle

\displaystyle=(2\pi)^{3}\delta^{(3)}(\mathbf{k}_{1}+\mathbf{k}_{2})\,P_{S}(k_{1}),

(58)

\displaystyle\langle S(\mathbf{k}_{1})S(\mathbf{k}_{2})S(\mathbf{k}_{3})\rangle_{c}

\displaystyle=(2\pi)^{3}\delta^{(3)}(\mathbf{k}_{1}+\mathbf{k}_{2}+\mathbf{k}_{3})\,B_{S}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3}),

(59)

\displaystyle\langle S(\mathbf{k}_{1})S(\mathbf{k}_{2})S(\mathbf{k}_{3})S(\mathbf{k}_{4})\rangle_{c}

\displaystyle=(2\pi)^{3}\delta^{(3)}(\mathbf{k}_{1}+\mathbf{k}_{2}+\mathbf{k}_{3}+\mathbf{k}_{4})\,T_{S}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},k_{4}),

(60)

where the subscript
c
denotes the connected part. For Gaussian fields, all connected correlators beyond the two-point function vanish identically. However, the PBH-induced isocurvature perturbations
S
are not Gaussian but originate from the Poisson statistics of the discrete PBH number density. As a consequence, connected correlators exist at all orders. In Fourier space, the Poisson process produces scale-independent (shot-noise) contributions,

P_{S}(k)=\frac{1}{\bar{n}_{\rm{PBH}}},\qquad B_{S}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3})=\frac{1}{\bar{n}_{\rm{PBH}}^{2}},\qquad T_{S}(k_{1},k_{2},k_{3},k_{4})=\frac{1}{\bar{n}_{\rm{PBH}}^{3}},

(61)

and similarly for higher-point functions. Here
P_{S}\equiv(2\pi^{2}/k^{3})\mathcal{P}_{S}
denotes the dimensionful power spectrum. Expressed in terms of dimensionless spectra and evaluated at wavenumbers
k\ll k_{\rm{PBH}}\sim\bar{n}_{\rm{PBH}}^{1/3}
, corresponding to the inverse mean PBH separation, the bispectrum and trispectrum scale as
(k/k_{\rm{PBH}})^{6}
and
(k/k_{\rm{PBH}})^{9}
, respectively. These contributions become relevant only for modes approaching the PBH spacing, where perturbative treatments are expected to break down, and are therefore neglected in the present work.

and statistically homogeneous with power spectra
\mathcal{P}_{\Phi}(k)
and
\mathcal{P}_{S}(k)
defined by

\displaystyle\langle\Phi(\mathbf{k},0)\Phi(\mathbf{k}^{\prime},0)\rangle=\frac{2\pi^{2}}{k^{3}}\mathcal{P}_{\Phi}(k)\delta(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{k}^{\prime}),\qquad\langle S(\mathbf{k},0)S(\mathbf{k}^{\prime},0)\rangle=\frac{2\pi^{2}}{k^{3}}\mathcal{P}_{S}(k)\delta(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{k}^{\prime}).

(62)

The adiabatic curvature perturbations originate from quantum fluctuations of the inflaton field and are conveniently described in terms of the gauge-invariant comoving curvature perturbation
\mathcal{R}

[
46
]
. Its relation to the Newtonian potential
\Phi
reads

-\mathcal{R}=\Phi+\frac{2}{3}\frac{\mathcal{H}^{-1}\Phi^{\prime}+\Phi}{1+\omega}\xrightarrow[k\eta\ll 1]{}\frac{5+3\omega}{3(1+\omega)}\,\Phi,

(63)

where the superhorizon limit follows from
\Phi^{\prime}\simeq 0
in the absence of non-adiabatic pressure perturbations. This implies

\mathcal{P}_{\Phi}(k)=\left(\frac{3(1+\omega(0))}{5+3\omega(0)}\right)^{2}\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{R}}(k)=\left(\frac{2}{3}\right)^{2}\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{R}}\left(\frac{k}{k_{\rm CMB}}\right)^{n_{s}-1},

(64)

where the normalization is set at
\eta\to 0
, deep in the radiation-dominated era with
\omega(0)=1/3
, prior to PBH domination.
11
11

11

A generalization to an arbitrary pre-PBH equation of state (e.g. kination) can be found in Ref.
[
112
]
.
Modes entering the horizon during a PBH-dominated phase (
k<k_{\rm eq}
) instead inherit the standard prefactor
(3/5)^{2}
. This difference is consistently accounted for by the transfer function in Eq. (
57
) through a factor
10/9
.
We adopt the usual power-law parametrization for the primordial spectrum,

\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{R}}(k)=\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{R}}(k/k_{\rm CMB})^{n_{s}-1}
,
with
\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{R}}\simeq 2.1\times 10^{-9}
,
n_{s}\simeq 0.96
, and pivot scale
k_{\rm CMB}\simeq 0.05\,{\rm Mpc}^{-1}
, consistent with
Planck
observations
[
11
]
. While this corresponds to a nearly scale-invariant spectrum on CMB scales, significant departures may arise at much smaller scales
k\gg k_{\rm CMB}
.
In particular, the discrete (Poisson) nature of PBHs induces an isocurvature component at formation
[
16
,
73
,
293
,
159
]
, with power spectrum

\mathcal{P}_{S}(k)=\frac{2}{3\pi}\left(\frac{k}{k_{\rm{PBH}}}\right)^{3},\qquad k_{\rm{PBH}}=a_{f}\left(\frac{4\pi}{3}n_{\rm{PBH}}(t_{f})\right)^{1/3},

(65)

where
k_{\rm{PBH}}^{-1}
corresponds to the typical comoving separation between neighboring PBHs at formation.

3.3
Suppression factor after evaporation

In the presence of linear metric perturbations, the PBH decay rate as measured by comoving observers,
\Gamma_{\rm obs}(\vec{x})
, may acquire a dependence on the position
\vec{x}
. Indeed, comoving observers with
x^{i}=\text{const}
measure a proper conformal time
d\eta_{c}^{2}=(1+2\Phi)\,d\eta^{2}
. Hence, they observe PBH to decay with a rate
\Gamma_{\rm obs}=d\log{M_{\rm\mathsmaller{PBH}}}/d\eta_{c}=\Gamma(1-\Phi)
where
\Gamma=d\log{M_{\rm\mathsmaller{PBH}}}/d\eta
. Additionally, if the PBH fluid has a non-vanishing velocity with respect to the observer, then the decay rate needs to be Lorentz transformed as well.
However, the effect of PBH decay on linear perturbations can be simplified by working in the synchronous gauge, where the metric takes the form
[
27
,
302
,
211
]

{\rm d}s^{2}=a^{2}\left({\rm d}\eta^{2}-H_{ij}{\rm d}x^{i}{\rm d}x^{j}\right).

(66)

Here,
H_{ij}=\delta_{ij}+\hat{k}_{i}\hat{k}_{j}h+\left(\hat{k}_{i}\hat{k}_{j}-\frac{1}{3}\delta_{ij}\right)6\varepsilon+\frac{1}{2}h_{ij}
, with
\hat{k}_{i}\equiv k_{i}/|k|
. The quantities
h
and
\varepsilon
are the trace and traceless component of the scalar mode in the synchronous gauge.
12
12

12

h
and
\varepsilon
corresponds
h
and
\eta
in
[
261
,
27
,
302
]
and to
\gamma
and
\varepsilon
in
[
211
]
.
For simplicity, we neglect sources of anisotropic stress.
Linearized Einstein equations in the synchronous gauge read
[
261
,
27
,
302
,
211
]
:

\displaystyle\delta_{\rm{PBH}}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=-\frac{h^{\prime}}{2}

(67)

\displaystyle\delta_{\mathrm{r}}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=-\frac{4}{3}\left(\theta_{\mathrm{r}}+\frac{h^{\prime}}{2}\right)+\frac{\left<\Gamma\rho_{\rm{PBH}}\right>}{\left<\rho_{\mathrm{r}}\right>}\left(\delta_{\rm{PBH}}-\delta_{\mathrm{r}}\right),

(68)

\displaystyle\theta_{\mathrm{r}}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\frac{k^{2}}{4}\delta_{\mathrm{r}}-\frac{\left<\Gamma\rho_{\rm{PBH}}\right>}{\left<\rho_{\mathrm{r}}\right>}\theta_{\mathrm{r}},

(69)

\displaystyle k^{2}\varepsilon-\frac{1}{2}\frac{a^{\prime}}{a}h^{\prime}

\displaystyle=-\frac{3}{2}\mathcal{H}^{2}\left(\frac{\left<\rho_{\rm{PBH}}\right>}{\left<\rho_{\mathrm{tot}}\right>}\delta_{\rm{PBH}}+\frac{\left<\rho_{\mathrm{r}}\right>}{\left<\rho_{\mathrm{tot}}\right>}\delta_{\mathrm{r}}\right),

(70)

\displaystyle k^{2}\varepsilon^{\prime}

\displaystyle=2\mathcal{H}^{2}\frac{\left<\rho_{\mathrm{r}}\right>}{\left<\rho_{\mathrm{tot}}\right>}\theta_{\mathrm{r}},

(71)

where
\theta\equiv\partial_{i}v^{i}
is the velocity divergence.
The initial conditions for the perturbations are
[
211
]
:

\displaystyle\delta_{\mathrm{r}}=-\frac{2}{3}C(k\eta)^{2},\quad\delta_{\rm{PBH}}=\frac{3}{4}\delta_{\mathrm{r}},\quad\theta_{\mathrm{r}}=-\frac{1}{18}C\left(k^{4}\eta^{3}\right),

(72)

\displaystyle h=C(k\eta)^{2},\quad\varepsilon=2C-\frac{1}{18}C(k\eta)^{2},

(73)

where
C=\mathcal{R}/2
.
These hold for RD, which is why we start the numerical evolution long before the onset of PBH domination. To translate back to Newtonian gauge, we use
[
261
,
302
,
211
]
:

\Phi(k,\eta)=\varepsilon-\mathcal{H}\frac{(6\varepsilon+h)^{\prime}}{2k^{2}}.

(74)

We refer to
App.
D
for more details on the derivation of linear perturbations in synchronous gauge and for a formulation in Newtonian gauge as well.
Solving for the system of Eqs. (
51
) and (
55
) followed by Eqs. (
67
-
71
), with
\left<\rho_{\rm{PBH}}\right>
,
\left<\rho_{r}\right>
, and
\Gamma\left<\rho_{\rm{PBH}}\right>
determined by Eqs. (
42
), (
43
), and (
44
), we obtain the evolution of the scalar potential
\Phi
in Eq. (
74
) during both the transition from radiation to matter when PBHs dominate at
\eta_{\rm eq}
and from matter back to radiation when PBHs evaporate at
\langle\eta_{\rm eva}\rangle
. We show the results and differences for monochromatic and extended mass functions in Fig.
10
.

The amplitude of the scalar field
\Phi_{\rm osc}
, right before it starts oscillating, just after PBH evaporation, can be decomposed into two distinct contributions,

Figure 10:
Shown is the evolution of energy densities (
\Omega_{\rm{PBH}}
, light blue) and (
\Omega_{r}
, dark blue) during PBH domination. The grey curve shows
\Phi_{k}
(normalized to
10/9
in RD), with the RD-to-MD transition governed by
\mathcal{T}
, and the MD-to-RD transition from PBH evaporation by
\mathcal{S}
. We choose
\langle\eta_{\rm eva}\rangle/\eta_{\rm eq}=225
here and in all future plots for better comparability with
[
211
]
.
Left:
Mass distribution following the Choptuik scaling.
Right:
Monochromatic mass distribution.

Figure 11:
Scalar evolution during PBH reheating. The figure displays the evolution of the Newtonian potential for different momentum modes (blue, green) as well as the best fit (orange) describing the universal evolution prior to the onset of oscillation. For comparison, the analytic approximation and neglecting decoupling for the Newtonian potential in the case of monochromatic mass distribution (black) is shown.

\Phi_{\rm osc}(k)\equiv\Phi(k,\eta_{\rm osc})=\left[T_{\Phi}^{\rm(eMD)}(k)\Phi(k,0)+T_{S}^{\rm(eMD)}(k)S(k,0)\right]\,\mathcal{S}_{\Phi}(k)\,,

(75)

where
\Phi(k,0)
and
S(k,0)
are the primordial seeds.
The transfer functions
T_{\Phi}^{\rm(eMD)}(k)
and
T_{S}^{\rm(eMD)}(k)
, given in Eq. (
56
), capture the decay of modes that entered the horizon during the radiation-dominated (RD) era, prior to the onset of PBH domination (eMD). Once the eMD phase begins, these modes effectively freeze until PBH evaporation. The quantity
\mathcal{S}_{\Phi}(k)
is the
suppression factor
which describes the decay of
\Phi
during PBH evaporation phase.
The physical origin of the decay encapsulated in
\mathcal{S}_{\Phi}(k)
is clear from examining the Poisson equation in the subhorizon limit
[
211
]
:

k^{2}\Phi\simeq\frac{3}{2}\mathcal{H}^{2}\left(\frac{\left<\rho_{\rm{PBH}}\right>}{\left<\rho_{\mathrm{tot}}\right>}\delta_{\mathrm{m}}+\frac{\left<\rho_{\mathrm{\rm r}}\right>}{\left<\rho_{\mathrm{tot}}\right>}\delta_{\mathrm{r}}\right),

(76)

where
\delta_{\rm{PBH}}
and
\delta_{\rm r}
denote the PBH and radiation density contrasts.
During the eMD, one has
\delta_{\rm{PBH}}\propto a
while
\delta_{\rm r}
remains approximately constant (and oscillatory), so the PBH contribution dominates and
\Phi
remains on an almost constant plateau. As Hawking evaporation reduces
\rho_{\rm m}
, the two source terms become comparable, driving
\Phi
to follow the oscillatory behaviour of
\delta_{\rm r}
.

During evaporation, and before it starts oscillating at a time
\eta_{\rm osc}(k)
, the potential in Eq. (
76
) follows the PBH
fraction
\Omega_{m}\equiv\rho_{\rm{PBH}}/\rho_{\rm tot}
,

\Phi(k,\eta)\ \propto\ \langle\Omega_{\rm m}(\eta)\rangle,\qquad\textrm{for}\quad\eta\lesssim\eta_{\rm osc}(k),

(77)

so that the suppression factor can be estimated as

\mathcal{S}_{\Phi}(k)\simeq\frac{\Phi(k,\eta_{\rm osc})}{\Phi(k,\eta\ll\eta_{\rm osc})}\simeq\langle\Omega_{\rm m}(\eta_{\rm osc})\rangle,

(78)

where
\langle\Omega_{\rm m}\rangle
is the PBH energy fraction averaged over the extended PBH
mass function obeying the Choptuik scaling

\langle\Omega_{\rm m}\rangle(\eta)=\int\!\frac{{\rm d}M}{M}\,\psi(M)\,\Omega_{\rm m}(\eta;M)=\frac{1+\gamma_{\rm M}}{\gamma_{\rm M}}\int_{M(\eta)}^{M_{\rm cut}}\!\frac{{\rm d}M}{M}\left(\frac{M}{M_{\rm cut}}\right)^{1+1/\gamma_{\rm M}}\Bigl(1-\frac{t(\eta)}{t_{\rm eva}(M)}\Bigr)^{1/3}.

(79)

We exploit
t_{\rm eva}\propto M^{3}
and
t\propto\eta^{3}
to change variables to

\eta_{\rm eva}\propto M
, and evaluate the resulting integral in closed form
with
Mathematica
(see App.
D.2
for more details)

\langle\Omega_{\rm m}\rangle=-(-1)^{2/3}(1+\gamma_{\rm M})\left.\frac{\eta}{\eta_{\rm eva}}\left(\frac{\eta_{\rm eva}}{\eta_{\rm cut}}\right)^{\!1+\frac{1}{\gamma_{\rm M}}}\!\!{}_{2}F_{1}\left(-\frac{1}{3},\frac{1}{3\gamma_{\rm M}};1+\frac{1}{3\gamma_{\rm M}};\frac{\eta_{\text{eva}}^{3}}{\eta^{3}}\right)\right|_{\eta_{\rm eva}=\eta}^{\eta_{\rm eva}=\eta_{\rm cut}},

(80)

where
{}_{2}F_{1}
denotes the hypergeometric function and

\eta_{\rm cut}\equiv\frac{1+2\gamma_{\rm M}}{1+\gamma_{\rm M}}\left<\eta_{\rm eva}\right>,

(81)

with
\eta_{\rm cut}\simeq 1.26\left<\eta_{\rm eva}\right>
for
\gamma_{\rm M}\simeq 0.36
.

Figure 12:
Suppression factor
\mathcal{S}_{\Phi}(k)
of the Newtonian potential
\Phi
for different values of the
critical exponent
\gamma_{\rm M}
entering the Choptuik’s PBH mass distribution
\psi(M)
in
Eq. (
31
).
In the sub-horizon limit
k\langle\eta_{\rm eva}\rangle\gg 1
, the analytical estimate in
Eq. (
78
) — with
\eta_{\rm osc}
determined from
Eq. (
82
) — (
solid lines
) and the analytical estimate in
Eq. (
421
) (
dashed line
) show excellent agreement with the
numerical computation (
black crosses
) obtained from Eq. (
74
).
For the value
\gamma_{\rm M}\simeq 0.36
predicted by General Relativity

[
81
,
4
,
132
,
267
,
285
,
245
,
177
,
208
]
, we find the asymptotic behaviour
\mathcal{S}_{\Phi}(k)\propto k^{-4/3}
.
In the limit
\gamma_{\rm M}\to 0
, corresponding to a monochromatic PBH mass function, we recover
the scaling
\mathcal{S}_{\Phi}(k)\propto k^{-1/3}

in agreement with Ref.
[
211
]
.
However, for
k\langle\eta_{\rm eva}\rangle\gtrsim\gamma_{\rm M}^{-1}
the suppression transitions
back to the asymptotic behaviour
\mathcal{S}_{\Phi}(k)\propto k^{-4/3}
.

The remaining ingredient is the oscillation time
\eta_{\rm osc}(k)
, defined as the
moment when
\Phi
starts oscillating, also called decoupling time in Ref.
[
211
]
. The latter can be determined as the time when the derivative
\Phi^{\prime}
equates the gradient term
k\Phi
in the evolution equation,

\left|\frac{\Phi^{\prime}}{\Phi}\right|\simeq\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}\,k\qquad\text{at}\qquad\eta=\eta_{\rm osc}(k).

(82)

In App.
D.2
, we show that the criterion in Eq. (
82
) gives better results than the one proposed in Ref.
[
211
]
that relies on the second derivative of
\Phi
, see in particular Fig.
19
for a comparison of the two approaches.
Using the asymptotic form of

\langle\Omega_{\rm m}\rangle
near the evaporation endpoint
\eta\to\eta_{\rm cut}^{-}
, we find

\Phi\propto(\eta_{\rm cut}-\eta)^{4/3}
and solve
Eq. (
417
) for
\eta_{\rm osc}
, obtaining

\eta_{\rm cut}-\eta_{\rm osc}\propto k^{-1}
. Substituting this into
Eq. (
415
) leads to the high–
k
limit

{\it Choptuik:}\qquad\qquad\mathcal{S}_{\Phi}(k)\simeq\kappa_{0}\left(\frac{k_{\rm eva}}{k}\right)^{4/3},\qquad\kappa_{0}\equiv\frac{3^{2/3}(1+\gamma_{M})^{7/3}}{2^{4/3}\gamma_{M}(1+2\gamma_{M})^{4/3}},

(83)

with
\kappa_{0}\simeq 2.3
for the value of the Choptuik exponent predicted by GR,

\gamma_{\rm M}\simeq 0.36
. This

\mathcal{S}_{\Phi}(k)\propto k^{-4/3}
scaling for a Choptuik-mass-distribution is one of the main results of this section.

In Fig.
12
, we compare the high-
k
limit in
Eq. (
83
) with the analytical estimate in
Eq. (
78
), as well as with the
fully numerical determination of
\mathcal{S}_{\Phi}(k)
.
The latter is obtained by measuring the decrease of
\Phi
from its plateau value
down to its value at the onset of the first oscillation.
Here
\Phi
is computed from Eq. (
74
) after numerically
integrating Eqs. (
67
–
71
),
with
\langle\rho_{m}\rangle
,
\langle\rho_{r}\rangle
, and

\langle\Gamma\rho_{m}\rangle
determined from
Eqs. (
42
)–(
44
), averaged over
\psi(M_{\rm{PBH}})
in Eq. (
31
) with
\gamma_{\rm M}\simeq 0.36
.
We have verified that the numerical extraction of
\mathcal{S}_{\Phi}(k)
is independent of the
duration of the matter era,
\eta_{\rm eva}/\eta_{\rm eq}
.
This is expected, since the dependence of
\Phi
on
\eta_{\rm eva}/\eta_{\rm eq}
appears
only through the transfer functions
T_{\Phi}
and
T_{S}
in Eq. (
75
),
as illustrated in Fig.
9
.
We find that the numerical solution for
\Phi(\eta)
before it starts oscillating is well approximated by
the following fit (see Fig.
11
, left panel):

F_{\Phi}(\eta)\equiv\frac{\Phi(k,\eta)}{\Phi(k,\eta\ll\eta_{\rm eva})}\simeq\begin{cases}\exp\!\left[-0.84\left(\dfrac{\eta}{\langle\eta_{\rm eva}\rangle}\right)^{3.43}\right],&\bigl(\eta<\langle\eta_{\rm eva}\rangle\bigr),\\[11.38092pt]
3.5\left(\dfrac{\eta_{\rm cut}-\eta}{\eta_{\rm cut}}\right)^{4/3},&\bigl(\langle\eta_{\rm eva}\rangle\leq\eta\leq\eta_{\rm osc}(k)\bigr).\end{cases}

(84)

which captures both the early plateau and the late–time decay, and reproduces
the numerically extracted suppression factor to good accuracy. Note that
\mathcal{S}_{\Phi}(k)\equiv F_{\Phi}(k,\eta_{\rm osc})
, see Eq. (
78
).

Finally, the same formalism reproduces the known monochromatic limit.
Taking
\gamma_{\rm M}\to 0
in the analytic expression for

\langle\Omega_{\rm m}\rangle
yields

{\it Mono.:}\qquad\qquad\mathcal{S}_{\Phi}(k)\xrightarrow[\gamma_{\rm M}\to 0]{}\left(\frac{\sqrt{12}}{k\langle\eta_{\rm eva}\rangle}\right)^{1/3},

(85)

in agreement with Ref.
[
211
]
.
However, as shown in Fig.
12
, the monochromatic scaling
\mathcal{S}_{\Phi}(k)\propto k^{-1/3}
transitions to
the Choptuik-mass-function scaling
\mathcal{S}_{\Phi}(k)\propto k^{-4/3}
behaviour once

k\langle\eta_{\rm eva}\rangle~\gtrsim~k_{\rm trans}\langle\eta_{\rm eva}\rangle\equiv\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2^{1/3}\gamma_{\rm M}}.

(86)

We refer the reader to App.
D.2
for a deeper analytical understanding of the transition wavenumber in Eq. (
86
). Note that
\gamma_{M}
enters only through the prefactor
\mathcal{S}_{\Phi}
, encoded in
\kappa_{0}
in Eq. (
83
). By contrast, the asymptotic scaling
\mathcal{S}_{\Phi}\propto k^{-4/3}
is independent of the precise value of
\gamma_{M}
. The latter only sets the transition scale, given in Eq. (
86
), below which the monochromatic scaling
\mathcal{S}_{\Phi}\propto k^{-1/3}
is recovered, see Fig.
12
.

4
The SIGW formalism

4.1
From scalar sources to GWs

At first order in cosmological perturbation theory, scalar, vector, and tensor perturbations decouple. However, at second order, scalar perturbations act as sources for tensor perturbations, thereby generating GWs
[
19
,
44
,
242
,
131
]
. These scalar-induced gravitational waves (SIGWs) arise inevitably at quadratic order in scalar perturbations, even in the absence of primordial tensor modes. Solving the equation of motion (EOM) for the tensor modes
h_{ij}
, in the Newtonian gauge defined by Eq. (
49
), yields

h^{\prime\prime}_{ij}(\mathbf{x},\eta)+2\mathcal{H}h^{\prime}_{ij}(\mathbf{x},\eta)-\Delta h_{ij}(\mathbf{x},\eta)=\mathcal{P}_{ij}^{ab}\mathcal{S}_{ab}(\mathbf{x},\eta),

(87)

where
\mathcal{H}=aH
is the conformal Hubble parameter,
\mathcal{P}_{ij}^{ab}
denotes the transverse-traceless projector with
a,b,i,j\in\{1,2,3\}
, and
\mathcal{S}_{ab}
represents the source term quadratic in scalar perturbations (
\Phi
and its derivatives)
[
242
,
115
]
:

\mathcal{S}_{ab}(\mathbf{x},\eta)=4\left[\partial_{a}\Phi\partial_{b}\Phi+\frac{2}{3(1+\omega)}\partial_{a}(\Phi+\Phi^{\prime}/\mathcal{H})\partial_{b}(\Phi+\Phi^{\prime}/\mathcal{H})\right],

(88)

where an underlying perfect fluid with equation-of-state parameter
\omega
is assumed. The scalar and tensor fluctuations can be decomposed into Fourier modes

\Phi(\mathbf{x},\eta)=\int\frac{d\mathbf{k}^{3}}{(2\pi)^{3}}e^{i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{x}}\Phi(\mathbf{k},\eta),\qquad h_{ij}(\mathbf{x},\eta)=\int\frac{d\mathbf{k}^{3}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\,e^{i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{x}}\,h_{ij}(\mathbf{k},\eta).

(89)

The tensor modes can be additionally decomposed into a sum over the two transverse polarizations labeled by
\lambda=\{\times,+\}

h_{ij}(\mathbf{k},\eta)=\sum_{\lambda}e^{\lambda}_{ij}(\mathbf{k})\,h_{\lambda}(\mathbf{k},\eta),

(90)

with the polarisation tensors
e_{ij}^{\lambda}
forming an orthonormal basis
e_{ij}^{\lambda}e_{ij}^{\tilde{\lambda}}=\delta_{\lambda\tilde{\lambda}}
, and satisfying
\mathcal{P}_{ij}^{ab}e_{ij}^{\lambda}=e_{ab}^{\lambda}
.
As we discussed in Sec.
3.2
, the initial condition for the scalar potential can arise from both adiabatic and isocurvature perturbations, which we denote by
\Phi(k,0)
and
S(k,0)
, respectively. Since the evolution equations are linear, the system can be decomposed into a linear combination of an initially adiabatic system and an initially isocurvature system

\Phi(\mathbf{k},\eta)=T_{\Phi}(k,\eta)\Phi(k,0)+T_{S}(k,\eta)S(k,0),

(91)

where the transfer functions
T_{\Phi}
and
T_{S}
describe the evolution of
\Phi(\mathbf{k},\eta)
assuming adiabatic and isocurvature initial conditions, respectively.
We suppose that the two seeds are uncorrelated

\left<\Phi(k,0)\,S(k,0)\right>=0.

(92)

The Fourier transform of
\mathcal{S}_{ab}(\mathbf{x},\eta)
in Eq. (
88
) projected on the
\lambda
basis reads
[
115
]

\mathcal{S}_{\lambda}(\mathbf{k},\eta)=4\int\frac{d^{3}q}{(2\pi)^{3}}e_{\lambda}^{ij}(k)q_{i}q_{j}X_{\mathbf{q}}X_{\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{q}}f_{X}\left(\eta,\frac{q}{k},\frac{|\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{q}|}{k}\right),\qquad X=\{\Phi,S\},

(93)

with the source function being given by
13
13

13

The definition of the source function
f_{X}
in Eq. (
95
) differs from the one in Ref.
[
242
]
, denoted
f_{X}^{\rm KT}
, by a factor

f_{X}^{\rm KT}~=~2\left(\frac{3+3\omega(0)}{5+3\omega(0)}\right)^{2}f_{X}~=~\frac{8}{9}f_{X}.

(94)

The extra factor of
2
in their
f_{\Phi}^{\rm KT}
yields a factor of
4
in
\Omega_{\rm GW}
, which is exactly compensated by a factor of
1/4
arising from their convention for
\mathcal{P}_{h}
and its relation to
\Omega_{\rm GW}
, compared with our definition in Eq. (
106
). The factor
\left(\frac{3+3\omega(0)}{5+3\omega(0)}\right)^{2}
with
\omega(0)=1/3
instead comes from the different normalization choice, since Ref.
[
242
]
is written in terms of
\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{R}}
whereas we use
\mathcal{P}_{\Phi}
, normalized to the early radiation era, preceding PBH domination, see Eq. (
64
).

\displaystyle f_{X}\left(\eta,\frac{q}{k},\frac{|\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{q}|}{k}\right)=

\displaystyle T_{X}(q\eta)T_{X}(|\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{q}|\eta)

(95)

\displaystyle+\frac{2}{3(1+\omega)}\left(T_{X}(q\eta)+\frac{T_{X}^{\prime}(q\eta)}{\mathcal{H}}\right)\left(T_{X}(|\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{q}|\eta)+\frac{T_{X}^{\prime}(|\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{q}|\eta)}{\mathcal{H}}\right).

The differential equation for the tensor modes
h
in Eq. (
87
), in Fourier space and projected on the
\lambda
basis, can be solved by Green’s method
[
114
]

h_{\lambda}(\mathbf{k},\eta)=\int_{0}^{x=k\eta}{\rm d}\overline{x}\,G_{h}(x,\overline{x})\,\frac{\mathcal{S}_{\lambda}(\mathbf{k},\overline{x})}{k^{2}},

(96)

where
x\equiv k\eta
and
\bar{x}\equiv k\bar{\eta}
. The Green’s function
G_{h}
is defined by the inhomogeneous differential equation

G_{h}^{\prime\prime}(x,\bar{x})+2\frac{\mathcal{H}}{k}\,G_{h}^{\prime}(x,\bar{x})+G_{h}(x,\bar{x})=\delta(x-\bar{x})\,.

(97)

and for
\mathcal{H}=2/\left[(1+3\omega)\eta\right]
with constant
\omega
reads
[
115
,
113
]
14
14

14

We use the same convention for
G_{h}(x,\tilde{x})
as in Refs.
[
114
,
113
]
(defined in Eq. (
98
)). This is related to
G_{h}(\eta,\tilde{\eta})
in Ref.
[
115
]
by
G_{h}(x,\bar{x})=k\,G_{h}(\eta,\bar{\eta})
and to
G_{k}(\eta,\bar{\eta})
in Ref.
[
242
,
213
,
214
]
by
G_{h}(x,\bar{x})=\frac{a(\bar{\eta})}{a({\eta})}k\,G_{h}(\eta,\bar{\eta})
.

G_{h}(x,\bar{x})=\frac{\pi}{2}\,\frac{\bar{x}^{\,b+3/2}}{x^{\,b+1/2}}\left[J_{b+1/2}(\bar{x})\,Y_{b+1/2}(x)-J_{b+1/2}(x)\,Y_{b+1/2}(\bar{x})\right]\Theta(x-\bar{x})\,,

(98)

where
b\equiv(1-3\omega)/(1+3\omega)
, and
J_{\alpha}
and
Y_{\alpha}
denote the cylindrical Bessel functions of the first and second kind, respectively.
Inserting Eq. (
93
) in Eq. (
96
), we obtain

h_{\lambda}(\mathbf{k},\eta)=4\sum_{X=\{\Phi,S\}}\int\frac{d^{3}q}{(2\pi)^{3}}\,e_{\lambda}^{ij}(\mathbf{k})\,\frac{q_{i}q_{j}}{k^{2}}\,X_{\mathbf{q}}\,X_{\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{q}}\,I_{X}(x,u,v),

(99)

with the kernel function
I_{X}\left(x,u,v\right)
given by

I_{X}\left(x,u,v\right)\equiv\int_{0}^{x}\mathrm{d}\overline{x}\,G_{h}(x,\overline{x})\,f_{X}\left(\overline{x},u,v\right),

(100)

where
v\equiv q/k
and
u\equiv|\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{q}|/k
parametrise the geometry of the triangle formed by
\mathbf{k}
,
\mathbf{q}
and
\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{q}
.
We define the GW power spectrum, summed over polarizations, as

\mathcal{P}_{h}(k,\eta)\equiv\frac{k^{3}}{2\pi^{2}}\sum_{\lambda}\int d^{3}\mathbf{x}\,e^{i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{x}}\left<h_{\lambda}(\eta,0)\,h_{\lambda}(\eta,\mathbf{x})\right>=\frac{k^{3}}{2\pi^{2}}\sum_{\lambda}\left\langle\left|h_{\lambda}(\mathbf{k},\eta)\right|^{2}\right\rangle_{\rm r},

(101)

where the prefactor
k^{3}/2\pi^{2}
ensures that
\mathcal{P}_{h}(k,\eta)\,d\ln k
is defined per unit of logarithmic interval
[k,\,k+dk]
. The subscript
``\rm r"
denotes that the disconnected contribution proportional to
(2\pi)^{3}\delta^{(3)}(0)
has been removed.
Substituting the expression for
h_{\lambda}
, one gets

\displaystyle\left\langle|h_{\lambda}(\mathbf{k},\eta)|^{2}\right\rangle_{\rm r}

\displaystyle=16\sum_{X=\{\Phi,S\}}\int\frac{d^{3}q}{(2\pi)^{3}}\int\frac{d^{3}p}{(2\pi)^{3}}\,e_{\lambda}^{ij}(\mathbf{k})e_{\lambda}^{mn*}(\mathbf{k})\frac{q_{i}q_{j}}{k^{2}}\frac{p_{m}p_{n}}{k^{2}}

(102)

\displaystyle\times I_{X}(x,u,v)\,I_{X}(x,\bar{u},\bar{v})\,\Big\langle X_{\mathbf{q}}X_{\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{q}}X_{\mathbf{p}}X_{-\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{p}}\Big\rangle_{\rm r},

where
\bar{v}\equiv p/k
and
\bar{u}\equiv|\mathbf{k}+\mathbf{p}|/k
.
Assuming Gaussian scalar perturbations and using Wick’s theorem, one finds

\langle X_{\mathbf{q}}X_{\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{q}}X_{\mathbf{p}}X_{-\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{p}}\rangle_{\rm r}=2\,(2\pi)^{3}\delta^{(3)}(\mathbf{q}+\mathbf{p})\,\frac{(2\pi^{2})^{2}}{q^{3}|\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{q}|^{3}}\mathcal{P}_{X}(q)\mathcal{P}_{X}(|\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{q}|).

(103)

The delta function enforces
\mathbf{p}=-\mathbf{q}
, so that
\bar{v}=v
and

\bar{u}=u
upon integration over
\mathbf{p}
, and both kernel
functions reduce to
I_{X}(x,u,v)
.
Using the polarization completeness relation

\sum_{\lambda}e^{\lambda}_{ij}(\hat{\mathbf{k}})\,e^{\lambda*}_{mn}(\hat{\mathbf{k}})=\frac{1}{2}\left[P_{im}P_{jn}+P_{in}P_{jm}-P_{ij}P_{mn}\right],

(104)

where
P_{ij}\equiv\delta_{ij}-\hat{k}_{i}\hat{k}_{j}

is the projector orthogonal to
\hat{\mathbf{k}}
, we obtain

\sum_{\lambda}\left[e_{\lambda}^{ij}(\mathbf{k})q_{i}q_{j}\right]^{2}=\frac{k^{4}}{32}\left[4v^{2}-(1+v^{2}-u^{2})^{2}\right]^{2}.

(105)

Changing variables to
u=q/k
and
v=|\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{q}|/k
, such that
\mathrm{d}^{3}q=2\pi k^{3}uv\,\mathrm{d}u\,\mathrm{d}v
, the tensor power spectrum reads
[
115
]

\overline{\mathcal{P}_{h}(k,\eta)}=8\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}v\int_{|1-v|}^{1+v}\mathrm{d}u\,\mathcal{K}(u,v)\sum_{X\in\{\Phi,S\}}\overline{I_{X}^{2}(x,k,u,v)}\,\mathcal{P}_{X}(uk)\,\mathcal{P}_{X}(vk),

(106)

with

\mathcal{K}(u,v)=\left(\frac{4v^{2}-(1+v^{2}-u^{2})^{2}}{4uv}\right)^{2}.

(107)

The overline denotes time averaging.
It is sometimes convenient to introduce
t=u+v-1
and
s=u-v
, for which the integration domain factorizes as
t\geq 0
,
|s|\leq 1
and
\mathrm{d}u\,\mathrm{d}v=\tfrac{1}{2}\mathrm{d}t\,\mathrm{d}s
. In these variables,

\overline{\mathcal{P}_{h}(k,\eta)}=4\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t\int_{-1}^{1}\mathrm{d}s\,\mathcal{K}(s,t)\sum_{X\in\{\Phi,S\}}\overline{I_{X}^{2}}\,\mathcal{P}_{X}(ku)\,\mathcal{P}_{X}(kv),

(108)

with

\mathcal{K}(s,t)=\left[\frac{t(2+t)(1-s^{2})}{(1+t)^{2}-s^{2}}\right]^{2}.

(109)

The GW energy density is given by Weinberg’s formula
[
333
,
266
]

\rho_{\rm GW}(\eta)\;=\;\frac{1}{32\pi G\,a^{2}(\eta)}\left\langle h_{ij}^{\prime}(\mathbf{x},\eta)\,h_{ij}^{\prime}(\mathbf{x},\eta)\right\rangle,

(110)

where
\left<\cdot\right>
denotes time and space average over wavelengths small compared to the horizon. Then the GW energy density per logarithmic interval is

\frac{d\rho_{\rm GW}}{d\ln k}\;=\;\frac{k^{2}}{32\pi G\,a^{2}(\eta)}\,\overline{\mathcal{P}_{h}(k,\eta)},

(111)

where
\overline{\mathcal{P}_{h}(k,\eta)}
is the time-average of the tensor power spectrum given in Eq. (
106
).
Finally, the dimensionless GW density parameter relative to the critical density is

\Omega_{\rm GW}(k,\eta)\;\equiv\;\frac{1}{\rho_{\rm tot}(\eta)}\frac{d\rho_{\rm GW}}{d\ln k}\;=\;\frac{k^{2}}{12\mathcal{H}^{2}}\overline{\mathcal{P}_{h}(k,\eta)}.

(112)

At late times
\eta_{\star}\gg\eta_{\rm eva}
, subhorizon GWs propagate freely and
\Omega_{\rm GW}(k,\eta)
is frozen during radiation to a value which we denote
\Omega_{\rm GW,\star}
.

4.2
Redshifting to today

The present-day GW power spectrum reads

\displaystyle\Omega_{\rm GW,0}h^{2}~=~\mathcal{D}(T_{\star},T_{0})~\Omega_{\rm GW,\star}\,,

(113)

where
\mathcal{D}(T_{\star},T_{0})
is the redshift factor of the radiation energy density between temperature
T_{\star}
and
T_{0}
,

\mathcal{D}(T_{\star},T_{0})\equiv h^{2}\frac{\rho_{\rm tot}(T_{\star})}{\rho_{\rm tot}(T_{0})}\left(\frac{a(T_{\star})}{a(T_{0})}\right)^{4}=\Omega_{\rm rad,0}h^{2}\Delta(T_{\star},T_{0}),

(114)

where we have assumed adiabatic evolution
T\propto g_{s,\star}^{-1/3}a^{-1}
for the radiation energy density
\rho_{\rm r}(T)=\pi^{2}g_{\star}T^{4}/30
, and introduced the quantity

\Delta(T_{1},T_{2})\equiv\left(\frac{g_{\star}(T_{1})}{g_{\star}(T_{2})}\right)\left(\frac{g_{s,\star}(T_{2})}{g_{s,\star}(T_{1})}\right)^{4/3}=0.39\left(\frac{g_{\star}(T_{\star})}{106.75}\right)\left(\frac{106.75}{g_{s,\star}(T_{\star})}\right)^{4/3}.

(115)

We use
g_{\star}(T_{0})\simeq 3.38
,
g_{s,\star}(T_{0})\simeq 3.94
, and the effective number of relativistic species
N_{\rm eff}\simeq 3.045

[
270
,
95
]
.
The current fraction of radiation energy is given by
\Omega_{\rm rad,0}h^{2}=h^{2}\rho_{\rm r}(T_{0})/\rho_{0}\simeq 4.18\times 10^{-5}
, with the current temperature of the cosmic microwave background
T_{0}\simeq 2.73~\mathrm{K}

[
335
]
, and the current critical density is
\rho_{0}=3M_{\rm pl}^{2}H_{0}^{2}
with
H_{0}\simeq 100h~\rm km/s/Mpc
and
h
being the reduced Hubble constant. Hence we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(T_{\star},T_{0})\simeq 1.62\times 10^{-5}\left(\frac{g_{\star}(T_{\star})}{106.75}\right)\left(\frac{106.75}{g_{s,\star}(T_{\star})}\right)^{4/3}.

(116)

For an adiabatic evolution, the GW frequency today
f_{0}
redshifts with respect to the frequency at emission
f_{\star}
by a factor

\frac{f_{0}}{f_{\star}}=\frac{a(T_{\star})}{a(T_{0})}=\left(\frac{g_{\star,s}(T_{0})}{g_{\star,s}(T_{\star})}\right)^{1/3}\frac{T_{0}}{T_{\star}}.

(117)

4.3
A roadmap of GW sources

We consider a cosmological history in which the Universe passes through four
successive epochs: an epoch of PBH formation (
\rm form
) around a conformal time

\eta\sim\eta_{f}
(Eq. (
17
)), an initial radiation-dominated
era for
\eta<\eta_{\rm eq}
(
{\rm RD}_{1}
), an early matter-dominated era
(
\rm eMD
) driven by a gas of PBHs during
\eta_{\rm eq}<\eta<\eta_{\rm eva}
,
and a final radiation-dominated era following PBH evaporation for

\eta>\eta_{\rm eva}
(
{\rm RD}_{2}
),

{\rm form}\;\longrightarrow\;{\rm RD}_{1}\;\longrightarrow\;{\rm eMD}\;\longrightarrow\;{\rm RD}_{2}\,.

(118)

Gravitational waves are sourced at second order in the Newtonian potential

\Phi
. The time evolution of the Newtonian potential through each cosmological
epoch is encoded in the transfer function
T_{X}(k,\eta)
, such that

\Phi(k,\eta)=\sum_{X\in\{\Phi,S\}}T_{X}(k,\eta)\,X(k,0)\,\Theta\!\left(k_{\rm{UV}}^{{(X)}}(\eta)-k\right),

(119)

where

T_{X}(k,\eta)=\begin{cases}T_{X}^{\rm(RD_{1})}(k,\eta),&\eta<\eta_{\rm eq},\\[4.0pt]
T_{X}^{\rm(eMD)}(k)\,F_{\Phi}(\eta),&\eta_{\rm eq}<\eta\leq\eta_{\rm osc}(k),\\[4.0pt]
T_{X}^{\rm(eMD)}(k)\,\mathcal{S}_{\Phi}(k)\,T_{\Phi}^{\rm(RD_{2})}(k,\eta),&\eta>\eta_{\rm osc}(k).\end{cases}

(120)

Each factor captures a distinct phase of the evolution:

•

T_{X}^{\rm(RD_{1})}
describes the oscillation and decay of sub-horizon
modes after horizon entry during
\rm RD_{1}
, prior to PBH domination at

\eta_{\rm eq}
(Eq. (
212
)).

•

T_{X}^{\rm(eMD)}
accounts for the subsequent freezing of sub-horizon
modes during the eMD era (Eq. (
57
)).

•

F_{\Phi}(\eta)
describes the exponential decay of the Newtonian
potential
\Phi
toward the end of eMD (Eq. (
84
)).

•

\mathcal{S}_{\Phi}(k)\equiv F_{\Phi}\!\left(\eta_{\rm osc}(k)\right)

encodes the suppression of
\Phi
induced by PBH evaporation, evaluated at
the conformal time
\eta_{\rm osc}(k)\sim\eta_{\rm eva}
at which the
Newtonian potential begins to oscillate
(Eqs. (
83
)
and (
82
)). This is
a central
result of this work
: it encodes the effect of the irreducible broadening of
the PBH mass function
\psi(M_{\rm{PBH}})

(Eq. (
31
)), generalising the monochromatic
approximation of Eq. (
85
) adopted in the
literature
[
213
,
211
,
106
,
293
,
111
,
52
,
112
]
.

•

T_{\Phi}^{\rm(RD_{2})}
describes the oscillatory decay of the potential
in the post-evaporation radiation-dominated era
\rm RD_{2}

(Eq. (
143
)).

Finally, the Heaviside function imposes a UV cutoff

k_{\rm{UV}}^{{(X)}}(\eta)
that removes modes beyond
the regime of validity of the GW calculation — specifically, modes that have
entered the nonlinear regime or that lie outside the range of applicability of
the PBH fluid description (see Sec.
4.4
for details).

Given the cosmological history in Eqs. (
118
)
and (
119
), the integration over GW emission time
\bar{x}

in the kernel function
I_{X}\left(x,u,v\right)
defined in
Eq. (
100
) can be split into four pieces,

\int_{0}^{x}\mathrm{d}\overline{x}=\underbrace{\int_{\mathcal{O}(0.1)x_{\rm f}}^{\mathcal{O}(10)x_{\rm f}}\mathrm{d}\overline{x}}_{\rm(form)}+\underbrace{\int_{\mathcal{O}(10)x_{\rm f}}^{x_{\rm eq}}\mathrm{d}\overline{x}}_{\rm(RD_{1})}+\underbrace{\int_{x_{\rm eq}}^{x_{\rm osc}}\mathrm{d}\overline{x}}_{\rm(eMD)}+\underbrace{\int_{x_{\rm osc}}^{x}\mathrm{d}\overline{x}}_{\rm(RD_{2})},

(121)

where each term corresponds, in chronological order, to GW production during
PBH formation, during
{\rm RD}_{1}
, during
\rm eMD
, and during
the radiation-dominated era following PBH evaporation (
{\rm RD}_{2}
). Neglecting cross-correlations between
components emitted at different epochs
and between adiabatic (
X=\Phi
) and isocurvature
(
X=S
) perturbations (Eq. (
92
)), the total SIGW
spectrum decomposes as (see Tab.
3
)

\Omega_{\rm GW}\simeq\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(form)}[\Phi]+\sum_{X\in\{\Phi,S\}}\left[\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(RD_{1})}[X]+\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(eMD)}[X]+\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(RD_{2})}[X]\right]\,,

(122)

where the bracket
[X]
indicates the nature of the primordial source
X(k,0)
,
with power spectrum given by Eq. (
24
) for

\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(form)}
and by Eqs. (
64
)
and (
65
) for the remaining contributions. We now discuss each contribution in turn, starting from the latest epoch of GW production and moving backward in time.
