Table 3:
Summary of the scalar-induced gravitational-wave sources classified by
epoch, perturbation type, physical origin, and corresponding figures.

1.

\boldsymbol{\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(RD_{2})}}

(Sec.
5
):
The GW signal generated after PBH evaporation splits into two physically
distinct components,

\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(RD_{2})}=\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(RD_{2},\rm Polt.)}+\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(RD_{2},\rm irred.)}\,,

(123)

depending on whether scalar modes re-enter the horizon before
(
k>k_{\rm reh}
) or after (
k<k_{\rm reh}
) evaporation.

For
k>k_{\rm reh}
, the scalar potential
\Phi
remains frozen during eMD
and starts oscillating once radiation domination is restored. This sudden
activation sources strong acoustic oscillations in the radiation fluid,
leading to a parametrically enhanced GW signal scaling as

(k\eta_{\rm eva})^{8}
. The enhancement is controlled by the sharpness of
the eMD-to-
{\rm RD}_{2}
transition and the amplitude of the scalar transfer
functions. This contribution is commonly referred to as
Poltergeist GWs
[
211
,
215
,
293
,
106
,
111
,
294
,
51
,
112
]
.

Both adiabatic and isocurvature perturbations contribute,

\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(RD_{2},\rm Polt.)}=\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(RD_{2},\rm Polt.)}[\Phi]+\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(RD_{2},\rm Polt.)}[S]\,.

(124)

The adiabatic and isocurvature components have been computed in
Refs.
[
211
,
215
,
106
,
51
]

and
Refs.
[
111
,
294
,
51
,
112
]
, respectively. We extend these results to extended PBH
mass distributions, including Choptuik critical scaling.

For
k<k_{\rm reh}
, the modes enter the horizon only after PBH evaporation
and are therefore insensitive to both the intermediate PBH era and PBH
isocurvature perturbations. The resulting signal reduces to the standard
radiation-era contribution,

\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(RD_{2},\rm irred.)}[S]=0,\qquad\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(RD_{2},\rm irred.)}=\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(RD_{2},\rm irred.)}[\Phi]\,,

(125)

already present in the absence of PBHs
[
19
,
44
,
242
]
. We refer to this unavoidable component as
the
irreducible
gravitational-wave background.

2.

\boldsymbol{\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(eMD)}}
(Sec.
6
):
SIGWs sourced during the PBH-dominated era, where the gravitational
potential remains approximately constant
[
242
]
. Both
adiabatic and isocurvature contributions are included,

\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(eMD)}=\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(eMD)}[\Phi]+\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(eMD)}[S]\,.

(126)

The adiabatic component has been studied for constant and time-dependent
decay rates
[
213
,
296
,
297
]
, while the
isocurvature contribution was investigated in
Ref.
[
294
]
. We revisit both contributions in the
context of extended PBH mass distributions.

3.

\boldsymbol{\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(RD_{1})}}
(Sec.
7
):
During the first radiation-dominated era, SIGWs are sourced by both
adiabatic perturbations and PBH isocurvature which we write as

\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(RD_{1})}=\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(RD_{1},irred.)}+\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(RD_{1},univ.)},

(127)

with

\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(RD_{1},irred.)}\equiv\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(RD_{1})}[\Phi],\qquad\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(RD_{1},univ.)}\equiv\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(RD_{1})}[S]\,.

(128)

The adiabatic term is the standard radiation-era SIGW background, and thus
has the same origin as the irreducible component generated after evaporation,

\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(RD_{2},\rm irred.)}
in Eq. (
125
). The isocurvature term is instead
sourced by Poisson fluctuations in the PBH number density. We therefore call
it the
universal
contribution: its spectral shape is fixed by the
statistics of a population of localized objects, largely independently of
their microscopic nature, whether PBHs, solitons, or axion clumps
[
107
,
259
,
258
,
257
,
113
]
.

4.

\boldsymbol{\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(form)}}

(Sec.
8
):
SIGWs generated at PBH formation, sourced by the enhanced primordial
curvature power spectrum responsible for the collapse into PBHs

[
312
,
300
]
. These perturbations source GWs at
second order upon horizon re-entry, directly linking the GW signal to
the small-scale primordial power spectrum.

Additional GW sources associated with PBHs (direct Hawking emission,
gravitational clustering, and binary inspirals) are discussed in a
companion work
[
163
]
and summarized in
Sec.
9
.

4.4
Ultraviolet cut-off
k_{{\mathrm{UV}}}

Our computation of the induced GW spectrum rests on two assumptions, namely that scalar perturbations remain in the linear regime and that the PBH population can be described as a pressureless perfect fluid with
c_{s}^{2}=\omega=0
. Both assumptions break down at sufficiently small scales, which motivates an ultraviolet cut-off
k_{\rm{UV}}
in Eq. (
119
) excluding modes for which the perturbative treatment loses its validity. We adopt two physically motivated choices, the non-linear scale
k_{\rm{NL}}
marking the breakdown of linear theory and the inter-PBH separation
k_{\rm{PBH}}
marking the breakdown of the fluid description of the PBH gas. The two are used as alternatives rather than combined:
k_{\rm{NL}}
provides a conservative lower estimate of the signal by restricting to modes for which linear theory is unambiguously valid, while
k_{\rm{PBH}}
provides a conservative upper bound by extrapolating the linear calculation up to the scale at which the fluid description itself fails. A third cut-off, associated with dissipative damping of the radiation fluid and denoted
k_{D}
, is briefly discussed below for completeness but is not included in the present analysis. The three scales are summarised in Tab.
4
.

Non-linear cut-off.

The most conservative choice retains only modes that remain in the linear regime at the epoch
\eta
of GW emission, that is,
k_{\rm{UV}}^{{(X)}}=k_{\rm{NL}}^{{(X)}}
, with the non-linear scale defined implicitly by the condition that the PBH density contrast reaches unity. For adiabatic and isocurvature initial conditions this gives, respectively,

\displaystyle\delta_{\rm{PBH}}\!\left(k_{\rm{NL}}^{(\Phi)},\eta\right)\equiv 1,\qquad

\displaystyle\Phi(k,\eta)=T_{\Phi}(k,\eta)\,\Phi(k,0),

(129)

\displaystyle\delta_{\rm{PBH}}\!\left(k_{\rm{NL}}^{(S)},\eta\right)\equiv 1,\qquad

\displaystyle\Phi(k,\eta)=T_{S}(k,\eta)\,S(k,0).

(130)

The non-linear scale inherits a time dependence through
\eta
, which matters most during eMD, when the density contrast grows linearly with the scale factor. As shown in App.
G.3
, evaluating the cut-off at the end of the eMD era is an excellent approximation, so that Eqs. (
129
) and (
130
) are understood to hold at
\eta\simeq\eta_{\rm eva}
. Combining them with the sub-horizon Poisson equation,

k^{2}\Phi(k,\eta)=\frac{3}{2}\mathcal{H}^{2}\delta_{\rm tot},

(131)

and the transfer-function decomposition of Eq. (
56
), one obtains

k_{\rm{NL}}\simeq\frac{\sqrt{3/2}\;k_{\rm eva}}{\left[T_{\Phi}^{\rm(eMD)}(k_{\rm{NL}})\,\Phi(k_{\rm{NL}},0)+T_{S}^{\rm(eMD)}(k_{\rm{NL}})\,S(k_{\rm{NL}},0)\right]^{1/2}},

(132)

where
T_{X}^{\rm(eMD)}(k)
from Eq. (
57
) describes the eMD evolution of adiabatic (
X=\Phi
) and isocurvature (
X=S
) perturbations. Since the two channels are statistically uncorrelated (Eq. (
92
)), their non-linear scales can be determined independently,

k_{\rm{NL}}^{\rm adia}\simeq\frac{\sqrt{3/2}\;k_{\rm eva}}{\left[T_{\Phi}^{\rm(eMD)}(k_{\rm{NL}}^{\rm adia})\,\Phi(k_{\rm{NL}}^{\rm adia},0)\right]^{1/2}},\qquad k_{\rm{NL}}^{\rm iso}\simeq\frac{\sqrt{3/2}\;k_{\rm eva}}{\left[T_{S}^{\rm(eMD)}(k_{\rm{NL}}^{\rm iso})\,S(k_{\rm{NL}}^{\rm iso},0)\right]^{1/2}}.

(133)

Substituting
\Phi(k,0)=\mathcal{P}_{\Phi}^{1/2}(k)
and
S(k,0)=\mathcal{P}_{S}^{1/2}(k)
from Eqs. (
64
) and (
65
), and evaluating the adiabatic spectrum at
k_{\rm eva}
, yields

\displaystyle k_{\rm{NL}}^{\rm adia}

\displaystyle\simeq 235\,k_{\rm eva}\left(\frac{k_{\rm eva}}{k_{\rm CMB}}\right)^{(1-n_{s})/4}\left[\frac{9/10}{T_{\Phi}^{\rm(eMD)}(k_{\rm{NL}}^{\rm adia})}\right],

(134)

\displaystyle k_{\rm{NL}}^{\rm iso}

\displaystyle\simeq\left(\frac{675\pi}{8}\right)^{1/7}k_{\rm eva}^{4/7}\,k_{\rm{PBH}}^{3/7}\left[\frac{1/5}{T_{S}^{\rm(eMD)}(k_{\rm{NL}}^{\rm iso})}\right].

(135)

The non-linear scale sets the maximum wavenumber that can be consistently included within perturbation theory and therefore controls both the peak frequency and the amplitude of the GW spectrum. The signal is maximised when
\beta_{\rm f}\gg\beta_{\rm c}
, since then
k_{\rm eq}>k_{\rm{NL}}
and the dominant modes enter the horizon during the eMD phase rather than before it. In that regime, the transfer functions reduce to their super-horizon plateau values
T_{\Phi}^{\rm(eMD)}\simeq 9/10
and
T_{S}^{\rm(eMD)}\simeq 1/5
.

The use of
k_{\rm{NL}}
as the ultraviolet cut-off, set by the strict condition
\delta_{\rm{PBH}}=1
, is a deliberately conservative choice in light of the lattice simulations of Ref.
[
134
]
. Those simulations find that GW production during an eMD epoch remains efficient up to the light-crossing scale of the largest halos forming prior to reheating,
k_{\rm cross}\simeq 8\,k_{\rm{NL}}
, beyond which the signal is suppressed by the incoherence of emission across the halo volume. In the intermediate range
k_{\rm{NL}}<k<k_{\rm cross}
, non-linear halo collapse contributes additional power on top of the linear prediction, so that truncating the integral at
k_{\rm{NL}}
underestimates the eMD-era contribution
\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(eMD)}
. No equivalent lattice study is currently available for the post-reheating Poltergeist component
\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(Polt.)}
, which is sourced during radiation domination by the rapid decay of scalar potentials at the end of the eMD phase, and we apply the same conservative prescription in that regime.

Fluid cut-off.

The alternative choice extrapolates the linear calculation beyond
k_{\rm{NL}}^{{(X)}}
up to the scale at which the fluid description of the PBH gas itself breaks down. The fluid approximation is valid only on scales larger than the mean comoving inter-PBH separation at formation, which sets

k_{\rm{UV}}^{{(X)}}=k_{\rm{PBH}},\qquad k_{\rm{PBH}}\equiv a_{f}\!\left(\frac{4\pi}{3}\,n_{\rm{PBH}}(t_{f})\right)^{1/3}.

(136)

This extrapolation includes modes for which linear theory no longer applies and therefore overestimates the GW amplitude. The resulting spectrum should accordingly be interpreted as a conservative upper bound. Including it is nevertheless useful in the present context, since one of the central conclusions of this work is that the GW signal from PBH reheating is substantially smaller than previously estimated in the literature, and any overestimate only strengthens that conclusion.
15
15

15

Note that
k_{\rm{PBH}}
coincides with the initial isocurvature non-linearity scale
k_{\rm{NL}}^{(S)}(0)
, defined by
\mathcal{P}_{S}\!\left(k_{\rm{NL}}^{(S)}(0)\right)\equiv 1
, up to a numerical factor
(3\pi/2)^{1/3}\simeq 1.67
. Because PBH density perturbations grow as
\delta_{\rm{PBH}}\propto a
during matter domination, however, the non-linearity scale shifts to significantly smaller wavenumbers by the time of evaporation,
k_{\rm{NL}}^{(S)}(\langle\eta_{\rm eva}\rangle)\ll k_{\rm{NL}}^{(S)}(0)
, so that the two cut-offs can differ substantially.

Dissipation cut-off.

The radiation fluid is not perfect. Photons, neutrinos, and other relativistic species have finite mean free paths
\ell_{\rm mfp}
, and on scales comparable to
\ell_{\rm mfp}
they diffuse across overdense regions into underdense ones, erasing the underlying scalar inhomogeneity. In a radiation-dominated background this process is known as Silk damping
[
319
,
332
,
205
,
220
]
and is governed by shear viscosity, with heat conduction subdominant at early times. The net effect on linear scalar perturbations is an exponential suppression of the Newtonian potential on small scales
[
104
,
344
]
,

\Phi(k,\eta)\simeq\Phi_{\rm rad}(k,\eta)\,\exp\!\left[-\frac{k^{2}}{k_{D}^{2}(\eta)}\right],

(137)

where
\Phi_{\rm rad}
is the perfect-fluid solution and the comoving Silk damping scale
k_{D}
is the cumulative diffusion length built up over a Hubble time,

k_{D}^{-2}(\eta)=\int_{0}^{\eta}{\rm d}\tilde{\eta}\,\frac{2\,\eta_{\rm sv}(\tilde{\eta})}{3a(\rho+p)}\sim\mathcal{H}^{-1}(\eta)\,\ell_{\rm mfp}(\eta),\qquad\ell_{\rm mfp}(\eta)\sim\frac{1}{a(\eta)\,n_{j}(\eta)\,\sigma_{j}(\eta)},

(138)

with
\eta_{\rm sv}
the shear viscosity of the plasma,
n_{j}
and
\sigma_{j}
the number density and scattering cross-section of the species
j
dominating the viscosity, and all lengths comoving. The middle expression makes the physical content transparent:
k_{D}^{-1}
is the geometric mean of the Hubble radius and the mean free path. Refs.
[
104
,
344
]
have shown that this scale has important consequences for the standard SIGW background, and analogous modifications are expected for the Poltergeist mechanism, though a quantitative determination of
k_{D}
in the PBH-reheating scenario lies beyond the scope of the present work.

A second dissipative effect, distinct from the suppression in Eq. (
137
), acts during the post-reheating phase. Once
\Phi
resumes oscillating as an acoustic wave in the radiation fluid, the same viscous transport drains energy from these oscillations at a rate
\tau_{d}^{-1}(k)\sim k^{2}\,\mathcal{H}(\eta)/k_{D}^{2}(\eta)
, so that the oscillating Newtonian potential acts as a coherent GW source only over a finite window of duration
\tau_{d}(k)
. The resulting suppression of the low-frequency tail of the GW spectrum is analogous to the mechanism identified in Ref.
[
104
]
and is expected to be subdominant relative to the cut-off on the scalar source in Eq. (
137
). We leave both effects for future work.

[Eq. (

)]
