Summary of the different cut-off scales used in this work, see discussion in Sec.
4.4
.

5
SIGWs following PBH evaporation

In this section we compute the gravitational-wave signal emitted during the radiation era that follows PBH evaporation (
\rm RD_{2}
), when the frozen Newtonian potential resumes oscillating. The signal naturally splits into two contributions, distinguished by when the sourcing scalar modes crossed the Hubble horizon:

•

modes that entered during the preceding eMD phase, remained frozen until evaporation, and are released at reheating — their GW contribution is the Poltergeist signal
\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(RD_{2},Polt.)}[\Phi]
.

•

modes that enter only during
\rm RD_{2}
itself and contribute as in a standard radiation era — their GW contribution is the irreducible background
\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(RD_{2},irred.)}[\Phi]
.

The two populations are separated by the reheating wavenumber
k_{\rm reh}
, the comoving Hubble scale at evaporation:

\Phi(k,\eta)=\underbrace{\Phi_{\rm eMD}(k,\eta)\,\Theta(k-k_{\rm reh})}_{\displaystyle\;\to\;\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(RD_{2},Polt.)}[\Phi]}\;+\;\underbrace{\Phi_{\rm RD_{2}}(k,\eta)\,\Theta(k_{\rm reh}-k)}_{\displaystyle\;\to\;\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(RD_{2},irred.)}[\Phi]}.

(139)

Inserted into the quadratic GW source, the two pieces of
\Phi
generate the two adiabatic contributions indicated under the braces. Adding the isocurvature contribution sourced by the PBH-number-density fluctuations
S
, the total
\rm RD_{2}
SIGW spectrum reads

\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(RD_{2})}=\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(RD_{2},Polt.)}[\Phi]+\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(RD_{2},irred.)}[\Phi]+\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(RD_{2},Polt.)}[S],

(140)

where
[\Phi]
and
[S]
label the adiabatic and isocurvature seed channels. The irreducible isocurvature contribution vanishes, since post-evaporation modes are insensitive to the PBH Poisson source, which has decayed by then.
Mixed terms arising from cross-correlations between the Poltergeist and irreducible mode sets are neglected: where their frequency supports overlap, such terms can at most enhance the total signal by an
\mathcal{O}(1)
factor through constructive interference, or partially suppress it through destructive interference. We show the representative benchmark spectra in Fig.
13
. The technical derivations are collected in App.
F
.

5.1
The Poltergeist kernel

The dominant contribution to Eq. (
122
),
denoted
\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(RD_{2})}
, is generated during the reheating
stage immediately following PBH evaporation.
At this stage, the sudden disappearance of the PBH-dominated matter
component triggers rapid oscillations of the Newtonian potential in the
subsequent RD era (noted
\rm RD_{2}
).
These oscillations correspond to acoustic waves in the radiation fluid
and act as an efficient source of SIGWs
[
213
]
.
The sharper the transition between the PBH-dominated and RD epochs,
the stronger the resulting sound waves and the larger the induced GW signal.
In the case of PBH evaporation, the decay rate develops a pole,

\Gamma\to\infty
as
t\to t_{\rm eva}
, leading to an almost
instantaneous reheating.
This extreme non-adiabaticity gives rise to a pronounced GW signal,
commonly referred to as the Poltergeist mechanism
[
211
,
106
,
293
,
111
,
52
,
112
]
, which typically dominates GW production from evaporating PBHs.
We now outline the calculation of the Poltergeist GW spectrum

\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(RD_{2})}
. Full details are provided in
App.
F
.

Immediately after PBH evaporation, the Newtonian potential is set by its
value at the onset of oscillations,
\Phi(k,\eta_{\rm osc})
, given in
Eq. (
75
), and subsequently evolves as

\Phi(k,\eta>\eta_{\rm osc})=\sum_{X\in\{\Phi,S\}}T_{X}(k,\eta)\,X(k,0),

(141)

where the composite transfer function factorises as (Eq. (
120
))

T_{X}(k,\eta)=T_{X}^{\rm(eMD)}(k)\,\mathcal{S}_{\Phi}(k)\,T_{\Phi}^{\rm(RD_{2})}(k,\eta).

(142)

Each factor encodes a distinct phase of the evolution:

T_{X}^{\rm(eMD)}
describes the early matter-dominated era
(Eq. (
57
)),
\mathcal{S}_{\Phi}
the
suppression induced by PBH evaporation
(Eq. (
83
)), and

T_{\Phi}^{\rm(RD_{2})}
the subsequent oscillatory decay during
radiation domination
[
106
]
,

T_{\Phi}^{\rm(RD_{2})}(k,\eta)=\frac{C_{1}\,j_{1}(c_{s}k\bar{\eta})+C_{2}\,y_{1}(c_{s}k\bar{\eta})}{c_{s}k\bar{\eta}},\qquad\bar{\eta}\equiv\eta-\frac{\eta_{\rm eva}}{2},\qquad c_{s}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}},

(143)

where
j_{n}
and
y_{n}
are spherical Bessel functions of the first and
second kind, respectively.
Imposing continuity of
\Phi
and
\Phi^{\prime}
across the transition fixes
the integration constants,

C_{1}=-\frac{1}{8}\left(c_{s}k\eta_{\rm eva}\right)^{3}y_{2}\!\left(\frac{c_{s}k\eta_{\rm eva}}{2}\right),\qquad C_{2}=\frac{1}{8}\left(c_{s}k\eta_{\rm eva}\right)^{3}j_{2}\!\left(\frac{c_{s}k\eta_{\rm eva}}{2}\right).

(144)

The source function defined in Eq. (
93
), during
\rm RD_{2}
, reads

\displaystyle f_{X}^{\rm(RD_{2})}(\eta,u,v)

\displaystyle=T_{X}(ku,\eta)\,T_{X}(kv,\eta)+\tfrac{1}{2}\!\left(T_{X}(ku,\eta)+\tfrac{T_{X}^{\prime}(ku,\eta)}{\mathcal{H}}\right)\!\left(T_{X}(kv,\eta)+\tfrac{T_{X}^{\prime}(kv,\eta)}{\mathcal{H}}\right).

(145)

Near reheating, where oscillation amplitudes are largest and GW
production is most efficient, the
T_{X}^{\prime}
terms are enhanced by a
factor
(k/k_{\rm eva})^{2}
relative to the remaining terms,
so the source simplifies to

\displaystyle f_{X}^{\rm(RD_{2})}(\eta,u,v)\simeq\frac{T_{X}^{\prime}(ku,\eta)\,T_{X}^{\prime}(kv,\eta)}{2\mathcal{H}^{2}}.

(146)

This enhancement arises because modes that were frozen during the
PBH-dominated era begin to oscillate on timescales much shorter than
the Hubble time — a defining feature of the Poltergeist mechanism.

The contribution to the GW kernel, defined in Eq. (
100
), due to GW emission after the
\Phi
starts oscillating is

I^{\rm(RD_{2})}_{X}(\bar{x},u,v)\equiv\int_{x_{\rm osc}}^{x}\mathrm{d}\overline{x}\,G_{h}(x,\overline{x})\,f_{X}^{\rm(RD_{2})}\left(\overline{x},u,v\right).

(147)

Since
\eta_{\rm osc}\in[\eta_{\rm eva},\eta_{\rm cut}]
, with
\eta_{\rm cut}\simeq 1.26\eta_{\rm eva}
(see Eq. (
81
)), we can safely identify
x_{\rm osc}\sim x_{\rm eva}
in the lower boundary of Eq. (
147
).
Substituting Eq. (
146
) into
Eq. (
147
) yields the kernel function

\displaystyle I^{\rm(RD_{2})}_{X}(\bar{x},u,v)\simeq\frac{1}{2k^{2}}\int_{x_{\rm eva}/2}^{\bar{x}}\mathrm{d}\tilde{\bar{x}}\;\tilde{\bar{x}}^{2}\,G_{h}^{\rm(RD_{2})}(\bar{x},\tilde{\bar{x}})\,T_{X}^{\prime}\!\left(uk,\tilde{\eta}\right)T_{X}^{\prime}\!\left(vk,\tilde{\eta}\right),\qquad X\in\{\Phi,S\},

(148)

where
\bar{x}\equiv k\bar{\eta}=x-x_{\rm eva}/2
,
x\equiv k\eta
,

x_{\rm eva}\equiv k\eta_{\rm eva}
, and

\tilde{\eta}=(\tilde{\bar{x}}+x_{\rm eva}/2)/k
.
The tensor Green function
G_{h}^{\rm(RD_{2})}(\bar{x},\tilde{\bar{x}})
is given by
Eq. (
445
).
In the case of interest,
x_{\rm eva}\gg 1
, differentiating
Eq. (
120
) gives
[
106
]

\displaystyle T_{X}^{\prime}\!\left(uk,\tilde{\eta}\right)\simeq-\,\mathcal{S}_{\Phi}(uk)\,T_{X}^{\rm(eMD)}(uk)\,c_{s}uk\left(\frac{x_{\rm eva}}{2\tilde{\bar{x}}}\right)^{\!2}\sin\!\left[c_{s}u\left(\tilde{\bar{x}}-x_{\rm eva}/2\right)\right].

(149)

Inserting Eqs. (
149
) and (
98
) into
Eq. (
148
) yields

I^{\rm(RD_{2})}_{X}(\bar{x},u,v)\simeq\frac{c_{s}^{2}uv}{32\bar{x}}\,x_{\mathrm{eva}}^{4}\,\mathcal{S}_{\Phi}(uk)\,\mathcal{S}_{\Phi}(vk)\,T_{X}^{\rm(eMD)}(uk)\,T_{X}^{\rm(eMD)}(vk)\,\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{osc}}(\bar{x},u,v),

(150)

where the oscillatory integral is

\mathcal{I}_{\mathrm{osc}}(\bar{x}\to\infty,u,v)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{\mathrm{d}x_{2}}{x_{2}+x_{3}}\,\sin(x_1-x_2)\,\sin(c_s u\, x_2)\,\sin(c_s v\, x_2),

(151)

with
x_{1}\equiv\bar{x}-x_{\rm eva}/2
,

x_{2}\equiv\tilde{\bar{x}}-x_{\rm eva}/2
,
and
x_{3}\equiv x_{\rm eva}/2
.
The GW spectrum sourced by the Poltergeist effect
during PBH reheating (
\rm RD_{2}
), as given by
Eq. (
106
), takes the form

\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(RD_{2})}(k,\eta_{\rm eva})=\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(RD_{2})}[\Phi]+\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(RD_{2})}[S],

(152)

with

\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(RD_{2})}[X]=\frac{1}{864}\left(\frac{k}{k_{\rm eva}}\right)^{\!8}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}v\int_{|1-v|}^{1+v}\mathrm{d}u\;\left[4v^{2}-\left(1+v^{2}-u^{2}\right)^{2}\right]^{2}\\
\times\;\overline{\mathcal{I}_{\rm osc}^{2}}(u,v)\,\mathcal{S}_{\Phi}^{2}(uk)\,\mathcal{S}_{\Phi}^{2}(vk)\,T_{X}^{(\rm eMD)\,2}(uk)\,T_{X}^{(\rm eMD)\,2}(vk)\,\mathcal{P}_{X}(uk)\,\mathcal{P}_{X}(vk),

(153)

where
X\in\{\Phi,S\}
labels adiabatic and isocurvature modes,

k_{\rm eva}
is the comoving horizon scale at PBH evaporation,
and the primordial power spectra
\mathcal{P}_{X}
are defined in
Eqs. (
64
) and (
65
). All intermediate steps leading to Eq. (
153
),
including the explicit form of the kernel, are presented in
App.
F
.
The present-day GW abundance is obtained by redshifting the spectrum
from the evaporation epoch,

\Omega_{\rm GW,0}^{\rm(RD_{2})}(k)=\mathcal{D}(T_{\rm eva},T_{0})\,\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(RD_{2})}(k,\eta_{\rm eva}),

(154)

where the redshift factor
\mathcal{D}(T_{\rm eva},T_{0})
is defined in
Eq. (
116
).
The remaining momentum integrals are evaluated both analytically and
numerically. The resulting GW spectra are presented in
Sec.
5.2
.

5.2
Resonant and low-frequency contributions

As discussed above, the Poltergeist mechanism sources gravitational waves
when scalar perturbations frozen during the PBH-dominated era begin to
oscillate after reheating. In the regime
k/k_{\rm eva}\gg 1
relevant for
PBH evaporation, the resulting SIGW spectrum admits a transparent analytic
interpretation that captures its dominant features. Using
Eq. (
96
), the tensor modes at first order in
\mathcal{O}(k_{\rm eva}/k)
can be written as

h_{\lambda}(\mathbf{k},\eta)\simeq\int_{0}^{\eta}\mathrm{d}\bar{\eta}\,G_{h}(\eta,\bar{\eta})\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}\mathbf{q}}{(2\pi)^{3}}\;\mathcal{K}_{\lambda}(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{q})\,\Phi_{\mathbf{q}}^{\prime}(\bar{\eta})\,\Phi_{\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{q}}^{\prime}(\bar{\eta}),

(155)

where
\mathcal{K}_{\lambda}(\mathbf{k},\mathbf{q})\equiv 2\,e_{\lambda}^{ij}(\mathbf{k})\,q_{i}\,q_{j}/\mathcal{H}^{2}
is a kinematic kernel, and the tensor Green function
and scalar modes oscillate as (see Eqs. (
463
)
and (
441
))

G_{h}(\eta,\bar{\eta})\propto\sin\!\left[k(\eta-\bar{\eta})\right],\qquad\Phi_{\mathbf{q}}(\bar{\eta})\propto\sin\!\left[c_{s}q\!\left(\bar{\eta}+\tfrac{\eta_{\rm eva}}{2}\right)\right].

(156)

After reheating, the tensor Green function oscillates with frequency
k
,
while scalar perturbations propagate as acoustic waves with sound speed

c_{s}=1/\sqrt{3}
. Two kinematic configurations dominate GW
production. Detailed derivations are given in App.
F
.

1.

Resonant configuration.

This contribution arises when the two scalar mode frequencies together
match the tensor frequency,

|\mathbf{k}|\simeq c_{s}\!\left(|\mathbf{q}|+|\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{k}|\right),\qquad\text{or equivalently}\qquad u+v\simeq c_{s}^{-1}.

(157)

The scalar source then oscillates in phase with the tensor Green function,
so contributions accumulate coherently over time. In the long-time limit,
the time integral produces a Dirac delta that enforces the kinematic resonance
condition, yielding (see Eq. (
474
))

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{I}^{2}_{\rm osc,res}}(u,v)

\displaystyle\simeq\frac{1}{32}\,\mathrm{Ci}^{2}\!\left(\left|1-c_{s}(u+v)\frac{k}{k_{\rm eva}}\right|\right)\;\xrightarrow{\;k\gg k_{\rm eva}\;}\frac{\pi c_{s}}{32}\frac{k_{\rm eva}}{k}\,\delta\!\left(c_{s}^{-1}-u-v\right),

(158)

where the cosine and sine integrals are defined as

\mathrm{Ci}(x)\equiv-\int_{x}^{\infty}\frac{\cos t}{t}\,\mathrm{d}t,\qquad\mathrm{Si}(x)\equiv\int_{0}^{x}\frac{\sin t}{t}\,\mathrm{d}t.

(159)

2.

Low-frequency configuration.

The second contribution originates from configurations in which both scalar
momenta are comparable and much larger than the GW wavenumber,

|\mathbf{q}|\sim|\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{k}|\gg|\mathbf{k}|,\qquad\text{or equivalently}\qquad u\sim v\gg 1.

(160)

In this regime the relevant phase of the time integral is

\Omega=k-c_{s}\!\left(|\mathbf{q}|-|\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{q}|\right)
.
Since
|\mathbf{q}|\simeq|\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{q}|
, the difference of scalar
frequencies satisfies
c_{s}\!\left(|\mathbf{q}|-|\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{q}|\right)\sim\mathcal{O}(k)
, so the phase
\Omega
varies slowly compared to the
individual scalar oscillations. For an oscillatory integral

\int\mathrm{d}\eta\,e^{i\Omega\eta}\sim 1/\Omega
, the oscillation-averaged
square scales as
1/\Omega^{2}
. The kernel is therefore enhanced by the small
frequency mismatch, leading to (see Eq. (
475
))

\displaystyle\overline{\mathcal{I}^{2}_{\rm osc,LF}}(u,v)

\displaystyle\simeq\frac{1}{8}\!\left[\mathrm{Ci}^{2}\!\left(\frac{k}{k_{\rm eva}}\right)+\left(\frac{\pi}{2}-\mathrm{Si}\!\left(\frac{k}{k_{\rm eva}}\right)\right)^{\!2}\right]\simeq\frac{1}{8}\frac{k_{\rm eva}^{2}}{k^{2}}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\frac{k_{\rm eva}}{k}\right)^{\!3}.

(161)

The Poltergeist-induced GW spectrum in Eq. (
152
) can
accordingly be written as the superposition of a resonant and a low-frequency
contribution for both adiabatic and isocurvature perturbations,

\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(RD_{2})}(k,\eta_{\rm eva})\simeq\sum_{X\in\{\Phi,S\}}\Bigl[\Omega_{\rm GW,res}^{\rm(RD_{2})}[X]+\Omega_{\rm GW,LF}^{\rm(RD_{2})}[X]\Bigr].

(162)

The resonant contribution captures the dominant part of the signal and
determines its overall shape and normalisation, while the low-frequency
contribution affects only the infrared tail and is numerically subdominant.
Both can be treated analytically. The derivations and their regimes of validity
are discussed in App.
F
.

We now summarise the final interpolating expressions for the resonant
contribution that are used throughout the remainder of this work. These
expressions provide a compact and accurate description of the dominant GW
signal and interpolate smoothly between the relevant momentum regimes. For
adiabatic perturbations,

\Omega_{\rm GW,res}^{\rm(RD_{2})}[\Phi]\simeq\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{R}}^{2}\left(\frac{k}{k_{\rm eva}}\right)^{\!5/3}\left(\frac{k}{k_{\rm CMB}}\right)^{\!2n_{s}-2}\!\!\!\!\frac{7.7\times 10^{-3}}{\bigl[1+0.24\,(k/k_{\rm eq})^{-4n(\omega)/9}\bigr]^{9}},

(163)

with
n(1/3)\simeq-1.83
(see Eq. (
481
)), while for isocurvature perturbations,

\Omega_{\rm GW,res}^{\rm(RD_{2})}[S]\simeq\left(\frac{k}{k_{\rm{PBH}}}\right)^{6}\left(\frac{k}{k_{\rm eva}}\right)^{5/3}\,\frac{1.5\times 10^{-6}}{\left[1+0.38\,\left(k/k_{\rm eq}\right)\right]^{8}}.

(164)

The derivation of these expressions, together with a discussion of their
regimes of validity, is presented in App.
F
.
The present-day GW energy density is obtained from Eq. (
154
).

Comparison with numerical results

In Fig.
13
, we compare our analytical estimates for the peak
amplitude of the SIGW spectrum with full numerical results for

\Omega_{\rm GW,0}
, finding excellent agreement across the relevant parameter
space for both adiabatic and isocurvature sources.
The figure also shows a direct comparison between the SIGW signal from a
monochromatic PBH mass
16
16

16

We also confirm earlier studies on monochromatic PBHs
[
211
]
as shown in Fig.
23
.
function and our new results for an extended mass
distribution following Choptuik’s critical scaling with a sharp UV cut-off.
In both cases, numerical results (dashed) are shown alongside the corresponding
analytical approximations (solid). The extended Choptuik mass distribution
leads to a substantial suppression of the GW signal — by several orders of
magnitude relative to the monochromatic case — primarily due to enhanced
damping at large wavenumbers. This suppression affects both the adiabatic and
isocurvature contributions, with the latter exhibiting an even stronger
reduction. The reason is that the isocurvature power spectrum peaks at high
wavenumbers (
k\sim k_{\rm{PBH}}
), where the suppression factor scales as

\mathcal{S}_{\Phi}^{4}(k)\sim(k_{\rm{PBH}}/k_{\rm eva})^{-16/3}

(Eq. (
83
)), compared to the milder

(k_{\rm{PBH}}/k_{\rm eva})^{-4/3}
(Eq. (
85
)) for a
monochromatic mass function.

5.3
Maximal GW signal

We now estimate the maximal post-reheating Poltergeist signal for the two UV
prescriptions introduced in Sec.
4.4
and summarised in
Tab.
4
. Their impact on the spectrum is shown in
Fig.
13
.

NL cutoff.

The conservative choice

k_{\rm{UV}}=k_{\rm{NL}}
cuts the convolution at the
scale where the scalar source becomes non-linear. For the adiabatic
contribution, the maximal signal follows by evaluating
Eq. (
163
) near

k\simeq k_{\rm{NL}}^{\rm adia}\lesssim k_{\rm eq}
. The
low-frequency branch of Eq. (
486
) is subdominant, and after
redshifting to today we obtain

\Omega_{\rm GW,0}^{\rm(RD_{2})}[\Phi]\!\left(k_{\rm{NL}}^{\rm adia}\right)h^{2}\simeq 1.8\times 10^{-22}\left(\frac{M_{\rm{PBH}}}{10^{4}\,\mathrm{g}}\right)^{1/10}\lesssim 1.4\times 10^{-21},

(165)

where the upper value corresponds to

M_{\rm{PBH}}=10^{9}\,{\rm g}
, the largest mass compatible with
BBN. The isocurvature contribution is maximized at

k\simeq k_{\rm{NL}}^{\rm iso}\lesssim k_{\rm eq}
, a hierarchy
realized for large initial PBH fractions, and gives

\Omega_{\rm GW,0}^{\rm(RD_{2})}[S]\!\left(k_{\rm{NL}}^{\rm iso}\right)h^{2}\simeq 5.8\times 10^{-26}\left(\frac{M_{\rm{PBH}}}{10^{4}\,\mathrm{g}}\right)^{-38/21}\leq 1.0\times 10^{-18},

(166)

with the upper value reached at

M_{\rm{PBH}}=1\,{\rm g}
, the smallest mass allowed by the
maximal reheating temperature, Eq. (
255
). Thus, with the
non-linear cutoff, both adiabatic and isocurvature Poltergeist
signals remain below the projected sensitivity of planned GW experiments, see Fig.
2
-left-middle.

Fluid cutoff.

The aggressive choice

k_{\rm{UV}}=k_{\rm{PBH}}
extends the convolution to
the mean PBH separation. Since
k_{\rm{PBH}}/k_{\rm eq}\propto\beta_{f}^{-2/3}
(Eq. (
19
)), this scale lies above the spectral
maximum for any realistic
\beta_{f}
, so that the signal is cut only after its peak, the approximation is exact for small
\beta_{f}
and receives only mild corrections for
\beta_{f}=\mathcal{O}(1)
.
Evaluating Eqs. (
163
) and (
164
), the adiabatic and isocurvature spectra peak at

k_{\rm peak}^{\rm adia}\simeq 1.3\,k_{\rm eq}
and

k_{\rm peak}^{\rm iso}\simeq 60.5\,k_{\rm eq}
, respectively, giving

\displaystyle\Omega_{\rm GW,0}^{\rm(RD_{2})}[\Phi]\!\left(k_{\rm peak}^{\rm adia}\right)h^{2}

\displaystyle\simeq 8.2\times 10^{-26}\left(\frac{k_{\rm eq}}{k_{\rm eva}}\right)^{5/3}\simeq 1.7\times 10^{-20}\left(\frac{M_{\rm{PBH}}}{10^{4}\,{\rm g}}\right)^{10/9}\left(\frac{\beta_{f}}{10^{-5}}\right)^{10/9},

(167)

\displaystyle\Omega_{\rm GW,0}^{\rm(RD_{2})}[S]\!\left(k_{\rm peak}^{\rm iso}\right)h^{2}

\displaystyle\simeq 1.0\times 10^{-18}\,\frac{k_{\rm eq}^{23/3}}{k_{\rm eva}^{5/3}\,k_{\rm{PBH}}^{6}}\simeq 2.7\times 10^{-27}\left(\frac{M_{\rm{PBH}}}{10^{4}\,{\rm g}}\right)^{10/9}\left(\frac{\beta_{f}}{10^{-5}}\right)^{46/9}.

(168)

This prescription gives an optimistic upper envelope within the coarse-grained
PBH-fluid description, but still does not yield an observable signal in most of the parameter space. We check that the Poltergeist signal is the only contribution leading to the
\Delta N_{\rm eff}
and future GW observatories constraints shown in Fig.
2
-right-middle.

Fig.
13
(bottom panel) confirms that, for the extended Choptuik mass distribution, the peak amplitude is essentially insensitive to the choice of cutoff as long as
k_{\rm{UV}}\gg k_{\rm eq}
: the dominant contribution comes from modes near
k_{\rm eq}
, well below either UV scale. In contrast, the monochromatic isocurvature spectrum peaks at
k_{\rm{UV}}
itself and is therefore strongly cutoff-dependent.

The irreducible contribution from inflationary SIGWs

After evaporation, the Universe returns to RD, and scalar perturbations that enter the horizon during this second RD era,
\mathrm{RD}_{2}
, inevitably source SIGWs. As discussed in App.
7.2
, this contribution can be estimated using the standard radiation-dominated result for a power-law primordial curvature spectrum. Since SIGWs are dominantly generated around horizon entry during radiation domination, only modes entering after the onset of
\mathrm{RD}_{2}
contribute to this irreducible signal. We therefore approximate the present-day contribution by

\Omega_{{\rm GW},0}^{\rm(RD_{2})}(k)\simeq\mathcal{D}(T,T_{0})\,Q(n_{s})A_{\zeta}^{2}\left(\frac{k}{k_{\rm CMB}}\right)^{2(n_{s}-1)}\Theta(k_{\rm eva}-k),

(169)

where
\mathcal{D}(T,T_{0})
accounts for the standard redshifting to today. This
\mathrm{RD}_{2}
signal is not diluted by the preceding
\rm eMD
and provides an irreducible SIGW background associated with the primordial scalar power spectrum.

Figure 13:

Comparison of the Poltergeist SIGW signal for adiabatic
\Phi
and isocurvature
S
initial conditions. We compare monochromatic (gray) and an extended
Choptuik-scaling (blue) PBH mass distribution. Numerical (dashed) and analytical (solid) results agree
well and demonstrate significant suppression in the extended case.
We fix
\beta_{\rm f}=0.6\times 10^{-6}
and
M_{\rm{PBH}}=10^{4}\rm g
for comparability with prior
works.
Left:
Non-linear cutoff
k_{\rm{UV}}=k_{\rm{NL}}
.
Right:
Fluid cutoff
k_{\rm{UV}}=k_{\rm{PBH}}
.

Bottom:
Solid and dashed lines correspond to the fluid
cut-off
k_{\rm{UV}}=k_{\rm{PBH}}
and the non-linear cut-off

k_{\rm{UV}}=k_{\rm{NL}}
, respectively.

6
SIGWs during PBH domination

In this section we compute the SIGW signal emitted during the PBH-dominated, early matter-dominated (eMD) era, between
\eta_{\rm eq}
and the onset of reheating. Throughout this phase the Newtonian potential
\Phi
does not decay on sub-horizon scales, so the quadratic source
f_{X}
in Eq. (
95
) remains active and sources GWs continuously. The total signal,

\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(eMD,\,PBH)}=\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(eMD,\,PBH)}[\Phi]+\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(eMD,\,PBH)}[S],

(170)

combines two contributions: a continuous build-up of the GW amplitude, proportional to the scale factor
a
, fed by the constant sub-horizon source deep inside the eMD era, and an overall transfer factor controlled by the way the eMD phase terminates at
\eta_{\rm eva}
. Here
[\Phi]
and
[S]
label the adiabatic and isocurvature seeds (cf. Eqs. (
64
) and (
65
)).

Notation.

Inside this section, the suffix
(\rm eMD,\,PBH)
distinguishes PBH-driven reheating from the instantaneous and gradual reference cases, labelled
(\rm eMD,\,inst.)
and
(\rm eMD,\,grad.)
. Elsewhere in the paper only PBH reheating is considered, and we drop the qualifier, writing

\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(eMD)}\equiv\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(eMD,\,PBH)}.

(171)

We proceed in three steps. We first isolate the linear-in-
a
growth in the idealised limit of a pure matter era and clarify its physical origin. We then include the finite eMD-to-
\rm RD_{2}
transition and specialise to PBH-driven reheating, for which the Choptuik mass function produces a characteristic
4/3
cusp in the decay of
\Phi
. Finally, we compare the resulting spectrum with the instantaneous and gradual reference cases and fix the ultraviolet cutoff. We show the representative benchmark spectra in Fig.
3
.
The technical derivations are collected in App.
G
.

6.1
The linear growth
\Omega_{\rm GW}\propto a
during pure matter domination

Deep inside the eMD era (
x_{\rm eq}\ll x\ll x_{\rm eva}
, with
x\equiv k\eta

and
x_{\rm eva}\equiv k\eta_{\rm eva}
marking reheating), the potential is
frozen,
\Phi^{\prime}\simeq 0
, and the source
f_{X}
becomes time-independent. The
GW kernel (Eq. (
100
)) can then be evaluated in closed
form (see App.
G
) and reads

I_{X}^{\rm(pure~MD)}(x,u,v,x_{\rm eva}\gg x)=\frac{5\alpha_{X}}{3}\,\frac{x^{3}+3x\cos x-3\sin x}{x^{3}},

(172)

where
X\in\{\Phi,S\}
labels the adiabatic and isocurvature channels and

\alpha_{X}\equiv T_{X}^{\rm(eMD)}(0)^{2}
are the squared large-scale limits of
the scalar transfer functions
T_{X}^{\rm(eMD)}
defined in
Eq. (
57
), with
\alpha_{\Phi}=(9/10)^{2}
and

\alpha_{S}=(1/5)^{2}
. Crucially,
I_{X}^{\rm(pure~MD)}
is independent of the
internal momenta
u,v
, so the scalar convolution factorizes cleanly from
the time-dependent kernel.
Splitting the GW spectrum into adiabatic and isocurvature pieces,

\Omega^{\rm(pure~MD)}_{\rm GW}=\Omega_{\rm GW}^{({\rm pure~MD})}[\Phi]+\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(pure~MD)}[S],

(173)

the adiabatic contribution, sourced by the CMB-normalized curvature power
spectrum with amplitude
\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{R}}
(cf. Eq. (
64
)) and spectral index
n_{s}
, takes the compact form

\Omega_{\rm GW}^{({\rm pure~MD})}[\Phi]=\frac{3}{25}\,\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{R}}^{2}\left(\frac{k}{\mathcal{H}}\right)^{\!2}\mathcal{J}_{\Phi}(n_{s}),

(174)

with
\tilde{k}\equiv k/k_{\rm{UV}}
and

\mathcal{J}_{\Phi}(n_{s}=1)=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{32}{15}\,\tilde{k}^{-1}-3+\frac{32}{35}\,\tilde{k}+\frac{1}{8}\,\tilde{k}^{2},&0<\tilde{k}\leq 1,\\[8.00003pt]
\displaystyle\frac{1}{840}\!\left(1-\frac{2}{\tilde{k}}\right)^{\!4}\!\left(105\tilde{k}^{2}+72\tilde{k}+16-\frac{32}{\tilde{k}}-\frac{16}{\tilde{k}^{2}}\right),&1<\tilde{k}\leq 2,\\[8.00003pt]
0,&\tilde{k}>2.\end{cases}

(175)

Here, we assumed the power spectrum to be exactly scale invariant for simplicity and refer to App.
G
for the
n_{s}\neq 1
case. The shape function
\mathcal{J}_{\Phi}
vanishes for
k>2k_{\rm{UV}}

by momentum conservation in the scalar convolution. In the deep infrared

k\ll k_{\rm{UV}}
, the leading term
\mathcal{J}_{\Phi}(1)\to(32/15)\,k_{\rm{UV}}/k
gives

\Omega_{\rm GW}^{({\rm pure~MD})}[\Phi]\simeq\frac{32}{125}\,\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{R}}^{2}\left(\frac{k}{\mathcal{H}}\right)^{\!2}\frac{k_{\rm{UV}}}{k}~\propto~a,

(176)

reflecting the linear-in-
a
growth of modes fed by a non-decaying source.

The isocurvature channel, driven by the Poisson PBH number-density
fluctuations of Eq. (
65
), gives for
0<\tilde{k}\leq 2

\Omega_{\rm GW}^{({\rm pure~MD})}[S]=\frac{1}{10206000\pi^{2}}\left(\frac{k}{\mathcal{H}}\right)^{\!2}\!\left(\frac{k_{\rm{UV}}}{k_{\rm{PBH}}}\right)^{\!6}\frac{(2-\tilde{k})^{4}}{\tilde{k}}\left(128+116\tilde{k}+40\tilde{k}^{2}+5\tilde{k}^{3}\right),

(177)

and
\Omega_{\rm GW}^{({\rm pure~MD})}[S]=0
for
\tilde{k}>2
. In the same deep-infrared limit,

\Omega_{\rm GW}^{({\rm pure~MD})}[S]\simeq\frac{128}{637875\pi^{2}}\left(\frac{k}{\mathcal{H}}\right)^{\!2}\!\left(\frac{k_{\rm{UV}}}{k_{\rm{PBH}}}\right)^{\!6}\frac{k_{\rm{UV}}}{k}~\propto~a,

(178)

which displays the same
\Omega_{\rm GW}\propto a
linear growth as the adiabatic channel of
Eq. (
176
). Details of the derivation are given in
App.
G
.

We now give more details on the origin of the IR growth
\Omega_{\rm GW}\propto a
in Eqs. (
176
) and (
178
). During an eMD phase the scalar potential is frozen on all relevant scales,

\Phi^{\prime}\simeq 0
, so that the source in Eq. (
93
) becomes
effectively time-independent,

S_{\lambda}(\mathbf{k},\eta)\simeq\mathcal{S}_{\lambda}(\mathbf{k})=\text{const.}

(179)

With this constant source, the tensor equation of motion

h_{\lambda}^{\prime\prime}(\mathbf{k},\eta)+\frac{4}{(1+3\omega)\eta}h_{\lambda}^{\prime}(\mathbf{k},\eta)+k^{2}h_{\lambda}(\mathbf{k},\eta)=\mathcal{S}_{\lambda}(\mathbf{k})

(180)

admits, for
\omega=0
,
the closed-form solution
[
25
]

h_{\lambda}(\mathbf{k},\eta)=\frac{\mathcal{S}_{\lambda}(\mathbf{k})}{k^{2}}\left[1+3\,\frac{k\eta\cos(k\eta)-\sin(k\eta)}{(k\eta)^{3}}\right],

(181)

with super-horizon and sub-horizon limits

h_{\lambda}(\mathbf{k},\eta)\simeq\frac{\mathcal{S}_{\lambda}(\mathbf{k})}{k^{2}}\begin{cases}(k\eta)^{2}/10,&k\eta\ll 1,\\[3.00003pt]
1,&k\eta\gg 1.\end{cases}

(182)

This shows that, once inside the horizon, the induced tensor mode settles to a constant particular solution rather than oscillating with a decaying envelope. From Eqs. (
112
) and (
101
), we have

\Omega_{\rm GW}(k)=\frac{k^{5}}{24\pi^{2}\mathcal{H}^{2}}\sum_{\lambda}\left\langle\left|h_{\lambda}(\mathbf{k},\eta)\right|^{2}\right\rangle_{\rm r}.

(183)

Using the eMD scaling
k^{2}/\mathcal{H}^{2}=(k\eta/2)^{2}\propto a
, one obtains

\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(eMD,\,PBH)}(k,\eta)\;\propto\;\mathcal{P}_{\Phi}^{2}(k)\begin{cases}k^{5}\,\eta^{6},&k\eta\ll 1,\\[3.00003pt]
k\,\eta^{2}\;\propto\;k\,a(\eta),&k\eta\gg 1,\end{cases}

(184)

namely a linear growth with the scale factor at sub-horizon scales, in agreement with Eqs. (
174
)–(
177
).
The situation during radiation domination is qualitatively different. From
Eq. (
96
) with
\omega=1/3
,

h_{\lambda}(\mathbf{k},\eta)=\frac{1}{k\eta}\int_{0}^{\eta}\!d\bar{\eta}\;\bar{\eta}\,\sin\!\big[k(\eta-\bar{\eta})\big]\,S_{\lambda}(\mathbf{k},\bar{\eta}),

(185)

with a source that now depends explicitly on time through the
oscillating RD transfer function

T_{\Phi}(x)=\frac{9}{x^{2}}\left[\frac{\sin(x/\sqrt{3})}{x/\sqrt{3}}-\cos\!\left(\tfrac{x}{\sqrt{3}}\right)\right].

(186)

Here,
x=k\eta
and substituting into Eq. (
93
) yields

\mathcal{S}_{\lambda}(\mathbf{k},\eta)\simeq\begin{cases}\displaystyle 6\!\int\!\frac{d^{3}q}{(2\pi)^{3}}\,e_{\lambda}^{ij}(k)\,q_{i}q_{j}\,\Phi_{\mathbf{q}}\Phi_{\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{q}},&x\ll 1,\\[9.95863pt]
\displaystyle\frac{54}{x^{2}}\!\int\!\frac{d^{3}q}{(2\pi)^{3}}\,e_{\lambda}^{ij}(k)\,q_{i}q_{j}\,\Phi_{\mathbf{q}}\Phi_{\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{q}}\,\frac{\cos\!\big(\tfrac{(v-u)x}{\sqrt{3}}\big)-\cos\!\big(\tfrac{(v+u)x}{\sqrt{3}}\big)}{u^{2}v^{2}},&x\gg 1,\end{cases}

(187)

so that, in sharp contrast to eMD, the source decays as

S_{\lambda}\propto x^{-2}
after horizon entry. Inserting this into
Eq. (
185
) gives

h_{\lambda}(\mathbf{k},\eta)\simeq\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{\eta^{2}}{6}\,\mathcal{S}_{\lambda}^{(0)}(\mathbf{k}),&x\ll 1,\\[8.5359pt]
\displaystyle\frac{1}{k\eta}\Big[\mathcal{A}_{\lambda}(\mathbf{k})\sin(k\eta)+\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}(\mathbf{k})\cos(k\eta)\Big],&x\gg 1,\end{cases}

(188)

where
\mathcal{A}_{\lambda},\mathcal{B}_{\lambda}\propto\mathcal{P}_{\Phi}^{1/2}(k)

are fixed around horizon crossing. Sub-horizon RD tensor modes therefore
propagate as free gravitational waves whose amplitude redshifts as

1/(k\eta)\propto 1/a
.
Since
k^{2}/\mathcal{H}^{2}=(k\eta)^{2}\propto a^{2}
during RD, the redshifting of the tensor amplitude exactly compensates the background scaling, leading to

\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(RD)}(k,\eta)\propto k^{3}\,\mathcal{P}_{\Phi}^{2}(k)\times\mathrm{const.},

(189)

that is, the usual frozen sub-horizon SIGW spectrum with its causal infrared scaling
\Omega_{\rm GW}\propto k^{3}
.
The physical difference can thus be summarized as

\frac{\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(eMD,\,PBH)}(k,\eta)}{\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(RD)}(k,\eta)}\propto\frac{a(\eta)}{k^{2}}.

(190)

In RD, GW production is localized around horizon re-entry and is followed by free propagation. In eMD, instead, the source remains active well inside the horizon and continuously feeds the tensor mode, yielding a constant induced strain and a secular enhancement of
\Omega_{\rm GW}
. This sustained sub-horizon sourcing is the origin of the amplification factor
\propto a(\eta)
appearing in Eq. (
178
).

6.2
PBH-driven reheating

The eMD era ends at
\eta_{\rm eva}
, after which the Universe returns to a
radiation-dominated phase, denoted by
\mathrm{RD}_{2}
. Following the decomposition
introduced in Eq. (
119
), we write the Newtonian potential as

\Phi(k,\eta)=\sum_{X\in\{\Phi,\,S\}}T_{X}(k,\eta)\,X(k,0)\,\Theta\!\left(k_{\rm{UV}}^{{(X)}}(\eta)-k\right),

(191)

where the transfer function is defined piecewise as

T_{X}(k,\eta)=\begin{cases}T_{X}^{\rm(eMD)}(k)\,F_{\Phi}(\eta),&\eta\leq\eta_{\rm osc}(k),\\[4.0pt]
T_{X}^{\rm(eMD)}(k)\,\mathcal{S}_{\Phi}(k)\,T_{\Phi}^{\rm(RD_{2})}(k,\eta),&\eta>\eta_{\rm osc}(k).\end{cases}

(192)

Here
T_{X}^{\rm(eMD)}(k)
is the eMD transfer function given in
Eq. (
57
). In the analytical estimates below, we
will use its form in the limit relevant for modes entering the horizon after
the onset of eMD, namely
k\ll k_{\rm eq}
. The function
F_{\Phi}(\eta)

describes the model-dependent evolution of
\Phi
during reheating and will be
specified below for PBH reheating in Eq. (
197
). The factor

\mathcal{S}_{\Phi}(k)
is the suppression factor defined in
Eq. (
83
), while
T_{\Phi}^{\rm(RD_{2})}(k,\eta)
is
the standard radiation-era transfer function given in
Eq. (
186
). Finally, the dimensionless time

x_{\rm osc}\equiv k\eta_{\rm osc}
marks the onset of oscillations of
\Phi
on
the scale
k
, as defined in
Eq. (
82
), and satisfies

x_{\rm osc}\gtrsim x_{\rm eva}
.
Inserting Eq. (
192
) into Eq. (
95
)
with
\omega=0
, the eMD source takes the form

f_{X}^{\rm(eMD,\,PBH)}(\bar{x},u,v)=\alpha_{X}\left[F_{\Phi}^{2}+\frac{2}{3}\!\left(F_{\Phi}+\frac{F_{\Phi}^{\prime}}{\mathcal{H}}\right)^{\!2}\right],\qquad\alpha_{X}\equiv\begin{cases}\left(\tfrac{9}{10}\right)^{2},&X=\Phi,\\[4.0pt]
\left(\tfrac{1}{5}\right)^{2},&X=S,\end{cases}

(193)

which depends on time only through
F_{\Phi}
and remains independent of

u,v
. For
x>x_{\rm osc}
, the kernel splits into pre- and
post-oscillation contributions,

I_{X}^{\rm(eMD\to RD_{2})}(x,u,v)=I^{\rm(eMD,\,PBH)}_{X}(x,u,v)+I^{\rm(RD_{2})}_{X}(x,u,v),

(194)

with

\displaystyle I^{\rm(eMD,\,PBH)}_{X}(x,u,v)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{x_{\rm osc}}\!\mathrm{d}\bar{x}\;G^{\rm(eMD\to RD_{2})}_{h}(x,\bar{x})\,f_{X}^{\rm(eMD,\,PBH)}(\bar{x},u,v),

(195)

\displaystyle I^{\rm(RD_{2})}_{X}(x,u,v)

\displaystyle=\int_{x_{\rm osc}}^{x}\!\mathrm{d}\bar{x}\;G^{\rm(RD_{2})}_{h}(x,\bar{x})\,f_{X}^{\rm(RD_{2})}(\bar{x},u,v),

(196)

where
G^{\rm(eMD\to RD_{2})}_{h}
(Eq. (
455
)) is the mixed
tensor Green’s function for GWs sourced during eMD and propagating into

\mathrm{RD}_{2}
, and
G^{\rm(RD_{2})}_{h}
(Eq. (
445
)) is the
standard radiation-era Green’s function. The second piece

I^{\rm(RD_{2})}_{X}
coincides with the Poltergeist kernel of
Sec.
5
. We now focus on
I^{\rm(eMD,\,PBH)}_{X}
, evaluated
in App.
G
.

The physical case of interest is reheating driven by the
evaporation of a PBH population with a Choptuik mass function. The finite
width of the mass distribution smears the evaporation time and causes

\Phi
to decay in a characteristic, non-exponential way. We find that the
envelope
F_{\Phi}
entering Eqs. (
192
)
and (
193
) is well described by the fit (Eq. (
84
))

F_{\Phi}(\eta)\simeq\begin{cases}\exp\!\left[-0.84\left(\dfrac{\eta}{\eta_{\rm eva}}\right)^{\!3.43}\right],&\eta<\eta_{\rm eva},\\[8.0pt]
3.5\left(\dfrac{\eta_{\rm cut}-\eta}{\eta_{\rm cut}}\right)^{\!4/3},&\eta_{\rm eva}\leq\eta\leq\eta_{\rm cut},\end{cases}

(197)

where
\eta_{\rm eva}
is the mean evaporation time and

z_{\rm cut}\equiv\eta_{\rm cut}/\eta_{\rm eva}\simeq 1.26
is set by the
heaviest PBHs surviving in the tail of the mass distribution. The key
qualitative feature of Eq. (
197
) is that
F_{\Phi}
vanishes
at
\eta_{\rm cut}
as a
4/3
cusp, which sits between the hard step
of instantaneous reheating and the exponential softening characteristic of
perturbative particle decay (see Sec.
6.3
and Fig.
14
).

Inserting Eq. (
197
) into Eq. (
193
) and
performing the time integral in Eq. (
195
)
(App.
G
), the eMD kernel admits compact asymptotic forms. In
the infrared,
x_{\rm eva}\ll 1
,

I^{\rm(eMD,\,PBH)}_{X}(x,u,v)\simeq 0.222\,\alpha_{X}\,\frac{\sin x}{x}\,x_{\rm eva}^{2},\qquad x_{\rm eva}\ll 1,

(198)

while in the ultraviolet,
x\gg x_{\rm eva}\gg 1
,

I^{\rm(eMD,\,PBH)}_{X}(x,u,v)\simeq 2.23\,\alpha_{X}\,\frac{x_{\rm eva}^{1/3}}{x}\,\sin\!\left(x-z_{\rm cut}\,x_{\rm eva}+\frac{5\pi}{6}\right).

(199)

The fractional UV scaling
I_{X}\propto x_{\rm eva}^{1/3}
is a distinctive
signature of PBH reheating, milder than the
x_{\rm eva}^{1}
scaling of
instantaneous reheating and stronger than the
x_{\rm eva}^{-1}
suppression
of perturbative (gradual) reheating. Physically, it reflects the
4/3
cusp
at
z=z_{\rm cut}
in Eq. (
197
), which softens, but does
not erase, the endpoint of the time integral. The full intermediate
expression and the comparison with the instantaneous and gradual cases are
given in Sec.
6.3
and App.
G
.

Because
I^{\rm(eMD,\,PBH)}_{X}
in both asymptotic regimes
(
198
)–(
199
) remains
independent of the internal momenta
u,v
, as did
I_{X}^{\rm(pure~MD)}
in
Eq. (
172
), the scalar convolution factorizes out of the
full GW spectrum. The SIGW spectrum sourced during a PBH-dominated eMD era
and observed after reheating then inherits the same spectral shape as

\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(pure~MD)}
, up to an overall rescaling,

\Omega^{\rm(eMD,\,PBH)}_{\rm GW}[X]=R^{\rm(eMD,\,PBH)}\times\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(pure~MD)}[X],

(200)

which is the quantity denoted
\Omega^{\rm(eMD)}_{\rm GW}[X]
in the rest of the paper, in line with the convention introduced in Eq. (
171
).

\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(pure~MD)}[X]
is given by
Eqs. (
173
), (
174
),
(
175
) and (
177
), and the transfer
factor defined as the ratio of oscillation-averaged squared kernels,

R^{\rm(eMD,\,PBH)}\,\equiv\,\overline{\bigl(I^{\rm(eMD,\,PBH)}_{X}\bigr)^{2}}\Big/\overline{\bigl(I_{X}^{\rm(pure~MD)}\bigr)^{2}},

(201)

where
I_{X}^{\rm(pure~MD)}
is given by Eq. (
172
), and
I^{\rm(eMD,\,PBH)}_{X}
is given by
Eqs. (
198
)–(
199
) for the analytical calculation, and by Eq. (
195
) for the numerical calculation. Eq. (
200
) makes the physics transparent: the overall
shape is controlled by the pure-MD convolution encoded in
Eqs. (
174
)–(
177
), while
the entire dependence on the reheating mechanism is given by the factor
R^{\rm(eMD,\,PBH)}
. The analogous factorisation for instantaneous and gradual reheating, which we summarise next, is derived in detail in App.
G
.

6.3
Comparison with instantaneous and gradual reheating

To assess what is specific to PBH reheating, we compare it with two reference scenarios in which the transition from matter to radiation domination proceeds at extremely different rates, namely instantaneous reheating, in which the source is switched off abruptly
[
213
]
, and gradual reheating driven by perturbative particle decay at a constant rate
\Gamma\sim H
, for which the source is suppressed smoothly
[
19
,
44
,
242
,
131
,
214
,
296
,
297
]
. The three reheating histories are characterised by the envelope of the Newtonian potential,

F_{\Phi}(\eta)=\begin{cases}\Theta(\eta_{\rm eva}-\eta),&\text{instantaneous reheating},\\[6.0pt]
\left(\dfrac{\eta_{\rm cut}-\eta}{\eta_{\rm cut}}\right)^{\!4/3}\;\;\text{near $\eta_{\rm cut}$},&\text{PBH reheating},\\[6.0pt]
\exp\!\left[-\dfrac{2}{3}\left(\dfrac{\eta}{\eta_{\rm eva}}\right)^{\!3}\right],&\text{gradual reheating},\end{cases}

(202)

which differ in the way the source is switched off. The instantaneous case is a hard step at
\eta=\eta_{\rm eva}
, the gradual case is a smooth exponential, and PBH reheating is intermediate between the two limits: the Choptuik mass function produces a continuous but non-analytic
4/3
cusp at
\eta=\eta_{\rm cut}
(cf. Eq. (
197
)).

This distinction matters because the ultraviolet behaviour of the GW kernel is controlled entirely by how the source switches off, that is, by the behaviour of
F_{\Phi}
near the endpoint of the time integral in Eq. (
195
). The three reheating histories therefore generate three qualitatively different ultraviolet kernels. In the regime
x\gg x_{\rm eva}\gg 1
, one finds

\displaystyle I^{\rm(eMD,\,inst.)}_{X}(x_{\rm eva},u,v)

\displaystyle\simeq\frac{5\alpha_{X}}{6}\,\cos(x-x_{\rm eva})

\displaystyle\propto\,x_{\rm eva}^{0},

(203)

\displaystyle I^{\rm(eMD,\,PBH)}_{X}(x_{\rm eva},u,v)

\displaystyle\simeq 2.23\,\alpha_{X}\,x_{\rm eva}^{-2/3}\sin\!\left(x-z_{\rm cut}x_{\rm eva}+\tfrac{5\pi}{6}\right)

\displaystyle\propto\,x_{\rm eva}^{-2/3},

(204)

\displaystyle I^{\rm(eMD,\,grad.)}_{X}(x_{\rm eva},u,v)

\displaystyle\simeq\frac{5\alpha_{X}\cos x}{2\,x_{\rm eva}^{2}}

\displaystyle\propto\,x_{\rm eva}^{-2}.

(205)

A sharper endpoint of
F_{\Phi}
yields a stronger ultraviolet kernel, with PBH reheating closer to the instantaneous case than to the gradual one, consistent with the rapid termination of the eMD phase by Hawking evaporation. The infrared regime, by contrast, is insensitive to the endpoint and gives a common scaling
I_{X}\propto x_{\rm eva}\sin x
in all three cases.

Since none of the kernels in Eqs. (
203
)–(
205
) depends on the internal momenta
u,v
, the scalar convolution factorises as in Eq. (
200
), and the three GW spectra inherit the same spectral shape as
\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(pure~MD)}[X]
, differing only by an overall transfer factor,

\Omega^{\rm(eMD,\,r)}_{\rm GW}[X]\,=\,R^{\rm(eMD,\,r)}\times\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(pure~MD)}[X],\qquad R^{\rm(eMD,\,r)}\,\equiv\,\overline{\bigl(I^{\rm(eMD,\,r)}_{X}\bigr)^{2}}/\overline{\bigl(I_{X}^{\rm(pure~MD)}\bigr)^{2}},

(206)

with
r\in\{\text{inst.},\,\text{PBH},\,\text{grad.}\}
. All the dependence on the reheating mechanism is repackaged into the scalar factor
R^{\rm(eMD,\,r)}
, whose parametric scaling follows immediately from Eqs. (
203
)–(
205
),

R^{\rm(eMD,\,inst.)}\,\propto\,x_{\rm eva}^{0}\;\gtrsim\;R^{\rm(eMD,\,PBH)}\,\propto\,x_{\rm eva}^{-4/3}\;\gtrsim\;R^{\rm(eMD,\,grad.)}\,\propto\,x_{\rm eva}^{-4}.

(207)

Eq. (
207
) is the central result of this comparison: the GW amplitudes follow the same ordering as the kernels, with the PBH-reheating signal lying between the instantaneous and gradual predictions and carrying a parametric dependence on
x_{\rm eva}
distinct from either. An explicit numerical comparison is shown in Fig.
14
, and full derivations of the kernels and transfer factors, including the intermediate regime
x_{\rm eva}\sim 1
, are collected in App.
G
.

6.4
The UV cutoff

For the GW signal
\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(eMD)}
generated during the eMD phase, we take the ultraviolet cutoff
k_{\rm{UV}}
to be set by the nonlinear scale
k_{\rm{NL}}^{\rm adia/iso}
, defined in Eqs. (
129
) and (
130
), beyond which linear perturbation theory ceases to be valid. This choice is motivated by numerical simulations of structure growth during an eMD era, which show that GW production is suppressed once nonlinear halo formation sets in
[
134
]
.
17
17

17

The reheating setup studied in Ref.
[
134
]
corresponds to the gradual-reheating scenario discussed in App.
G
. We assume that the same cutoff prescription can also be applied to the PBH-reheating scenarios considered here.

In addition, modes with
k>k_{\rm eq}
are already suppressed before the onset of eMD due to the behaviour of the transfer function
T_{X}^{\rm(eMD)}(k)
in Eq. (
57
). However, in our analytical estimate, in Eq. (
192
), we have used the transfer functions
T_{X}^{\rm(eMD)}(k)
in the regime relevant for modes entering the horizon after the beginning of eMD, namely
k\ll k_{\rm eq}
. To account conservatively for the suppression of earlier-entering modes, we therefore impose an additional cutoff at
k_{\rm eq}
by hand
[
44
,
242
]
.
We thus adopt the prescription summarized in Tab.
4
,

k_{\rm{UV}}^{\rm adia/iso}=\min\!\left[k_{\rm{NL}}^{\rm adia/iso},\,k_{\rm eq}\right],

(208)

where
k_{\rm{NL}}^{\rm adia/iso}
are given in Eqs. (
129
) and (
130
).

Figure 14:
SIGW signal generated during the PBH-induced eMD phase,
\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(eMD,\,PBH)}
, compared with the instantaneous and gradual reheating reference cases of Sec.
6.3
. In the eMD case the cutoff is unambiguous, since
k_{\rm eq}<k_{\rm{NL,UV}}
acts as the effective UV cutoff, see Eq. (
208
).

7
SIGWs during the early radiation era

In this section we compute the SIGW signals sourced during a radiation-dominated era. Two channels are active during the first radiation era
\rm RD_{1}
, between PBH formation at
\eta_{f}
and matter–radiation equality at
\eta_{\rm eq}
, corresponding in the taxonomy of Sec.
4.3
to the adiabatic (irreducible) and isocurvature (universal) seeds,

\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(RD_{1})}~=~\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(RD_{1})}[\Phi]~+~\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(RD_{1})}[S]~\equiv~\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(RD_{1},irred.)}~+~\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(RD_{1},univ.)}.

(209)

In addition, the post-evaporation radiation era
\rm RD_{2}
carries its own irreducible adiabatic contribution, already announced in Sec.
5
,

\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(RD_{2},irred.)}~\equiv~\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(RD_{2})}[\Phi].

(210)

The adiabatic components
\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(RD_{1})}[\Phi]
and
\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(RD_{2})}[\Phi]
are sourced by the standard primordial curvature spectrum and are present in any cosmology with a radiation-dominated phase
[
19
,
44
,
242
]
. They share the same RD-era formalism, differing only in the integration window of the GW kernel and in the post-emission redshift history, and are therefore derived jointly below. The isocurvature component
\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(RD_{1})}[S]
is sourced instead by the Poisson statistics of the discrete PBH distribution,
\left<S(\mathbf{x})S(\mathbf{y})\right>\simeq\left<\delta_{\rm{PBH}}(\mathbf{x})\delta_{\rm{PBH}}(\mathbf{y})\right>
, which seeds the Newtonian potential at second order in perturbation theory and generates the universal contribution characteristic of any localized-object population
[
107
,
259
,
258
,
116
,
113
]
. Crucially, none of these three contributions benefits from the Poltergeist enhancement of Sec.
5
, since GW production takes place entirely in a radiation background. The two
\rm RD_{1}
contributions are further diluted by the entropy release at PBH evaporation, while the
\rm RD_{2}
contribution is not. As we show below, the universal channel inherits an additional radiation-era
\kappa^{-2}
suppression of the isocurvature-to-curvature transfer, fixing the geometric hierarchy
\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(RD_{1})}[S]\propto(k_{\rm eq}/k_{\rm{PBH}})^{4}
, while the two adiabatic channels are set by the small primordial scalar variance
A_{\zeta}^{2}\sim 10^{-18}
. The combined radiation-era signal therefore lies well below the projected sensitivity of all planned GW observatories. We treat the universal channel first, then the joint adiabatic derivation for both eras. We show the representative benchmark spectra in Fig.
15
.

7.1
The universal PBH-isocurvature signal

For modes deep inside the horizon,
\kappa\equiv k/k_{\rm eq}\gg 1
, the coupled scalar evolution Eqs. (
51
)–(
55
) specialised to radiation domination (
\omega=c_{s}^{2}=1/3
,
\mathcal{H}=1/\eta
) with purely isocurvature initial conditions,

\Phi(k,0)=0,\qquad S(k,0)\neq 0,

(211)

admits the solution (see App.
C.3
and Ref.
[
107
,
259
]

\displaystyle T_{S}^{\rm(RD_{1})}(k,\eta)\equiv\frac{\Phi(k,\eta)}{S(k,0)}

\displaystyle=\frac{3}{2\sqrt{2}\,\kappa}\,\mathcal{F}(k\eta)+\mathcal{O}(\kappa^{-2}),\qquad\textrm{for}\quad\eta<\eta_{\rm eq},

(212)

with the shape function

\mathcal{F}(x)\equiv\frac{1}{x^{3}}\!\left[6+x^{2}-2\sqrt{3}\,x\sin(c_s x)-6\cos(c_s x)\right].

(213)

The prefactor
\kappa^{-1}
in Eq. (
212
) encodes the inefficient subhorizon transfer
S(k,0)\to\Phi(k,\eta)
during radiation domination. We emphasize that
T_{S}^{\rm(RD_{1})}(k,\eta)
is a time-dependent sourcing function active throughout
\rm RD_{1}
, and is conceptually distinct from the late-time matter-era freeze-out value
T_{S}^{\rm(eMD)}(\kappa)\simeq\kappa^{-2}
entering Eq. (
57
). The two coincide only in their asymptotic
\kappa^{-2}
scaling at
\eta\gtrsim\eta_{\rm eq}
. Since each factor of
T_{S}^{\rm(RD_{1})}
in the source contributes one power of
\kappa^{-1}
, one anticipates parametrically

\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(RD_{1})}[S]~\propto~\big(T_{S}^{\rm(RD_{1})}\big)^{4}~\propto~\kappa^{-4},

(214)

which is the origin of the smallness of the signal at its peak
k\sim k_{\rm{PBH}}\gg k_{\rm eq}
.

We now substitute Eq. (
212
) into the source function (
95
) with
\omega=1/3
. Since
\mathcal{H}=1/\bar{\eta}
in RD, one has
T_{S}^{{\rm(RD_{1})}\prime}/\mathcal{H}=z\,{\rm d}T_{S}^{\rm(RD_{1})}/{\rm d}z
with
z\equiv u\bar{x}
or
v\bar{x}
. It is convenient to introduce the auxiliary shape function

\mathcal{U}(z)\equiv\mathcal{F}(z)+z\,\mathcal{F}^{\prime}(z).

(215)

in terms of which the source takes the factorised form

f_{S}(\bar{x},k,u,v)=\frac{9}{8\,\kappa^{2}\,u\,v}\left[\mathcal{F}(u\bar{x})\,\mathcal{F}(v\bar{x})+\frac{1}{2}\,\mathcal{U}(u\bar{x})\,\mathcal{U}(v\bar{x})\right].

(216)

The overall factor
\kappa^{-2}
makes the parametric suppression manifest at the level of the source. Combining
f_{S}
with the radiation-era tensor Green function obtained from Eq. (
98
) at
\omega=1/3
,

G_{h}^{\rm(RD_{1})}(x,\bar{x})=\frac{\bar{x}}{x}\sin(x-\bar x)\,\Theta(x-\bar{x}),

(217)

the isocurvature kernel of Eq. (
100
) reads

I_{S}^{\rm(RD_{1})}(x_{c},u,v)\equiv\int_{x_{f}}^{x_{\rm eq}}\!{\rm d}\bar{x}\,G_{h}^{\rm(RD_{1})}(x_{c},\bar{x})\,f_{S}(\bar{x},k,u,v),

(218)

where
x_{f}\equiv k\eta_{f}
marks the time at which the Poisson source is imprinted, and
x_{\rm eq}\equiv k\eta_{\rm eq}
the time at which the radiation phase ends. For the modes that source the bulk of the signal,
k\sim k_{\rm{PBH}}
, one finds
x_{\rm eq}\simeq k_{\rm{PBH}}/k_{\rm eq}\simeq\beta_{f}^{-2/3}\gamma_{\rm H}^{-1/2}\gg 1
, so the kernel accumulates many oscillations within
[x_{f},x_{\rm eq}]
and the extension

x_{f}\to 0,\qquad x_{\rm eq}\to\infty,

(219)

is an excellent approximation, providing moreover a conservative upper envelope of the signal
[
107
,
259
]
.
18
18

18

The hierarchy
x_{\rm eq}\gg 1
degrades for
\beta_{f}\gtrsim 0.03
, but the resulting finite-time corrections only decrease the signal, leaving our analytic estimate as an upper bound.

In the limit (
219
), introducing
w_{\pm}\equiv(u\pm v)/\sqrt{3}
,
w_{u}\equiv u/\sqrt{3}
,
w_{v}\equiv v/\sqrt{3}
together with
\mathcal{L}(w)\equiv\ln|1-w^{2}|+i\pi\,\Theta(|w|-1)
, the oscillation-averaged kernel reads
[
107
]

\overline{I_{S}^{\rm(RD_{1})\,2}}(u,v)=\frac{1}{2}\!\left|\frac{9}{32\,u^{4}v^{4}\,\kappa^{2}}\,\mathcal{J}(u,v)\right|^{2},

(220)

with

\mathcal{J}(u,v)=-3u^{2}v^{2}+(u^{2}-3)(u^{2}+2v^{2}-3)\,\mathcal{L}(w_{u})+(v^{2}-3)(v^{2}+2u^{2}-3)\,\mathcal{L}(w_{v})\\
-\frac{1}{2}(u^{2}+v^{2}-3)^{2}\bigl[\mathcal{L}(w_{+})+\mathcal{L}(w_{-})\bigr].

(221)

The logarithmic and step-function singularities at
w_{u},w_{v},w_{\pm}=1
reflect the acoustic resonances of the radiation fluid:
u,v=\sqrt{3}
corresponds to individual scalar modes resonating with the tensor mode, while
u\pm v=\sqrt{3}
corresponds to resonances involving the sum or difference of the two scalar acoustic frequencies.

Inserting Eq. (
220
) into the master formula (
106
) yields the induced spectrum at equality,

\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(RD_{1})}[S](k,\eta_{\rm eq})=\frac{1}{12}\!\left(\frac{k}{\mathcal{H}_{\rm eq}}\right)^{\!2}\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\!{\rm d}v\!\int_{|1-v|}^{1+v}\!{\rm d}u\,\mathcal{K}(u,v)\,\overline{I_{S}^{\rm(RD_{1})\,2}}\,\mathcal{P}_{S}(uk)\,\mathcal{P}_{S}(vk),

(222)

together with the causal infrared cutoff on the source spectrum,

\mathcal{P}_{S}(k)\;\longrightarrow\;\mathcal{P}_{S}(k)\,\Theta(k-k_{\rm eq}),

(223)

which removes modes that remain super-horizon throughout
\rm RD_{1}
and therefore cannot source GWs during that epoch. The spectrum dependence on the IR-cutoff is shown in Fig.
15
-top. Combining
k/\mathcal{H}_{\rm eq}\sim\kappa
,
\overline{I_{S}^{\rm(RD_{1})\,2}}\propto\kappa^{-4}
, and the white-noise scaling
\mathcal{P}_{S}\propto k^{3}
from Eq. (
65
), one finds the intermediate-regime amplitude

\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(RD_{1})}[S](k,\eta_{\rm eq})\;\simeq\;\mathcal{A}_{S}^{2}\,\frac{k^{2}\,k_{\rm eq}^{4}}{k_{\rm{PBH}}^{6}}\,\mathcal{G}(k),\qquad k_{\rm eq}\lesssim k\lesssim k_{\rm{PBH}},

(224)

where
\mathcal{A}_{S}\equiv 2/(3\pi)
is the dimensionless Poisson amplitude defined by Eq. (
65
), and
\mathcal{G}(k)
collects the residual cutoff dependence of the double momentum integral. This dependence is mild, with
\mathcal{G}(k)\to\mathcal{G}_{\infty}\simeq 2.38
as
k_{\rm IR}\to 0
and
k_{\rm UV}\to\infty
. The overall prefactor
(k_{\rm eq}/k_{\rm{PBH}})^{4}
makes explicit the
\kappa^{-4}
suppression anticipated in Eq. (
214
).

The convolution in Eq. (
222
) can be evaluated in closed form using the analytic kernel of Eqs. (
220
)–(
221
). The result is well reproduced by the broken power-law fit

\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(RD_{1})}[S](k,\eta_{\rm eq})\simeq\mathcal{A}_{S}^{2}\,\mathcal{G}_{\infty}\,\left(\frac{k_{\rm eq}}{k_{\rm{PBH}}}\right)^{\!4}\!\left[10^{3/c}+\left(\frac{k}{k_{\rm{PBH}}}\right)^{\!-2/c}+\left(\frac{100\,k^{3}}{k_{\rm eq}\,k_{\rm{PBH}}^{2}}\right)^{\!-1/c}\right]^{-c}\Theta(k_{\rm{PBH}}-k),

(225)

with
c=2
and where the explicit
\Theta(k_{\rm{PBH}}-k)
enforces the ultraviolet cutoff at the inverse PBH separation, beyond which the fluid description of the PBH gas breaks down (see Sec.
4.4
). The three terms inside the bracket correspond, respectively, to a flat plateau at the largest momenta (first term), a Poisson intermediate regime inherited from
\mathcal{P}_{S}\propto k^{3}
(second term), and a causal infrared suppression enforced by Eq. (
223
) (third term). The corresponding asymptotic scalings read

\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(RD_{1})}[S](k,\eta_{\rm eq})\simeq\mathcal{A}_{S}^{2}\,\mathcal{G}_{\infty}\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{10^{2}\,k^{3}\,k_{\rm eq}^{3}}{k_{\rm{PBH}}^{6}},&k\lesssim 10^{-2}\,k_{\rm eq},\\[5.16663pt]
\displaystyle\frac{k^{2}\,k_{\rm eq}^{4}}{k_{\rm{PBH}}^{6}},&k_{\rm eq}\lesssim k\lesssim k_{\rm{PBH}},\\[5.16663pt]
\displaystyle 10^{-3}\left(\frac{k_{\rm eq}}{k_{\rm{PBH}}}\right)^{\!4},&k\lesssim k_{\rm{PBH}}\ \text{(plateau)},\end{cases}

(226)

all controlled by the same
(k_{\rm eq}/k_{\rm{PBH}})^{4}
hierarchy. The fit and the asymptotic limits are shown in the left panel of Fig.
15
for several choices of
k_{\rm IR}
.

Because
\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(RD_{1})}[S]
is produced before PBH domination, it is subsequently diluted by the entropy release at PBH evaporation in exactly the same way as any pre-existing radiation-like relic. Using the dilution factor
D
of Eq. (
264
), the present-day spectrum reads

\Omega_{\rm GW,0}^{\rm(RD_{1})}[S](k)=\Omega_{\rm r,0}\,D^{-4/3}\,\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(RD_{1})}[S](k,\eta_{\rm eq}),

(227)

with
\Omega_{\rm r,0}\simeq 4.18\times 10^{-5}
. The right panel of Fig.
15
shows the diluted spectrum for representative PBH parameters: at fixed
\beta_{f}
, heavier
M_{\rm{PBH}}
extends the eMD duration
N_{\rm MD}
, increasing
D
and thereby suppressing the present-day signal further.

7.2
The irreducible inflationary signal

We derive here the irreducible adiabatic SIGW signal sourced during a radiation-dominated era, applicable to both
\rm RD_{1}
and
\rm RD_{2}
. The source is the standard near-scale-invariant primordial curvature spectrum

\mathcal{P}_{\zeta}(k)=A_{\zeta}\left(\frac{k}{k_{\rm CMB}}\right)^{n_{s}-1},\qquad A_{\zeta}\simeq 2.1\times 10^{-9},\quad n_{s}\simeq 0.96,

(228)

which generates a SIGW background in any cosmology with a radiation-dominated phase
[
19
,
44
,
242
]
. The two emission epochs share the same RD-era integrand of the kernel (
100
) — the source function
f_{\Phi}
of Eq. (
95
) evaluated at
\omega=1/3
, convolved against the radiation-era tensor Green function (
217
) — and differ only in the conformal-time window
[\bar{\eta}_{1},\bar{\eta}_{2}]
over which that kernel is integrated:

[\bar{\eta}_{1},\bar{\eta}_{2}]=\begin{cases}[\eta_{f},\eta_{\rm eq}],&\rm RD_{1},\\[2.0pt]
[\eta_{\rm osc},\eta],&\rm RD_{2},\end{cases}

(229)

i.e. from PBH formation to matter–radiation equality for
\rm RD_{1}
, and from the onset of post-evaporation oscillations to the time of observation for
\rm RD_{2}
.

It is convenient to first compute the result obtained when the kernel is integrated over the unrestricted radiation-era window
\bar{\eta}\in[0,\infty]
— the radiation-era envelope — and then re-impose the finite windows (
229
) as effective momentum cutoffs. In the unrestricted limit, the oscillation-averaged kernel becomes time-independent up to an overall
(k\eta)^{-2}
scaling
[
242
]
,

\overline{I_{\rm RD}^{2}(u,v;x\to\infty)}=\frac{9}{16\,x^{2}}\!\left[\frac{3(u^{2}+v^{2}-3)}{4u^{3}v^{3}}\right]^{\!2}\!\mathcal{T}(u,v),

(230)

with

\mathcal{T}(u,v)\equiv\Bigg[-4uv+(u^{2}+v^{2}-3)\,\log\!\left|\frac{3-(u+v)^{2}}{3-(u-v)^{2}}\right|\Bigg]^{\!2}+\pi^{2}(u^{2}+v^{2}-3)^{2}\,\Theta(u+v-\sqrt{3}).

(231)

Substituting Eq. (
230
) together with the power-law spectrum (
228
) into the master formula (
106
) yields the radiation-era envelope

\displaystyle\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(RD)}[\Phi](k)~

\displaystyle\equiv~Q(n_{s})\,A_{\zeta}^{2}\,\left(\frac{k}{k_{\rm CMB}}\right)^{\!2(n_{s}-1)}

(232)

\displaystyle Q(n_{s})~

\displaystyle\equiv~\frac{1}{27}\!\int_{0}^{\infty}\!{\rm d}v\!\int_{|1-v|}^{1+v}\!{\rm d}u\,\mathcal{K}(u,v)\,\overline{I_{\rm RD}^{2}(u,v)}\,u^{n_{s}-1}v^{n_{s}-1},

(233)

with
Q(n_{s})\simeq 0.81
for
n_{s}\simeq 0.96

[
242
]
. We use the bare superscript “
(\rm RD)
” in Eq. (
232
) to denote the unrestricted-window envelope. It is identical for both radiation-dominated phases and serves as a master result from which the era-specific amplitudes are extracted.

The era-specific amplitudes follow by re-imposing the windows (
229
) on the kernel of Eq. (
100
). Since SIGW production during radiation domination is concentrated around horizon entry
\eta\sim k^{-1}
, restricting the kernel to
\bar{\eta}\in[\bar{\eta}_{1},\bar{\eta}_{2}]
selects modes whose horizon entry lies inside that interval,
\bar{\eta}_{2}^{-1}\lesssim k\lesssim\bar{\eta}_{1}^{-1}
. Applying this to Eq. (
229
) yields the causal momentum cutoffs

\displaystyle\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(RD_{1})}[\Phi](k,\eta_{\rm eq})

\displaystyle\simeq~\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(RD)}[\Phi](k)\,\Theta(k-k_{\rm eq}),

(234)

\displaystyle\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(RD_{2})}[\Phi](k,\eta_{\rm eva})

\displaystyle\simeq~\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(RD)}[\Phi](k)\,\Theta(k_{\rm eva}-k).

(235)

The Heaviside factors are sharp idealisations of the smooth transitions at horizon entry. Treating them as such provides a conservative estimate of the irreducible lower bound on the SIGW signal.
The two epochs differ further in their post-emission redshift history: the
\rm RD_{1}
contribution is diluted during the intervening eMD phase, while the
\rm RD_{2}
contribution is not. Using the dilution factor
D
of Eq. (
264
) and the redshift factor
\mathcal{D}(T_{\rm eva},T_{0})
of Eq. (
116
), the present-day spectra read

\displaystyle\Omega_{\rm GW,0}^{\rm(RD_{1})}[\Phi](k)

\displaystyle\simeq~D^{-4/3}\,\mathcal{D}(T_{\rm eva},T_{0})\,\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(RD_{1})}[\Phi](k,\eta_{\rm eq}),

(236)

\displaystyle\Omega_{\rm GW,0}^{\rm(RD_{2})}[\Phi](k)

\displaystyle\simeq~\mathcal{D}(T_{\rm eva},T_{0})\,\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(RD_{2})}[\Phi](k,\eta_{\rm eva}).

(237)

In both cases the amplitude is set entirely by the small primordial scalar variance
A_{\zeta}^{2}\sim 10^{-18}
, providing only an irreducible lower bound on the GW background that lies many orders of magnitude below current observational sensitivity.

Figure 15:
Top:
Cutoff dependence of SIGWs from PBH Isocurvature during
RD_{1}
. We show the induced spectra for different IR cutoffs (red curves) and compare with our analytic broken PL approximations (dashed gray lines). In reality
k_{\rm IR}
is chosen as
k_{\rm eq}
.
Bottom:
Resulting SIGW signal including dilution during early and late time MD adjusted to the PBH scenario.

8
SIGWs from PBH formation

We now turn to the SIGW component sourced at PBH formation,
\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(form)}[\Phi]
.
PBH formation requires a primordial curvature power spectrum

\mathcal{P}_{\mathcal{R}}(k)
that is strongly enhanced around the
formation scale
k_{f}
defined in Eq. (
280
). We model this
enhancement by the log-normal profile of Eq. (
24
),
characterized by an amplitude
\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{R}}
, a width

\Delta
, and a peak position
k_{f}
. In the narrow-width approximation
(cf. Eq. (
317
)), the initial PBH energy
fraction is exponentially sensitive to the smoothed density variance and
can be written as

\beta_{f}\simeq\frac{\sigma_{0}(M)}{\sqrt{2\pi}\,\delta_{c}}\exp\!\left[-\frac{\delta_{c}^{2}}{2\sigma_{0}^{2}(M)}\right],

(238)

where
\delta_{c}
is the collapse threshold and
\sigma_{0}(M)
denotes the
rms density contrast smoothed on the mass scale
M
, as defined in
Eq. (
299
). The threshold depends on the shape of the
collapsing perturbation, and typically lies in the range

\delta_{c}\simeq 0.40
–
0.67

[
284
,
126
]
. For
definiteness, we fix
\delta_{c}\simeq 0.5
in the numerical analysis. In the plots, however, we do not rely on the asymptotic expression in
Eq. (
238
). Instead, we use the full numerical
Press–Schechter prediction for
\beta_{f}
derived in
App.
B
, see Eqs. (
282
) and
(
298
). The same enhanced curvature spectrum that
triggers PBH formation also sources, at second order, an adiabatic
scalar-induced gravitational-wave background

\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(form)}[\Phi]
at the horizon re-entry of the scale

k_{f}
. We show the representative benchmark spectra in Fig.
16
.

8.1
Spectrum at formation

Inserting the log-normal curvature spectrum into the master formula (
106
) gives analytic approximations for the SIGW spectrum at formation
[
300
]
. We use the dimensionless wavenumber

\kappa_{f}\equiv\frac{k}{k_{f}}.

(239)

For broad spectra,
\Delta\gtrsim 0.1
, the result can be written as

\displaystyle\frac{\Omega_{{\rm GW}}^{\rm(form)}[\Phi]}{\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{R}}^{2}}

\displaystyle\simeq\frac{4\kappa_{f}^{3}}{5\sqrt{\pi}}\,\frac{e^{9\Delta^{2}/4}}{\Delta}\Bigg[\left[\left(\ln\kappa_{f}+\frac{3\Delta^{2}}{2}\right)^{2}+\frac{\Delta^{2}}{2}\right]{\rm erfc}\!\left[\frac{\ln\!\left(\sqrt{3/2}\,\kappa_{f}\right)+3\Delta^{2}/2}{\Delta}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 48.36958pt-\frac{\Delta}{\sqrt{\pi}}\,\exp\!\left[-\frac{\left(\ln\!\left(\sqrt{3/2}\,\kappa_{f}\right)+3\Delta^{2}/2\right)^{2}}{\Delta^{2}}\right]\left[\ln\!\left(\sqrt{2/3}\,\kappa_{f}\right)+\frac{3\Delta^{2}}{2}\right]\Bigg]

(240)

\displaystyle\hskip-56.9055pt+\frac{0.0659}{\Delta^{2}}\,\kappa_{f}^{2}e^{\Delta^{2}}\exp\!\left[-\frac{\left(\Delta^{2}+\ln\!\left(\sqrt{3/4}\,\kappa_{f}\right)\right)^{2}}{\Delta^{2}}\right]+\frac{1}{3}\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\,\kappa_{f}^{-4}\,\frac{e^{8\Delta^{2}}}{\Delta}\exp\!\left[-\frac{\ln^{2}\kappa_{f}}{2\Delta^{2}}\right]{\rm erfc}\!\left[\frac{4\Delta^{2}-\ln(\kappa_f/4)}{\sqrt{2}\Delta}\right].

For narrow spectra,
\Delta\lesssim 0.1
, it is useful to define

\chi_{f}\equiv\kappa_{f}e^{\Delta^{2}}.

(241)

The spectrum is then approximated by

\displaystyle\frac{\Omega_{{\rm GW}}^{\rm(form)}[\Phi]}{\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{R}}^{2}}

\displaystyle\simeq 3\kappa_{f}^{2}e^{\Delta^{2}}\Bigg[{\rm erf}\!\left(\frac{1}{\Delta}{\rm arcsinh}\frac{\chi_{f}}{2}\right)-{\rm erf}\!\left(\frac{1}{\Delta}{\rm Re}\,{\rm arccosh}\frac{\chi_{f}}{2}\right)\Bigg]\left(1-\frac{\chi_{f}^{2}}{4}\right)^{2}\left(1-\frac{3\chi_{f}^{2}}{2}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt\times\Bigg\{\left[\frac{1}{2}\left(1-\frac{3\chi_{f}^{2}}{2}\right)\ln\!\left|1-\frac{4}{3\chi_{f}^{2}}\right|-1\right]^{2}+\frac{\pi^{2}}{4}\left(1-\frac{3\chi_{f}^{2}}{2}\right)^{2}\Theta\!\left(2-\sqrt{3}\chi_{f}\right)\Bigg\}.

(242)

8.2
Present-day amplitude

The SIGW signal
\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(form)}[\Phi]
is generated at PBH formation, deep inside the first radiation era
\rm RD_{1}
. It is therefore subsequently diluted by the entropy release at PBH evaporation in exactly the same way as the
\rm RD_{1}
contributions of Sec.
7
. Combining the dilution factor
D
of Eq. (
264
) with the redshift factor
\mathcal{D}(T_{f},T_{0})
of Eq. (
114
), the present-day amplitude reads

\Omega_{\rm GW,0}^{\rm(form)}[\Phi](k)\simeq D^{-4/3}\,\mathcal{D}(T_{f},T_{0})\,\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(form)}[\Phi](k,\eta_{f}),

(243)

and the corresponding frequency redshift is

\displaystyle\frac{f_{0}}{f_{f}}=\frac{a(T_{f})}{a(T_{0})}

\displaystyle=D^{-1}\left(\frac{g_{\star,s}(T_{0})}{g_{\star,s}(T_{f})}\right)^{\!1/3}\!\frac{T_{0}}{T_{f}}

(244)

\displaystyle\simeq 2.5\times 10^{-25}\,D^{-1}\,\gamma_{\rm H}^{-1/2}\left(\frac{M_{{\rm PBH},f}}{10^{9}~{\rm g}}\right)^{1/2}\left(\frac{g_{\star}(T_{f})}{106.75}\right)^{1/4}\left(\frac{106.75}{g_{\star,s}(T_{f})}\right)^{1/3},

(245)

where in the second line we have used the expression of
T_{f}
in Eq. (
249
). The SIGW frequency at production
f_{f}
is related to the comoving scale at formation
k_{f}=2\pi f_{f}
, which we can derive from Eqs. (
270
) and (
277
)

\displaystyle k_{f}

\displaystyle\simeq\frac{5.4\times 10^{18}~{\rm Mpc}^{-1}}{D^{1/3}}\,\frac{\gamma_{\rm H}^{1/2}}{h_{\star}^{1/3}(T_{f})}\left(\frac{106.75}{g_{\star}(T_{f})}\right)^{1/12}\left(\frac{10^{9}~{\rm g}}{M_{\rm PBH}}\right)^{1/2}.

(246)

We deduce

f_{0}\simeq\frac{8.2~{\rm kHz}}{D^{1/3}}\,\frac{\gamma_{\rm H}^{1/2}}{h_{\star}^{1/3}(T_{f})}\left(\frac{106.75}{g_{\star}(T_{f})}\right)^{1/12}\left(\frac{10^{9}~{\rm g}}{M_{\rm PBH}}\right)^{1/2}.

(247)

Figure 16:
Present-day SIGW signal
\Omega_{\rm GW,0}^{\rm(form)}[\Phi]
from PBH formation, Eq. (
243
), in the
(M_{\rm{PBH}},\beta_{f})
plane. The width
\Delta
of the curvature peak is kept as a free parameter.

9
High-frequency GWs: PBH mergers and Hawking emission

In addition to SIGWs, a gas of PBHs can also generate gravitational waves through mergers and direct graviton emission. We briefly summarize the relevant production channels here and refer to the companion paper
[
163
]
for a detailed treatment:

1.

Isolated Mergers.

Because PBHs are randomly distributed in space, they naturally exhibit local over- and underdensities. When two or three PBHs are sufficiently close, their mutual gravitational attraction can overcome the Hubble expansion, leading to the formation of bound systems that decouple from the Hubble flow. These systems can subsequently inspiral and merge—potentially mediated by the presence of a third body—thereby emitting gravitational waves.

2.

Clusters.

Beyond isolated few-body systems, PBHs can form clusters of various sizes throughout the eMD era. Within these clusters, PBHs virialize, and dynamical interactions—such as hyperbolic encounters—can lead to the formation of bound binaries or three-body systems. These systems then merge and emit gravitational waves.

3.

Hawking evaporation.

PBHs emit Hawking radiation with an (approximately) blackbody spectrum (see Sec.
2.1
). This emission includes gravitons, with characteristic frequencies set by the initial PBH temperature.

All of these mechanisms produce gravitational waves at very high frequencies, rendering them inaccessible to current and planned interferometers. In principle, they could contribute to the effective number of relativistic degrees of freedom,
N_{\rm eff}
. In
[
163
]
, these contributions and their associated spectra are computed in detail, and conservative upper bounds are derived. It is shown that the high-frequency contributions remain negligible for
N_{\rm eff}
and therefore do not modify the newly obtained constraints.

10
Conclusion

The main result of this work is that the scalar-induced gravitational-wave (SIGW) signal from a realistic gas of evaporating ultra-light primordial black holes (PBHs) is orders of magnitude weaker than the monochromatic Poltergeist amplitudes commonly quoted in the recent literature. The physical origin of this suppression is the irreducible width of the PBH mass function. Spherical gravitational collapse in general relativity, through Choptuik’s critical scaling, imposes the universal infrared behaviour (see Fig.
6
)

\psi_{f}\propto M_{\rm{PBH}}^{1+1/\gamma_{\rm M}}\simeq M_{\rm{PBH}}^{3.78}.

This tail cannot be removed by tuning the primordial curvature spectrum. Once this realistic mass spread is included, PBHs evaporate over a finite time interval rather than simultaneously. The matter-to-radiation transition is therefore smoothed, and the post-evaporation suppression of the Newtonian potential steepens from the monochromatic scaling
\mathcal{S}_{\Phi}\propto k^{-1/3}
to
\mathcal{S}_{\Phi}\propto k^{-4/3}
, see Figs.
10
and
12
. As a result, the SIGW spectrum is no longer dominated by the parametrically enhanced Poltergeist signal. Instead, it becomes qualitatively similar to the spectrum produced in a generic early matter-dominated era with gradual reheating, such as one driven by the decay of a heavy long-lived particle. This suppression relaxes the BBN bound on
\Delta N_{\rm eff}
from PBH-induced SIGWs and reopens regions of the
(M_{\rm{PBH}},\beta_{f})
plane that were previously thought to be excluded or within reach of future GW searches, see Fig.
2
.

We have also provided a complete inventory of the SIGW signal in PBH reheating scenarios. In total, we computed eight distinct contributions to
\Omega_{\rm GW}
, summarized in Tab.
3
. These contributions are generated throughout the cosmological history

{\rm form}\to{\rm RD}_{1}\to{\rm eMD}\to{\rm RD}_{2},

where PBH formation is followed by an initial radiation era
\rm(RD_{1})
, a PBH-driven early matter era
\rm(eMD)
, and a final radiation era
\rm(RD_{2})
after PBH evaporation. This decomposition shows that, once the Poltergeist enhancement is suppressed by the realistic mass distribution, the remaining SIGW channels become comparable to it and can even dominate in parts of parameter space, although they remain below the projected reach of future GW observatories. For the benchmark of Fig.
1
,the dominant effect of the realistic mass spread is confined to the post-evaporation Poltergeist component,
\Omega_{\rm GW}^{\rm(RD_{2},Polt.)}
, which is highly sensitive to the duration of reheating. By contrast, the eMD contribution changes only mildly, while the other SIGW channels in Tab.
3
are essentially insensitive to the details of the PBH-to-radiation transition. We also revisited earlier results for the SIGW signal from a monochromatic PBH gas and from a generic eMD phase with a constant decay rate. We confirm these results and provide new analytic expressions in Apps.
G
and
I
.

The study of the high-frequency contributions from Hawking gravitons and binary mergers, summarised in Sec.
9
, are reported in the companion paper
[
163
]
where we show that they do not impact the constraints in Fig.
2
.

Since our result differs from the usual expectation
[
211
,
293
,
106
,
111
,
52
,
176
,
175
,
33
,
110
,
113
,
215
,
292
,
116
,
117
]
, we now discuss its robustness. Three independent arguments support this conclusion.

•

The IR slope of the PBH mass function is universal.
The exponent
1+1/\gamma_{\rm M}\simeq 3.78
induced by Choptuik’s critical scaling is fixed by spherical gravitational collapse in general relativity. It is therefore independent of the PBH formation channel, whether PBHs originate from inflationary peaks, first-order phase transitions, topological defects, scalar-field fragmentation, or other mechanisms. Naively, departure from sphericity is expected to broaden the mass function further and can only strengthen the suppression. While the critical exponent
\gamma_{M}
depends on the equation of state
\omega
at horizon crossing
[
267
]
, the high-
k
scaling of the suppression factor,
\mathcal{S}_{\Phi}\propto k^{-4/3}
, does not. Only the overall prefactor of
\mathcal{S}_{\Phi}
depends on
\gamma_{M}
, making the suppression weakly sensitive to the equation of state. Most of this dependence is instead expected to enter through the transfer functions of
S
and
\Phi
, as studied in
[
112
]
.

•

Our prescription is conservative.
We have made choices that maximize, rather than minimize, the SIGW signal. First, we impose a sharp ultraviolet cutoff on
\psi(M_{\rm{PBH}})
. This removes the high-mass tail of the PBH distribution. In a realistic mass function, this tail would make evaporation last longer than
\eta_{\rm cut}\simeq 1.26\,\langle\eta_{\rm eva}\rangle
, smooth the
4/3
cusp of the envelope
F_{\Phi}
, and further suppress the Poltergeist signal. Second, for both adiabatic and isocurvature seeds, we consider the two cutoff prescriptions summarized in Tab.
4
. Cutting the GW convolution at the non-linear scale
k_{\rm{NL}}
gives a lower estimate, while extending it up to the fluid scale
k_{\rm{PBH}}
gives an upper envelope. Our most aggressive prescription therefore combines the narrowest mass distribution allowed by general relativity with the largest physically motivated UV cutoff. It should be interpreted as an upper bound on the SIGW signal. The middle-left panel of Fig.
2
shows that even with this upper envelope choice, the SIGW bounds from
\Delta N_{\rm eff}
and the projected reach of GW interferometers are relaxed wherever the pre-existing BBN bound from energy injection does not already dominate (green region).

•

Agreement with the monochromatic limit.
As shown in Fig.
23
, in the limit
\gamma_{\rm M}\to 0
the analytic and numerical pipelines of this work reproduce the monochromatic Poltergeist amplitudes of Refs.
[
211
,
112
]
, confirming that the orders-of-magnitude reduction we report is driven entirely by the universal IR slope.

A major remaining uncertainty is the contribution from genuinely non-linear halo dynamics during eMD. Adiabatic lattice simulations
[
134
]
, whose results are summarised in App.
H
, find that the GW signal extends beyond the
k_{\rm{NL}}
scale, until the shell-crossing scale
k_{{NL}}\,\simeq\,8\,k_{\rm{NL}}]
, while the isocurvature analogue remains unexplored. This makes the latter a natural target for future numerical work.

” funded by the German Research Foundation (DFG) within the German Excellence Strategy (Project No. 390831469). N.L. is grateful to the
