Values of the geometric factor
\kappa
, the mean PBH mass in units of
M_{f}
,
\gamma_{\rm H}^{(k_{f})}=\langle M_{\rm{PBH}}\rangle/M_{f}
, and the same mean mass in units of the horizon mass at the compaction scale,
\gamma_{\rm H}^{(r_{m})}=\langle M_{\rm{PBH}}\rangle/M_{H}(r_{m})=\gamma_{\rm H}^{(k_{f})}/\kappa^{2}
. The values are shown for the widths used in Fig.
6
, for the two amplitudes
\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{R}}=0.01
and
\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{R}}=0.04
, and for both the Press–Schechter and peak-theory prescriptions. The commonly quoted estimate
\gamma_{\rm H}^{(r_{m})}\sim 0.2
of Ref.
[
71
]
is recovered in the narrow-peak limit
\Delta\simeq 0.1
with the Press–Schechter prescription.

B.4
Narrow-peak approximation

When the curvature spectrum is sufficiently narrow, the integral over horizon masses is dominated by the horizon mass associated with the compaction scale,

M_{H}\simeq M_{H}(r_{m})=\kappa^{2}M_{f}.

(312)

The total abundance then reduces to

\beta_{f}\simeq\int d\ln M_{\rm{PBH}}\,\frac{d\beta(M_{\rm{PBH}},\kappa^{2}M_{f})}{d\ln M_{\rm{PBH}}}\simeq\int_{\delta_{\ell,c}}^{\infty}d\delta_{\ell}\,\frac{M(\delta_{m}(\delta_{\ell}))}{\kappa^{2}M_{f}}\,P(\delta_{\ell},\kappa^{2}M_{f}).

(313)

The second equality follows from integrating over the Dirac distribution in Eq. (
282
). For Press–Schechter statistics and rare events,

\nu\equiv\frac{\delta_{\ell,c}}{\sigma_{0}(r_{m})}\gg 1,

(314)

the integral is exponentially dominated by values close to the threshold. For an order-of-magnitude estimate of the total abundance, the mild mass weighting can be neglected in the threshold-dominated region,

\frac{M(\delta_{m}(\delta_{\ell}))}{\kappa^{2}M_{f}}\simeq\mathcal{O}(1),\qquad\beta_{f}\sim\int_{\delta_{\ell,c}}^{\infty}d\delta_{\ell}\,P(\delta_{\ell},\kappa^{2}M_{f}).

(315)

The Gaussian tail gives

\int_{\delta_{\ell,c}}^{\infty}d\delta_{\ell}\,P(\delta_{\ell},\kappa^{2}M_{f})=\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{erfc}\!\left(\frac{\nu}{\sqrt{2}}\right)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}\,\nu}\exp\!\left(-\frac{\nu^{2}}{2}\right)\left[1+\mathcal{O}(\nu^{-2})\right],\qquad\nu\gg 1.

(316)

Thus the standard rare-event estimate is

\beta_{f}\simeq\frac{\sigma_{0}(r_{m})}{\sqrt{2\pi}\,\delta_{\ell,c}}\exp\!\left[-\frac{\delta_{\ell,c}^{2}}{2\sigma_{0}^{2}(r_{m})}\right],\qquad\frac{\delta_{\ell,c}}{\sigma_{0}(r_{m})}\gg 1.

(317)

The linear threshold is obtained from Eq. (
283
),

\delta_{\ell,c}=\frac{4}{3}\left(1-\sqrt{1-\frac{3}{2}\delta_{c}}\right).

(318)

The same narrow-peak approximation gives the differential distribution by evaluating Eq. (
287
) at
M_{H}\simeq\kappa^{2}M_{f}
,

\frac{d\beta(M_{\rm{PBH}},T_{f})}{d\ln M_{\rm{PBH}}}\simeq\frac{2\mathcal{K}}{\gamma_{\rm M}}\,\frac{\tilde{q}_{f}^{1+1/\gamma_{\rm M}}}{\sqrt{\tilde{\Lambda}_{f}}}\,P\!\left(\delta_{\ell}(M_{\rm{PBH}}),\kappa^{2}M_{f}\right),

(319)

where

\tilde{q}_{f}\equiv\frac{M_{\rm{PBH}}}{\mathcal{K}\kappa^{2}M_{f}},\qquad\tilde{\Lambda}_{f}\equiv 1-\frac{3}{2}\left(\delta_{c}+\tilde{q}_{f}^{1/\gamma_{\rm M}}\right),\qquad\delta_{\ell}(M_{\rm{PBH}})=\frac{4}{3}\left(1-\sqrt{\tilde{\Lambda}_{f}}\right).

(320)

For
\tilde{q}_{f}^{1/\gamma_{\rm M}}\ll 1
,

\delta_{\ell}(M_{\rm{PBH}})\simeq\delta_{\ell,c}+\frac{\tilde{q}_{f}^{1/\gamma_{\rm M}}}{\sqrt{1-\frac{3}{2}\delta_{c}}}+\mathcal{O}\!\left(\tilde{q}_{f}^{2/\gamma_{\rm M}}\right).

(321)

Combining the Gaussian Press–Schechter probability with Eqs. (
317
) and (
319
), the normalized PBH mass distribution becomes

\psi_{f}(M_{\rm{PBH}})\simeq A\,\left(\frac{M_{\rm{PBH}}}{\mathcal{K}\kappa^{2}M_{f}}\right)^{1+1/\gamma_{\rm M}}\exp\!\left[-B\,\frac{\left(M_{\rm{PBH}}/(\mathcal{K}\kappa^{2}M_{f})\right)^{1/\gamma_{\rm M}}}{\sigma_{0}^{2}(r_{m})}\right],

(322)

with

A\equiv\frac{2\mathcal{K}}{\gamma_{\rm M}}\frac{\delta_{\ell,c}}{\sigma_{0}^{2}(r_{m})\sqrt{1-\frac{3}{2}\delta_{c}}},\qquad B\equiv\frac{4}{3}\frac{1-\sqrt{1-\frac{3}{2}\delta_{c}}}{\sqrt{1-\frac{3}{2}\delta_{c}}}.

(323)

This expression recovers the universal infrared scaling and the exponential ultraviolet cutoff, as introduced in the introduction

\psi_{f}(M_{\rm{PBH}})\propto M_{\rm{PBH}}^{1+1/\gamma_{\rm M}}\exp\left[-c_{1}\left(\frac{M_{\rm{PBH}}}{\langle M_{\rm{PBH}}\rangle}\right)^{c_{2}}\right].

(324)

Earlier derivations can be found in Refs.
[
287
,
340
,
227
]
and in Ref.
[
73
, Eq. (3.12)]
. In the limit
\Delta\to 0
, with smoothing at the compaction scale
r_{H}\simeq r_{m}\simeq\kappa/k_{f}
, one finds
\sigma_{0}\simeq 4\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{R}}^{1/2}/9
up to window-function factors. Therefore the relaxation of Eq. (
322
) toward a sharp cutoff requires both
\Delta\to 0
and
\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{R}}\to 0
. This is consistent with our numerical check that the distribution for
\Delta\ll 0.1
remains close to the
\Delta=0.1
case shown in orange in Fig.
6
.

Appendix C
Evolution of
\Phi
in a radiation-matter universe

In this appendix we derive the evolution of the Newtonian potential
\Phi
in a universe filled with matter and radiation, and extract the transfer functions
T_{\Phi}(\kappa)
and
T_{S}(\kappa)
that map adiabatic and isocurvature initial conditions onto the late-time matter-era amplitude used in the main text. We first set up the coupled two-fluid perturbation equations in the Newtonian gauge, casting them as a closed system for
\Phi
and the PBH-radiation isocurvature mode
S
. We then solve this system, numerically and through analytic interpolation formulae, and compare our transfer functions with those of Ref.
[
112
]
.

C.1
Two-fluid perturbation equations

From the Einstein equations for a homogeneous and isotropic background one recovers the standard Friedmann and continuity relations,

\displaystyle H^{2}=\frac{8\pi G}{3}\,\overline{\rho},\qquad\dot{H}=-\tfrac{3}{2}(1+\omega)H^{2},\qquad\dot{\overline{\rho}}+3(1+\omega)H\,\overline{\rho}=0,

(325)

where
H\equiv\dot{a}/a
is the Hubble parameter and
\omega\equiv\overline{p}/\overline{\rho}
denotes the equation-of-state parameter.
Expressed in conformal time
\eta
, these relations take the form

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}^{2}=\frac{8\pi G}{3}\overline{\rho}a^{2},\qquad\mathcal{H}^{\prime}=-\frac{1+3\omega}{2}\mathcal{H}^{2},\qquad\overline{\rho}^{\prime}+3(1+\omega)\mathcal{H}\overline{\rho}=0,

(326)

where
\mathcal{H}=a^{\prime}/a
and
dt=a\,d\eta
. The two Hubble parameters satisfy
H=\mathcal{H}/a
and
\dot{H}=(\mathcal{H}^{\prime}-\mathcal{H}^{2})/a^{2}
.
For a universe filled with a perfect fluid of constant
\omega
, the background evolution is given by

\overline{\rho}\propto a^{-3(1+\omega)},\qquad a\propto t^{\frac{2}{3(1+\omega)}}\propto\eta^{\tfrac{2}{1+3\omega}},\qquad\mathcal{H}=\frac{2}{(1+3\omega)\eta}.

(327)

We now work with scalar metric perturbations in the Newton gauge,

\displaystyle ds^{2}=a^{2}(\eta)\Big[(1+2\Psi)\,d\eta^{2}-(1-2\Phi)\,\delta_{ij}\,dx^{i}dx^{j}\Big].

(328)

In the Newtonian gauge, the stress–energy tensor can be written as

T^{\mu}_{\ \nu}\;=\;\begin{pmatrix}\bar{\rho}+\delta\rho&-(\bar{\rho}+\bar{p})\,v_{j}\\[6.0pt]
(\bar{\rho}+\bar{p})\,v^{i}&-(\bar{p}+\delta p)\,\delta^{i}_{\ j}\end{pmatrix},

(329)

with the velocity convention
v_{i}=\delta_{ij}v^{j}
.
Setting the anisotropic stress to zero (so the spatial block is
\propto\delta^{i}_{\ j}
) enforces the equality of the Newtonian potentials,
\Phi=\Psi
.
The linearized Einstein equations in position space read
[
298
,
46
]

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}\Phi-3\mathcal{H}\big(\Phi^{\prime}+\mathcal{H}\Phi\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{3}{2}\mathcal{H}^{2}\,\delta,

(330)

\displaystyle\!\left(\Phi^{\prime}+\mathcal{H}\Phi\right)

\displaystyle=-\frac{3}{2}\mathcal{H}^{2}(1+\omega)\,v,

(331)

\displaystyle\Phi^{\prime\prime}+3\mathcal{H}\Phi^{\prime}-3\omega\mathcal{H}^{2}\Phi

\displaystyle=\frac{3}{2}\mathcal{H}^{2}\,\frac{\delta p}{\overline{\rho}}.

(332)

where primes denote
d/d\eta
, and
\mathcal{H}\equiv a^{\prime}/a
. We have introduced the velocity potential
\partial_{i}v\equiv v_{i}
, the density contrast
\delta\equiv\delta\rho/\bar{\rho}
, the equation of state
w\equiv\bar{p}/\bar{\rho}
, and the sound speed

c_{s}^{2}\equiv\left.\frac{\delta p}{\delta\rho}\right|_{\delta p_{\rm nad}=0}=\frac{\bar{p}^{\prime}}{\bar{\rho}^{\prime}}=\omega-\frac{\omega^{\prime}}{3(1+\omega)\mathcal{H}}.

(333)

Setting
\delta p_{\rm nad}=0
restricts to adiabatic modes, meaning pressure perturbations are entirely induced by density perturbations. Consequently the local perturbative relation follows the background equation of state,
\delta p/\delta\rho=d\bar{p}/d\bar{\rho}

[
40
]
.
Energy–momentum conservation implies
[
46
]

\displaystyle\delta^{\prime}-(1+w)\,(-\Delta v+3\Phi^{\prime})+3\mathcal{H}\,(c_{s}^{2}-w)\,\delta

\displaystyle=-3\mathcal{H}\delta_{\rm p,nad},

(334)

\displaystyle v^{\prime}+\mathcal{H}v+\frac{c_{s}^{2}}{1+w}\,\delta^{C}+\Phi

\displaystyle=-\frac{\delta_{\rm p,nad}}{1+\omega},

(335)

where
\delta^{C}\equiv\delta-3(1+\omega)\mathcal{H}v
is the comoving density contrast and
\delta_{p,\rm nad}
is the non-adiabatic pressure perturbation

\delta_{p,\rm nad}\equiv\frac{\delta p_{\rm nad}}{\overline{\rho}},\qquad\textrm{where}\quad\delta p_{\rm nad}\equiv\delta p-c_{s}^{2}\delta\rho.

(336)

Combining Eqs. (
330
) and (
332
) leads to

\Phi^{\prime\prime}+3\mathcal{H}(1+c_{s}^{2})\Phi^{\prime}+\left[3(c_{s}^{2}-\omega)\mathcal{H}^{2}-c_{s}^{2}\Delta\right]\Phi=\frac{3}{2}\mathcal{H}^{2}\delta_{p,\rm nad}.

(337)

We now suppose that the stress-energy tensor of the universe is a linear sum of two fluids such that we can write
(a=\{1,2\})

\rho=\sum_{a}\rho_{a},\qquad p=\sum_{a}p_{a},\qquad v=\sum_{a}\frac{1+\omega_{a}}{1+\omega}\Omega_{a}v_{a}.

(338)

It is straightforward to show that

\omega=\sum_{a}\Omega_{a}\omega_{a},\qquad c_{s}^{2}=\sum_{a}\frac{1+\omega_{a}}{1+\omega}\Omega_{a}c_{a}^{2},

(339)

where
c_{a}
is the speed of sound of fluid
a
, and
\Omega_{a}=\rho_{a}/\rho_{a}
with
\sum_{a}\Omega_{a}=1
. Assuming that the perfect fluids are not coupled to each other, continuity and Euler equations hold separately

\displaystyle\delta_{a}^{\prime}-(1+w_{a})\,(-\Delta v_{a}+3\Phi^{\prime})+3\mathcal{H}\,(c_{a}^{2}-\omega_{a})\,\delta_{a}

\displaystyle=-3\mathcal{H}\delta_{\rm p,nad,a},

(340)

\displaystyle v_{a}^{\prime}+\mathcal{H}v_{a}+\frac{c_{a}^{2}}{1+\omega_{a}}\,\delta_{a}^{C}+\Phi

\displaystyle=-\frac{\delta_{\rm p,nad,a}}{1+\omega},

(341)

where
\delta_{a}^{C}\equiv\delta_{a}-3(1+\omega_{a})\mathcal{H}v_{a}
.
Using Eq. (
336
), the non-adiabatic pressure perturbation of the mixture can be expressed as
22
22

22

Note the minus sign difference with respect to
[
298
, Eq. 5.191]
which has a typo.

\delta_{p,\rm nad}=\sum_{a}\Omega_{a}\delta_{p,\rm nad,a}-\sum_{a}(c_{s}^{2}-c_{a}^{2})\Omega_{a}\delta_{a},\qquad\textrm{where}\quad\delta_{p,\rm nad,a}\equiv\delta p_{a}-c_{a}^{2}\delta\rho_{a}.

(342)

Plugging Eq. (
339
) into Eq. (
342
), we obtain

\displaystyle\delta_{p,\rm nad}

\displaystyle=\sum_{a}\Omega_{a}\delta_{p,\rm nad,a}+c_{ab}S_{ab},\qquad c_{ab}\equiv\frac{\Omega_{a}\Omega_{b}(1+\omega_{a})(1+\omega_{b})}{1+\omega}(c_{a}^{2}-c_{b}^{2})

(343)

where
c_{ab}
is a parameter and
S_{ab}
is the so-called isocurvature perturbation between fluids
a
and
b

S_{ab}\equiv\frac{\delta_{a}}{(1+\omega_{a})}-\frac{\delta_{b}}{(1+\omega_{b})}.

(344)

Using Eq. (
340
), we calculate

\displaystyle S_{ab}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=-\Delta v_{ab}-3\mathcal{H}\left(\frac{\delta_{\rm p,nad,a}}{1+\omega_{a}}-\frac{\delta_{{\rm p,nad,b}}}{1+\omega_{b}}\right),\qquad v_{ab}\equiv v_{a}-v_{b},

(345)

where
v_{\rm ab}
is the relative fluid velocity between fluids
a
and
b
.
We now assume the fluids are barotropic

{p_{a}=\omega_{a}(\rho_{a})\rho_{a}}\qquad\implies\qquad\delta_{p,\rm nad,a}=\delta p_{a}-c_{a}^{2}\delta\rho_{a}=0,

(346)

and for
b
as well.
Using Eq. (
341
), we calculate

\displaystyle S_{ab}^{\prime\prime}+\mathcal{H}S_{ab}^{\prime}-\frac{\Omega_{a}(1+\omega_{a})c_{b}^{2}+\Omega_{b}(1+\omega_{b})c_{a}^{2}}{1+\omega}\Delta S_{ab}=(c_{a}^{2}-c_{b}^{2})\frac{\Delta\delta^{C}}{1+\omega},

(347)

where
\delta^{(C)}\equiv\delta-3(1+\omega)\mathcal{H}v
is the comoving density contrast. Using Eqs. (
330
) and (
331
), we obtain Poisson’s equation

\nabla^{2}\Phi=\frac{3}{2}\mathcal{H}^{2}\,\delta^{(C)}.

(348)

Using Eqs. (
337
) and (
343
) and Eqs. (
347
) and (
348
) we get

\displaystyle\Phi^{\prime\prime}+3\mathcal{H}(1+c_{s}^{2})\Phi^{\prime}+\left[3(c_{s}^{2}-\omega)\mathcal{H}^{2}-c_{s}^{2}\Delta\right]\Phi=\frac{3}{2}\mathcal{H}^{2}c_{ab}S_{ab},

(349)

\displaystyle S_{ab}^{\prime\prime}+\mathcal{H}S_{ab}^{\prime}-\frac{\Omega_{a}(1+\omega_{a})c_{b}^{2}+\Omega_{b}(1+\omega_{b})c_{a}^{2}}{1+\omega}\Delta S_{ab}=2(c_{a}^{2}-c_{b}^{2})\frac{\Delta^{2}\Phi}{3\mathcal{H}^{2}(1+\omega)}.

(350)

We can use the chain rules:

X^{\prime}(\eta)=y^{\prime}\dot{X}(y)=y\mathcal{H}\dot{X}(y),\qquad X^{\prime\prime}(\eta)=(y^{\prime})^{2}\ddot{X}(y)+y^{\prime\prime}\dot{X}(y)=y^{2}\mathcal{H}^{2}\ddot{X}(y)+\frac{1-3\omega}{2}y\mathcal{H}^{2}\dot{X}(y),

(351)

where we used
y^{\prime\prime}=y(\mathcal{H}^{\prime}+\mathcal{H}^{2})=y(1-3\omega)\mathcal{H}^{2}/2
, cf. Eq. (
7
). Then Eqs. (
349
) and (
350
) become, in Fourier space,

\displaystyle y^{2}\ddot{\Phi}_{\mathbf{k}}(y)+\frac{y}{2}\left(7+6c_{s}^{2}-3\omega\right)\dot{\Phi}_{\mathbf{k}}+\left[3(c_{s}^{2}-\omega)+c_{s}^{2}\left(\frac{k}{\mathcal{H}}\right)^{2}\right]\Phi=\frac{3}{2}c_{ab}S_{ab,\mathbf{k}},

(352)

\displaystyle y^{2}\ddot{S}_{ab,\mathbf{k}}+\frac{3}{2}y(1-\omega)\dot{S}_{ab,\mathbf{k}}+\frac{\Omega_{a}(1+\omega_{a})c_{b}^{2}+\Omega_{b}(1+\omega_{b})c_{a}^{2}}{1+\omega}\left(\frac{k}{\mathcal{H}}\right)^{2}S_{ab,\mathbf{k}}=\frac{2(c_{a}^{2}-c_{b}^{2})}{3(1+\omega)}\left(\frac{k}{\mathcal{H}}\right)^{4}\Phi_{\mathbf{k}}.

(353)

Figure 17:
Cosmological evolution of the Newtonian perturbation
\Phi
and isentropic perturbation
S
assuming adiabatic (
left
) and isocurvature (
right
) initial conditions, for
\kappa=k/k_{\rm reh}=10^{-1}
(
top
) and
10^{2}
(
bottom
). The universe is dominated by radiation for
y<1
and by matter for
y>1
. In all panels we plot the absolute value of the perturbations.

Figure 18:
Transfer function of the Newtonian potential
\Phi
assuming adiabatic (
blue
) and isocurvature (
red
) initial conditions. The solid lines are obtained from numerically integrating Eqs. (
360
) and (
361
), the dashed lines correspond to
Eqs. (
365
) and (
367
), while the dotted lines correspond to Eq. (
368
).

Long before evaporation
\eta\ll\eta_{\rm eva}
, the universe containing PBH can be described as a mixture of a matter and radiation background,
\rho=\rho_{m}+\rho_{r}
with
\rho_{m}\equiv\rho_{\rm{PBH}}
. We have

\Omega_{m}=\frac{y}{1+y},\qquad\Omega_{r}=\frac{1}{1+y},\qquad\mathcal{H}=k_{\rm eq}\sqrt{\frac{1+y}{2y^{2}}},

(354)

where
y\equiv a/a_{\rm eq}
which upon solving
a^{\prime}(\eta)=\mathcal{H}a
leads to

y(\eta)=\left(\frac{\eta}{\eta_{\star}}\right)^{2}+2\left(\frac{\eta}{\eta_{\star}}\right),\qquad\textrm{with}\quad\eta_{\star}\equiv\frac{\eta_{\rm eq}}{\sqrt{2}-1}=\frac{2\sqrt{2}}{k_{\rm eq}}.

(355)

Using Eq. (
339
), we also have

\omega_{m}=c_{m}^{2}=0,\qquad\omega_{r}=c_{r}^{2}=\frac{1}{3},\qquad\implies\qquad\omega=\frac{1}{3(1+y)},\qquad c_{s}^{2}=\frac{1}{3(1+\frac{3}{4}y)}.

(356)

We denote by
S
the isocurvature perturbation between PBHs and radiation

S\equiv\delta_{\rm{PBH}}-\frac{3}{4}\delta_{\rm r}.

(357)

The system of equations (
349
) and (
350
) become (with
\kappa\equiv k/k_{\rm eq}
and
x\equiv k\eta
)

\displaystyle\kappa^{2}\Phi_{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime\prime}(x)+\kappa\sqrt{\frac{1+y}{2y^{2}}}\frac{16+9y}{4+3y}\Phi_{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime}(x)+\frac{3+8\kappa^{2}y}{24y+18y^{2}}\Phi_{\mathbf{k}}(x)=-\frac{S_{\mathbf{k}}(x)}{4+3y},

(358)

\displaystyle\kappa^{2}S_{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime\prime}(x)+\kappa\sqrt{\frac{1+y}{2y^{2}}}S_{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime}(x)+\frac{y\kappa^{2}}{4+3y}S_{\mathbf{k}}(x)=-\frac{4y^{2}\kappa^{4}}{3(4+3y)}\Phi_{\mathbf{k}}(x).

(359)

where
y(x)
follows from Eq. (
355
).
Using the chain rules in Eq. (
351
), the system of equation becomes

\displaystyle\ddot{\Phi}_{\mathbf{k}}(y)+\left(7-\frac{1}{1+y}+\frac{8}{4+3y}\right)\frac{\dot{\Phi}_{\mathbf{k}}(y)}{2y}+\frac{(3+8\kappa^{2}y)}{3(1+y)(4+3y)}\frac{\Phi_{\mathbf{k}}(y)}{y}=-\frac{2S_{\mathbf{k}}(y)}{y(1+y)(4+3y)},

(360)

\displaystyle\ddot{S}_{\mathbf{k}}(y)+\frac{2+3y}{2y(1+y)}\dot{S}_{\mathbf{k}}(y)+\frac{2y}{(1+y)(4+3y)}\kappa^{2}S_{\mathbf{k}}(y)=-\frac{8y^{2}}{3(1+y)(4+3y)}\kappa^{4}\Phi_{\mathbf{k}}(y).

(361)

For more details, we refer the reader to pioneering works
[
240
,
241
]
, a textbook
[
298
]
, and more recent Refs.
[
112
,
347
]
.

C.2
Transfer functions

The solution of the system of Eqs. (
360
) and (
361
) are displayed in Fig.
17
. The scalar metric perturbation approaches a constant at late times in the matter era, which we write as
23
23

23

With the metric convention of Eq. (
328
), the transfer function
T_{S}
differs by an overall minus sign from the convention commonly used in the literature, e.g.
[
240
,
112
]
. This sign originates from the source terms in Eqs. (
359
) and (
361
). Since only the scaling and magnitude of
T_{S}
are relevant, we omit this overall sign in this paper.

\Phi_{\mathbf{k}}(y\gg 1)=T_{\Phi}(\kappa)\Phi_{\mathbf{k}}(0)+T_{S}(\kappa)S_{\mathbf{k}}(0).

(362)

where
\Phi_{\mathbf{k}}(0)
,
S_{\mathbf{k}}(0)
are the initial condition, and
T_{\Phi}(\kappa)
,
T_{S}(\kappa)
are the transfer functions.
For adiabatic perturbations and
\kappa\equiv k/k_{\rm reh}\ll 1
, we can simply use conservation of the comoving curvature perturbation
\mathcal{R}\simeq\left(5+3\omega\right)\Phi/\left(3+3\omega\right)
on superhorizon scales

\kappa\ll 1:\qquad T_{\Phi}(\kappa)\simeq\left(\frac{3+3\omega}{5+3\omega}\right)_{\omega=0}\left(\frac{5+3\omega}{3+3\omega}\right)_{\omega=1/3}=\frac{9}{10},

(363)

where the factor
\left(\frac{3+3\omega}{5+3\omega}\right)_{\omega=0}
arises because
\Phi(0)
is defined in the pre-PBH radiation era, see Eq. (
64
).
Ref.
[
204
]
calculated the transfer function for
\delta^{(C)}
which upon using Poisson’s equation in Eq. (
348
) can be converted in a transfer function for
\Phi

\kappa\gg 1:\qquad T_{\Phi}(\kappa)\simeq 10.8\frac{\textrm{ln}\left(0.13\kappa\right)}{\kappa^{2}}.

(364)

For any given
k
, we find that a very good interpolation formulae is given by

T_{\Phi}(\kappa)\simeq\left(\frac{10}{9}+\frac{0.09\kappa^{2}}{\ln\left(1+0.13\kappa\right)}\right)^{-1}.

(365)

For isocurvature perturbations, Ref.
[
240
]
found

T_{S}(\kappa)\simeq\begin{cases}\dfrac{1}{5},&\kappa\ll 1,\\[6.0pt]
\dfrac{C}{\kappa^{2}},&\kappa\gg 1.\end{cases}

(366)

where
C=3/4
. Instead, we find that the numerical solution prefers
C\simeq 1
, in agreement with Ref.
[
112
]
. The first line of Eq. (
366
) can be determined using conservation of
\zeta=-\Phi+\delta_{\rm tot}/3(1+\omega)
. We find that a very good interpolation function is

T_{S}(\kappa)\simeq\left(5^{1/c}+\kappa^{2/c}\right)^{-c},\qquad c=2.

(367)

Ref.
[
112
]
calculates the transfer functions to be:

T_{\Phi}(\kappa)\simeq\left(10/9+0.06\kappa^{1.83}\right)^{-1},\qquad\textrm{and}\qquad T_{S}(\kappa)\simeq\left(5+\kappa^{2}\right)^{-1}.

(368)

The power-law (instead of a log) makes
T_{\Phi}(\kappa)
more appropriate to derive an analytical function.
In Fig.
18
, we compare the analytical formulae of Ref.
[
112
]
, with the more precise analytical formulae in Eqs. (
365
) and (
367
) and the numerical solution obtained from solving the system of Eqs. (
360
) and (
361
). We conclude that the transfer function in Eq. (
368
) derived in Ref.
[
112
]
are good enough to approximate the numerical solution and hence we rely on them in the main text.

C.3
Analytical solution in RD

For modes entering the horizon well before matter-radiation equality (
\kappa\gg 1
), the system (
358
)–(
359
) admits a closed-form solution throughout the radiation-dominated (RD) regime
y\ll 1
, equivalently
x/\kappa\ll 1
. Both limits are controlled by the same small parameter since, from Eq. (
355
) and
\eta_{\star}=2\sqrt{2}/k_{\rm eq}
,

y=\frac{x}{\sqrt{2}\,\kappa}+\frac{x^{2}}{8\kappa^{2}}+\mathcal{O}(\kappa^{-3}),\qquad\frac{1}{y}=\frac{\sqrt{2}\,\kappa}{x}-\frac{1}{4}+\mathcal{O}(\kappa^{-1}).

(369)

Expanding every coefficient of Eqs. (
358
)–(
359
) to next-to-leading order in
\kappa^{-1}
and dividing by
\kappa^{2}
yields the reduced system
24
24

24

Our sign convention for the metric perturbation differs from that of Ref.
[
115
]
: we use
ds^{2}=a^{2}[(1+2\Psi)d\eta^{2}-(1-2\Phi)\delta_{ij}dx^{i}dx^{j}]
, while Ref.
[
115
]
uses
ds^{2}=a^{2}[-(1+2\Psi)d\eta^{2}+(1+2\Phi_{\rm D})\delta_{ij}dx^{i}dx^{j}]
, so that
\Phi_{\rm D}=-\Phi
. Eqs. (
370
)–(
371
) match Eqs. (2.8)–(2.9) of Ref.
[
115
]
after this identification, which flips the sign of the
S
–
\Phi
coupling terms.

\displaystyle\Phi_{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime\prime}+\frac{4}{x}\Phi_{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime}+\frac{1}{3}\Phi_{\mathbf{k}}+\frac{1}{4\sqrt{2}\,\kappa x}\!\left[x\Phi_{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime}+(1-x^{2})\Phi_{\mathbf{k}}+2S_{\mathbf{k}}\right]

\displaystyle\simeq 0,

(370)

\displaystyle S_{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime\prime}+\frac{1}{x}S_{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime}+\frac{x^{2}}{6}\Phi_{\mathbf{k}}-\frac{1}{2\sqrt{2}\,\kappa}\!\left[S_{\mathbf{k}}^{\prime}-\frac{x}{2}S_{\mathbf{k}}+\frac{x^{3}}{12}\Phi_{\mathbf{k}}\right]

\displaystyle\simeq 0.

(371)

The hierarchy between the leading and bracketed terms motivates the perturbative ansatz
\Phi_{\mathbf{k}}=\kappa^{-1}\Phi_{1}+\mathcal{O}(\kappa^{-2})
and
S_{\mathbf{k}}=S_{\mathbf{k}}(0)+\kappa^{-1}S_{1}+\mathcal{O}(\kappa^{-2})
, assuming pure isocurvature initial conditions
\Phi_{\mathbf{k}}(0)=0
,
S_{\mathbf{k}}(0)={\rm const}
. At leading order the bracketed terms decouple and we recover
\Phi_{0}=0
and
S_{0}=S_{\mathbf{k}}(0)
. At order
\kappa^{-1}
, only
S_{0}
sources
\Phi_{1}
, so that

\Phi_{1}^{\prime\prime}+\frac{4}{x}\Phi_{1}^{\prime}+\frac{1}{3}\Phi_{1}=-\frac{S_{\mathbf{k}}(0)}{2\sqrt{2}\,x},

(372)

whose regular-at-origin solution combines a particular piece
\propto(6/x^{3}+1/x)
with a homogeneous contribution built from the spherical Bessel functions
j_{1}(c_{s}x)/x^{2}
and
y_{1}(c_{s}x)/x^{2}
, where
c_{s}=1/\sqrt{3}
, yielding

\Phi_{1}(x)=-\frac{3S_{\mathbf{k}}(0)}{2\sqrt{2}\,x^{3}}\left[6+x^{2}-2\sqrt{3}\,x\sin(c_sx)-6\cos(c_sx)\right].

(373)

Substituting (
373
) into the order-
\kappa^{-1}
part of (
371
), the linear-in-
x
pieces of the source cancel exactly, leaving
(xS_{1}^{\prime})^{\prime}=(S_{\mathbf{k}}(0)/4\sqrt{2})[6-2\sqrt{3}x\sin(c_sx)-6\cos(c_sx)]
, which integrates with
S_{1}(0)=0
to

S_{1}(x)=\frac{3S_{\mathbf{k}}(0)}{2\sqrt{2}}\left[x+\sqrt{3}\sin(c_sx)-2\sqrt{3}\,{\rm Si}(c_{s}x)\right],

(374)

where
{\rm Si}
denotes the sine-integral function. Collecting (
373
)–(
374
), the finite-
x
RD transfer functions read

\displaystyle\frac{\Phi_{\mathbf{k}}(x)}{S_{\mathbf{k}}(0)}

\displaystyle=-\frac{3}{2\sqrt{2}\,\kappa\,x^{3}}\left[6+x^{2}-2\sqrt{3}\,x\sin(c_sx)-6\cos(c_sx)\right],

(375)

\displaystyle\frac{S_{\mathbf{k}}(x)}{S_{\mathbf{k}}(0)}

\displaystyle=1+\frac{3}{2\sqrt{2}\,\kappa}\left[x+\sqrt{3}\sin(c_sx)-2\sqrt{3}\,{\rm Si}(c_{s}x)\right],

(376)

valid throughout the RD regime
x/\kappa\ll 1
at leading order in
\kappa^{-1}
, and in agreement with Eqs. (2.11)–(2.12) of Ref.
[
115
]
. The late-time transfer function
T_{S}(\kappa)
appearing in (
366
) follows by evaluating
\Phi_{\mathbf{k}}
at the epoch where the RD solution matches the matter-era plateau. For
\kappa\gg 1
the mode is deeply subhorizon at equality, so Poisson’s equation (
348
) together with
\mathcal{H}^{2}\delta_{m}^{C}\propto y^{-2}\cdot y=\text{const}
during MD freezes
\Phi_{\mathbf{k}}
at its value at equality, reached at
x_{\rm eq}\equiv k\eta_{\rm eq}=2(\sqrt{2}-1)\kappa
. Plugging
x_{\rm eq}
into (
375
) gives the closed-form expression, which after sign flip to match the literature convention, see footnote
23
, reads

T_{S}(\kappa)=\frac{3}{2\sqrt{2}\,\kappa\,x_{\rm eq}^{3}}\left[6+x_{\rm eq}^{2}-2\sqrt{3}\,x_{\rm eq}\sin(c_sx_{\rm eq})-6\cos(c_sx_{\rm eq})\right],

(377)

valid for any
\kappa\gg 1
. In the deep-subhorizon regime the
x_{\rm eq}^{2}
term dominates, the remaining contributions being suppressed by
1/x_{\rm eq}^{2}\sim\kappa^{-2}
, which yields

T_{S}(\kappa)\simeq\frac{3(2+\sqrt{2})}{8\,\kappa^{2}}\approx\frac{1.28}{\kappa^{2}}.

(378)

This reproduces the Kodama–Sasaki scaling in (
366
) with an order-unity coefficient consistent with the numerical value
C\simeq 1
. A precise determination of
C
would require matching (
375
) onto the MD solution across
y\sim 1
, which lies beyond our leading-order RD treatment.

Appendix D
Evolution of
\Phi
during PBH evaporation

Building on the two-fluid framework of App.
C
, here we account for the energy exchange that accompanies PBH decay into radiation, which modifies the perturbation equations relative to the conserved-fluid case. Working in the synchronous gauge comoving with the PBH component, we follow the Newtonian potential
\Phi
through PBH domination, evaporation, and reheating, and obtain an analytic estimate of the suppression factor
\mathcal{S}_{\Phi}(k)
entering Eq. (
402
). We show that
\Phi
stays on a plateau during PBH domination and decays into oscillations once PBHs evaporate, and compute
\mathcal{S}_{\Phi}(k)
for the Choptuik mass distribution, recovering the monochromatic scaling of Ref.
[
211
]
as a limiting case.

D.1
Perturbation equations

When PBH decays into dark radiation, energy and momentum are exchanged between the two fluids.
At linear order, this exchange modifies the usual conservation equations.
Splitting the time and spatial components of
\nabla_{\mu}T^{\mu\nu}
, one obtains
[
27
]

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mu}T^{\mu 0}_{\rm{PBH}}=-C,\qquad

\displaystyle\nabla_{\mu}T^{\mu 0}_{\rm r}=+C,

(379)

\displaystyle\partial_{i}\!\left(\nabla_{\mu}T^{\mu i}_{\rm{PBH}}\right)=-D,\qquad

\displaystyle\partial_{i}\!\left(\nabla_{\mu}T^{\mu i}_{\rm r}\right)=+D,

(380)

where
C
characterises the rate of energy transfer and
D
the corresponding momentum transfer between the fluids.
A major simplification occurs in the synchronous gauge comoving with the PBH component defined by the metric
[
27
,
302
,
211
]

{\rm d}s^{2}=a^{2}\left({\rm d}\eta^{2}-H_{ij}{\rm d}x^{i}{\rm d}x^{j}\right),

(381)

where
H_{ij}=\delta_{ij}+\hat{k}_{i}\hat{k}_{j}h+\left(\hat{k}_{i}\hat{k}_{j}-\frac{1}{3}\delta_{ij}\right)6\varepsilon+\frac{1}{2}h_{ij}
, with
\hat{k}_{i}\equiv k_{i}/|k|
. The quantities
h
and
\varepsilon
are the trace and traceless component of the scalar mode in the synchronous gauge.
25
25

25

h
and
\varepsilon
corresponds
h
and
\eta
in
[
261
,
27
,
302
]
and to
\gamma
and
\varepsilon
in
[
211
]
.
For simplicity, we neglect sources of anisotropic stress. One additional gauge degree of freedom must be fixed, and we impose that the PBH fluid has a vanishing velocity
[
27
,
302
]

\theta_{\rm{PBH}}=0.

(382)

Since we have
\delta g_{00}=\delta g_{0i}=\theta_{\rm{PBH}}=0
, then the decay is isotropic so the only non-zero component of the interaction term is the energy transfer.
Its perturbed expression follows directly from the local decay rate:

C^{(s)}=a\,\Gamma_{\rm{PBH}}\,\rho_{\rm{PBH}}\,\big(1+\delta_{\rm{PBH}}^{(s)}\big),

(383)

where the superscript
(s)
indicates that the quantities are expressed in the synchronous gauge.
Because the decaying particles have no peculiar motion in this gauge and the decay products carry no net momentum, the spatial part of the interaction vector vanishes:

D^{(s)}=0.

(384)

With
D^{(s)}=0
, the Euler equation for PBH reduces to
\theta_{\rm{PBH}}^{(s)\prime}=-\mathcal{H}\,\theta_{\rm{PBH}}^{(s)}=0
,
so the velocity divergence stays zero at all times. The continuity and Euler equations (
379
) and (
380
) in the synchronous gauge read
[
261
,
27
,
302
,
211
]
:

\displaystyle\delta_{\mathrm{m}}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=-\frac{h^{\prime}}{2}

(385)

\displaystyle\delta_{\mathrm{r}}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=-\frac{4}{3}\left(\theta_{\mathrm{r}}+\frac{h^{\prime}}{2}\right)+\frac{\left<\Gamma\rho_{\mathrm{m}}\right>}{\left<\rho_{\mathrm{r}}\right>}\left(\delta_{\mathrm{m}}-\delta_{\mathrm{r}}\right),

(386)

\displaystyle\theta_{\mathrm{r}}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=\frac{k^{2}}{4}\delta_{\mathrm{r}}-\frac{\left<\Gamma\rho_{\mathrm{m}}\right>}{\left<\rho_{\mathrm{r}}\right>}\theta_{\mathrm{r}},

(387)

\displaystyle k^{2}\varepsilon-\frac{1}{2}\frac{a^{\prime}}{a}h^{\prime}

\displaystyle=-\frac{3}{2}\mathcal{H}^{2}\left(\frac{\left<\rho_{\mathrm{m}}\right>}{\left<\rho_{\mathrm{tot}}\right>}\delta_{\mathrm{m}}+\frac{\left<\rho_{\mathrm{r}}\right>}{\left<\rho_{\mathrm{tot}}\right>}\delta_{\mathrm{r}}\right),

(388)

\displaystyle k^{2}\varepsilon^{\prime}

\displaystyle=2\mathcal{H}^{2}\frac{\left<\rho_{\mathrm{r}}\right>}{\left<\rho_{\mathrm{tot}}\right>}\theta_{\mathrm{r}},

(389)

where
\theta\equiv\partial_{i}v^{i}
is the velocity divergence. The background quantities
\left<\rho_{\mathrm{r}}\right>
,
\left<\rho_{\mathrm{m}}\right>
, and
\left<\Gamma\rho_{\mathrm{m}}\right>
are given by Eqs. (
43
), (
42
), (
44
).
The initial conditions for the perturbations are
[
211
]

\displaystyle\delta_{\mathrm{r}}=-\frac{2}{3}C(k\eta)^{2},\quad\delta_{\mathrm{m}}=\frac{3}{4}\delta_{\mathrm{r}},\quad\theta_{\mathrm{r}}=-\frac{1}{18}C\left(k^{4}\eta^{3}\right),

(390)

\displaystyle h=C(k\eta)^{2},\quad\varepsilon=2C-\frac{1}{18}C(k\eta)^{2},

(391)

where
C=\mathcal{R}/2
.
These initial conditions hold for radiation domination, which is why we start the numerical evolution long before the onset of PBH domination. To translate back to Newtonian gauge, where the metric in the absence of anisotropies is

ds^{2}=a^{2}(\tau)\left[-(1+2\Psi)d\tau^{2}+(1-2\Phi)\delta_{ij}dx^{i}dx^{j}\right],

(392)

we use
[
261
,
302
,
211
]
:

\Phi=\varepsilon-\mathcal{H}\frac{(6\varepsilon+h)^{\prime}}{2k^{2}}.

(393)

Alternatively, we can perform the scalar gauge transformation
[
261
]

\displaystyle\Psi

\displaystyle=\mathcal{H}\alpha+\alpha^{\prime},\qquad\Phi=\eta-\mathcal{H}\alpha,

(394)

\displaystyle\delta^{(n)}

\displaystyle=\delta^{(s)}+\frac{\bar{\rho}^{\prime}}{\bar{\rho}}\alpha,\qquad\theta^{(n)}=\theta^{(s)}+k^{2}\alpha,\qquad\alpha=\frac{(6\eta+h)^{\prime}}{2k^{2}},

(395)

where
\Psi=\Phi
in the absence of anisotropic stress. One obtains
[
302
]

\displaystyle\delta^{\prime}_{\rm m}

\displaystyle=-\theta_{\rm m}+3\Phi^{\prime}-\Gamma\,\Phi,

(396)

\displaystyle\theta^{\prime}_{\rm m}

\displaystyle=-\mathcal{H}\,\theta_{\rm m}+k^{2}\Phi,

(397)

\displaystyle\delta^{\prime}_{\rm r}

\displaystyle=-\frac{4}{3}\left(\theta_{\rm r}-3\Phi^{\prime}\right)+\frac{\left<\Gamma\rho_{\mathrm{m}}\right>}{\left<\rho_{\mathrm{r}}\right>}\left(\delta_{\rm m}-\delta_{\rm r}+\Phi\right),

(398)

\displaystyle\theta^{\prime}_{\rm r}

\displaystyle=\frac{k^{2}}{4}\delta_{\rm r}+k^{2}\Phi-\frac{3\left<\Gamma\rho_{\mathrm{m}}\right>}{4\left<\rho_{\mathrm{r}}\right>}\left(\frac{4}{3}\theta_{\rm r}-\theta_{\rm m}\right),

(399)

The Newtonian potential obeys Poisson’s relativistic equation
[
280
]

\Phi^{\prime}=-\frac{k^{2}\Phi+3\mathcal{H}^{2}\Phi+\frac{3}{2}\mathcal{H}^{2}\left(\frac{\left<\rho_{\mathrm{m}}\right>}{\left<\rho_{\mathrm{tot}}\right>}\delta_{\rm m}+\frac{\left<\rho_{\mathrm{r}}\right>}{\left<\rho_{\mathrm{tor}}\right>}\delta_{\rm r}\right)}{3\mathcal{H}},\qquad\rho_{\rm tot}=\rho_{\rm m}+\rho_{\rm r},

(400)

and the superhorizon adiabatic initial conditions used for numerical integration are

\delta_{{\rm m},{\rm ini}}=-2\Phi_{\rm ini},\qquad\delta_{{\rm r},{\rm ini}}=\frac{4}{3}\delta_{{\rm m},{\rm ini}},\qquad\theta_{{\rm m},{\rm ini}}=\theta_{{\rm r},{\rm ini}}=\frac{k^{2}\eta_{\rm ini}}{3}\Phi_{\rm ini}.

(401)

D.2
Suppression factor of the Newtonian potential

From the previous equations, we can calculate the Newtonian potential during PBH domination, evaporation and reheating. The amplitude right before it starts oscillating, during reheating, can be parametrized as (see Sec.
3.3
)

\Phi_{\rm osc}(k)=\bigl[T_{\Phi}(\kappa)\,\Phi_{\bf k}(0)+T_{S}(\kappa)\,S_{\bf k}(0)\bigr]\,\mathcal{S}_{\Phi}(k),

(402)

where
T_{\Phi}
and
T_{S}
are the usual transfer functions for modes entering the
horizon during the early radiation era, and
\mathcal{S}_{\Phi}(k)
is a
suppression factor encoding the decay of
\Phi
during PBH evaporation.
Its physical origin is transparent from the subhorizon Poisson equation

k^{2}\Phi\simeq\frac{3}{2}\,\mathcal{H}^{2}\left(\frac{\langle\rho_{\rm{PBH}}\rangle}{\langle\rho_{\rm tot}\rangle}\,\delta_{\rm m}+\frac{\langle\rho_{\rm r}\rangle}{\langle\rho_{\rm tot}\rangle}\,\delta_{\rm r}\right),

(403)

which shows that
\Phi
is maintained constant by PBH isocurvature during the early
matter–dominated phase, and is driven into oscillations once the PBH
fraction decays and the radiation contribution takes over.
We can also understand the suppression factor from the evolution equation for
\Phi
, cf. Eq. (
349
), expressed directly in terms of the background fractions
\langle\Omega_{\rm m}\rangle
and
\langle\Omega_{\rm r}\rangle
, where
\left<\cdot\right>
denotes the averaging over the PBH mass distribution:

\Phi^{\prime\prime}+\left(\frac{9+7\left<\Omega_{r}\right>}{3+\left<\Omega_{r}\right>}\right)\mathcal{H}\Phi^{\prime}+\left<\Omega_{r}\right>\left(\frac{3(1-\left<\Omega_{r}\right>)\mathcal{H}^{2}+4k^{2}}{3(3+\left<\Omega_{r}\right>)}\right)\Phi=-\frac{2\left<\Omega_{m}\right>\left<\Omega_{r}\right>}{3+\left<\Omega_{r}\right>}\mathcal{H}^{2}\delta_{\rm{PBH}},

(404)

where we neglected
\delta_{r}\ll\delta_{\rm{PBH}}
.
During PBH domination,
\eta\ll\left<\eta_{\rm eva}\right>
, we have
\left<\Omega_{r}\right>\simeq 0
and
\left<\Omega_{m}\right>\simeq 1
such that Eq. (
404
) becomes

\eta\ll\left<\eta_{\rm eva}\right>:\qquad\Phi^{\prime\prime}+3\mathcal{H}\Phi^{\prime}\simeq 0,

(405)

and
\Phi
stays on a near-constant plateau,
\Phi^{\prime}\simeq 0
.
During PBH evaporation,
\eta\sim\left<\eta_{\rm eva}\right>
, we have
\left<\Omega_{m}\right>\propto\exp\left(-\int^{\eta}_{0}\!{\rm d}\tilde{\eta}\,\Gamma\right)
, cf. Eq. (
37
). Taking the sub-horizon limit
k\gg\mathcal{H}
, and approximating
\left<\Omega_{r}\right>\simeq 1
, in Eq. (
404
) gives

\eta\sim\left<\eta_{\rm eva}\right>:\qquad k^{2}\Phi=-\frac{3}{2}\mathcal{H}^{2}\left<\Omega_{m}\right>\delta_{\rm{PBH}}-3\left(\Phi^{\prime\prime}+4\mathcal{H}\Phi^{\prime}\right).

(406)

We define
\eta_{\rm osc}
the time when the two terms on the right hand side are equal to each other

-\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{H}^{2}\left<\Omega_{m}\right>\delta_{\rm{PBH}}(\eta_{\rm osc})\equiv\Phi^{\prime\prime}(\eta_{\rm osc})+4\mathcal{H}\Phi^{\prime}(\eta_{\rm osc}).

(407)

For
\eta\lesssim\eta_{\rm osc}
, the first term in Eq. (
406
) dominate leading to

\Phi\simeq-\frac{3}{2}\mathcal{H}^{2}\left<\Omega_{m}\right>\delta_{\rm{PBH}}\propto\left<\Omega_{m}\right>\propto\exp\left[-\int^{\eta}_{0}\!{\rm d}\tilde{\eta}\,\Gamma\right].

(408)

For
\eta\gtrsim\eta_{\rm osc}
, the second term in Eq. (
406
) dominates and we have

\Phi^{\prime\prime}+4\mathcal{H}\Phi^{\prime}+\frac{k^{2}}{3}\Phi\simeq 0,

(409)

whose solution,
\Phi\propto a^{-2}\cos(k\eta/\sqrt{3})
, features acoustic oscillations with a decaying envelope.
The suppression factor
\mathcal{S}_{\Phi}(k)
can be estimated as

\mathcal{S}_{\Phi}(k)\simeq\frac{\Phi(\eta_{\rm osc})}{\Phi(\eta\ll\eta_{\rm osc}))},

(410)

where
\Phi(\eta\ll\eta_{\rm osc})
is the plateau value before the exponential decrease, taken as a constant, and
\Phi(\eta_{\rm osc})
is the amplitude at the onset of the oscillation, estimated to occur at the time
\eta_{\rm osc}
. According to Eq. (
408
), during PBH evaporation before it starts oscillating, the Newtonian potential is proportional to

\eta\lesssim\eta_{\rm osc}:\qquad\Phi~\propto~\left<\Omega_{\rm m}\right>,

(411)

were
\left<\Omega_{\rm m}\right>
is the average of
\Omega_{\rm m}\equiv\rho_{m}/\rho_{\rm tot}
in Eq. (
37
) over the Choptuik’s mass distribution
\psi(M)
in Eq. (
31
):

\left<\Omega_{\rm m}\right>(\eta)=\int\!\frac{dM}{M}\psi(M)\Omega_{\rm m}(\eta;M)=\frac{1+\gamma_{\rm M}}{\gamma_{\rm M}}\int^{M_{\rm cut}}_{M(\eta)}\!\frac{dM}{M}\!\!\left(\frac{M}{M_{\rm cut}}\right)^{\!\frac{1}{\gamma_{\rm M}}+1}\!\!\left(1-\frac{t(\eta)}{t_{\rm eva}(M)}\right)^{\!1/3}.

(412)

The lower bound of the integral prevents the piece
1-t(\eta)/t_{\rm eva}(M)
to turn negative.
Given that
t_{\rm eva}\propto M^{3}
and
t(\eta)\propto\eta^{3}
during matter domination, we can change the integration variable from
M
to
\eta_{\rm eva}
using the relation
M\propto\eta_{\rm eva}
. Eq. (
412
) becomes

\left<\Omega_{\rm m}\right>(\eta)=\frac{1+\gamma_{\rm M}}{\gamma_{\rm M}}\int^{\eta_{\rm cut}}_{\eta}\frac{d\eta_{\rm eva}}{\eta_{\rm eva}}\left(\frac{\eta_{\rm eva}}{\eta_{\rm cut}}\right)^{\!1+1/\gamma_{\rm M}}\left(1-\frac{\eta^{3}}{\eta^{3}_{\rm eva}}\right)^{\!1/3},

(413)

where
\eta_{\rm cut}=(1+2\gamma_{\rm M})\left<\eta_{\rm eva}\right>/(1+\gamma_{\rm M})
is determined from
\int d\log(\eta_{\rm eva})\,\psi(\eta_{\rm eva})\,\eta_{\rm eva}=\left<\eta_{\rm eva}\right>
.
The integral has an exact analytical expression

\langle\Omega_{\rm m}\rangle=-(-1)^{2/3}(1+\gamma_{\rm M})\left.\frac{\eta}{\eta_{\rm eva}}\left(\frac{\eta_{\rm eva}}{\eta_{\rm cut}}\right)^{\!1+\frac{1}{\gamma_{\rm M}}}\!\!{}_{2}F_{1}\left(-\frac{1}{3},\frac{1}{3\gamma_{\rm M}};1+\frac{1}{3\gamma_{\rm M}};\frac{\eta_{\text{eva}}^{3}}{\eta^{3}}\right)\right|_{\eta_{\rm eva}=\eta}^{\eta_{\rm eva}=\eta_{\rm cut}},

(414)

where
{}_{2}F_{1}
denotes the hypergeometric function. Using the approximation
\Phi\propto\left<\Omega_{m}\right>
in Eq. (
411
), the suppression factor defined in Eq. (
410
) can be calculated analytically as

\mathcal{S}_{\Phi}(k)\simeq\left<\Omega_{m}(\eta_{\rm osc})\right>,

(415)

where
\left<\Omega_{m}(\eta)\right>
is given by Eq. (
80
) and where have used
\left<\Omega_{m}(\eta\ll\eta_{\rm osc})\right>=1
. The time
\eta_{\rm osc}
when
\Phi
starts oscillating can be determined analytically from plugging Eq. (
407
) into Eq. (
406
), which leads to

Figure 19:
Suppression factor
\mathcal{S}_{\Phi}(k)
of the Newtonian potential
\Phi
for different values of the
critical exponent
\gamma_{\rm M}
entering Choptuik’s PBH mass distribution
\psi(M)
in
Eq. (
31
).
We compare the analytical expression in Eq. (
415
) using

\eta_{\rm osc}
determined from Eq. (
417
) (
solid lines
) and
from Eq. (
416
) (
dashed lines
), which rely respectively on
the first and second derivatives of the Newtonian potential,
\Phi^{\prime}
and
\Phi^{\prime\prime}
, with
\Phi
given by Eq. (
80
).
The discontinuity in the dashed curves is caused by a sign change of
\Phi^{\prime\prime}
occurring near

\eta_{\rm osc}\simeq\eta_{\rm cut}(1-\gamma_{\rm M}/2)
.
This sign flip produces an abrupt shift in the solution value for
\eta_{\rm osc}
when using the
second-derivative condition of Eq. (
416
).
In contrast, the first-derivative criterion in Eq. (
417
) (solid lines)
eliminates this step-like behavior and yields a smooth suppression factor.

\left|\frac{\Phi^{\prime\prime}}{\Phi}\right|\simeq\frac{k^{2}}{6}\quad\text{at}\quad\eta=\eta_{\rm osc}(k).

(416)

where we have neglected
4\mathcal{H}\Phi^{\prime}/\Phi^{\prime\prime}\ll 1
which is valid for
k\gg\mathcal{H}
.
We note that the second derivative of
\Phi\propto\Omega_{m}
changes sign at

\eta_{\rm osc}=\eta_{\rm zero}
, defined by
\langle\Omega_{m}\rangle^{\prime\prime}(\eta_{\rm zero})=0
,
which occurs at
\eta_{\rm zero}\simeq\eta_{\rm cut}(1-\gamma_{M}/2)
.
As shown by the dashed curves in Fig.
19
, this produces a step-like behavior in the suppression factor
\mathcal{S}_{\Phi}(k)
when
k\langle\eta_{\rm eva}\rangle\sim\gamma_{M}^{-1}
.
Since the physical origin of these discontinuities is unclear, we avoid them by replacing the
second-derivative criterion with a first-derivative one, substituting
Eq. (
416
) with

\left|\frac{\Phi^{\prime}}{\Phi}\right|\simeq\sqrt{\frac{2}{3}}\,k\qquad\text{at}\qquad\eta=\eta_{\rm osc}(k),

(417)

where the factor
\sqrt{2/3}
is chosen such that
Eqs. (
416
) and (
417
) return the same
start-of-oscillation time
\eta_{\rm osc}
in the sub-horizon limit
k\langle\eta_{\rm eva}\rangle\gg 1
.
As illustrated by the solid curves in Fig.
19
, the suppression factor

\mathcal{S}_{\Phi}(k)
from Eq. (
415
), with
\eta_{\rm osc}
determined using
Eq. (
417
), now exhibits a smooth behavior.

D.3
High-
k
limit for the Choptuik mass function

We next derive an analytic estimate for the suppression factor
\mathcal{S}_{\Phi}(k)
in the limit

k\langle\eta_{\rm eva}\rangle\gg 1
, for which the start-of-oscillation time approaches the evaporation time
of the heaviest PBHs, i.e.
\eta_{\rm osc}\to\eta_{\rm cut}^{-}
.
Using Mathematica, we calculate the limit

\langle\Omega_{\rm m}\rangle~\xrightarrow[\eta\to\eta_{\rm cut}^{-}]{}~\frac{3^{4/3}}{4}\,\frac{1+\gamma_{\rm M}}{\gamma_{\rm M}}\left(1-\frac{\eta}{\eta_{\rm cut}}\right)^{4/3}.

(418)

We therefore deduce that the Newtonian potential scales as

\Phi\propto\langle\Omega_{\rm m}\rangle\propto(\eta_{\rm cut}-\eta)^{4/3}.

(419)

Substituting Eq. (
419
) into either
Eq. (
416
) or, equivalently,
Eq. (
417
), yields the time
\eta_{\rm osc}
at which
\Phi
starts oscillating

\frac{4}{9}\,(\eta_{\rm cut}-\eta_{\rm osc})^{-2}\simeq\frac{k^{2}}{6}\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad\eta_{\rm cut}-\eta_{\rm osc}\;\simeq\;\sqrt{\frac{8}{3}}\,k^{-1}.

(420)

Plugging Eq. (
420
) into
Eqs. (
418
) and (
415
)
yields the high-
k
limit of the suppression factor,

\mathcal{S}_{\Phi}(k)\simeq\kappa_{0}\left(\frac{2}{k\langle\eta_{\rm eva}\rangle}\right)^{4/3}\simeq\kappa_{0}\left(\frac{k_{\rm eva}}{k}\right)^{4/3},\qquad\kappa_{0}\equiv\frac{3^{2/3}(1+\gamma_{M})^{7/3}}{2^{4/3}\gamma_{M}(1+2\gamma_{M})^{4/3}}\simeq 2.3,

(421)

where we have used
k_{\rm eva}\equiv\mathcal{H}_{\rm eva}=2/\langle\eta_{\rm eva}\rangle
, following Eq. (
46
) for
\left<\eta_{\rm eva}\right>\gg\eta_{\rm eq}
,
together with
\eta_{\rm cut}/\langle\eta_{\rm eva}\rangle=(1+2\gamma_{\rm M})/(1+\gamma_{\rm M})
and
\gamma_{\rm M}\simeq 0.36
.
In Fig.
12
of the main text, we show that the analytic solution is validated against a full numerical integration of the
perturbation equations in the sub-horizon limit
k\left<\eta_{\rm eva}\right>\gg 1
.

D.4
Monochromatic limit

The expression in Eq. (
80
) can also be used to derive the suppression
factor in the monochromatic limit (
\gamma_{\rm M}\rightarrow 0
), as done in
Ref.
[
211
]
.
To this end, we expand the hypergeometric function
{}_{2}F_{1}
in the limit where its second and third
arguments become large:

{}_{2}F_{1}\!\left(-\frac{1}{3},\frac{1}{3\gamma_{\rm M}};\,1+\frac{1}{3\gamma_{\rm M}},\frac{\eta_{\rm eva}^{3}}{\eta^{3}}\right)\xrightarrow[\gamma_{\rm M}\to 0]{}\left(1-\frac{\eta_{\rm eva}^{3}}{\eta^{3}}\right)^{1/3},

(422)

which, when combined with the prefactor in Eq. (
80
), gives

\langle\Omega_{\rm m}\rangle\xrightarrow[\gamma_{\rm M}\to 0]{}(1+\gamma_{\rm M})\left(1-\frac{\eta^{3}}{\eta_{\rm cut}^{3}}\right)^{1/3}\;\propto\;\left(1-\frac{t}{t_{\rm cut}}\right)^{1/3},

(423)

where we used
\eta a=3t
to convert from cosmic to conformal time.
Substituting Eq. (
423
) into Eq. (
416
) with
\Phi\propto\langle\Omega_{\rm m}\rangle
,
yields the time
\eta_{\rm osc}
at which
\Phi
starts oscillating

\frac{\eta_{\rm cut}^{3}}{\eta_{\rm osc}^{3}}~\simeq~1+\frac{\sqrt{12}}{k\eta_{\rm osc}},

(424)

which after plugging nack into Eq. (
423
) gives the suppression factor

\mathcal{S}_{\Phi}(k)\xrightarrow[\gamma_{\rm M}\to 0]{}\left(\frac{\sqrt{12}}{k\langle\eta_{\rm eva}\rangle}\right)^{1/3},

(425)

where we used
\eta_{\rm cut}\simeq\langle\eta_{\rm eva}\rangle
in the monochromatic limit and
\eta_{\rm osc}\simeq\eta_{\rm cut}
in the high
k
limit.
This reproduces the scaling obtained in Ref.
[
211
]
for a monochromatic PBH mass
function.
As shown in Fig.
12
, this scaling is reached only for unrealistically small
values of
\gamma_{\rm M}
.
For small but finite
\gamma_{\rm M}
, we observe that the monochromatic scaling

\mathcal{S}_{\Phi}(k)\propto k^{-1/3}
in Eq. (
425
) transitions to the Choptuik-mass-function behavior

\mathcal{S}_{\Phi}(k)\propto k^{-4/3}
in Eq. (
421
) once

k\langle\eta_{\rm eva}\rangle~\gtrsim~k_{\rm trans}\langle\eta_{\rm eva}\rangle~\equiv~\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2^{1/3}\gamma_{\rm M}}\sim\gamma_{\rm M}^{-1},

(426)

where
k_{\rm trans}
is defined as the value of
k
for which Eqs. (
421
) and (
425
) intersect, expanded in the limit
\gamma_{\rm M}\ll 1
.

The transition scale
k_{\rm trans}
in Eq. (
426
) may also be identified as the wavenumber at which the next-to-leading–order correction to the monochromatic limit in Eq. (
422
) becomes non-negligible.
We need to expand

{}_{2}F_{1}\!\left(-\frac{1}{3},\frac{1}{3\gamma_{M}};1+\frac{1}{3\gamma_{M}};z\right),\qquad z=\left(\frac{\eta_{\rm cut}}{\eta}\right)^{3},

(427)

for
\gamma_{M}\to 0
. First we use

{}_{2}F_{1}(a,b,c,z)=(1-z)^{-a}_{2}F_{1}\left(a,c-b,c,\frac{z}{z-1}\right)

(428)

to write

_{2}F_{1}\left(-\frac{1}{3},\frac{1}{3\gamma_{M}},1+\frac{1}{3\gamma_{M}},z\right)=\left|(1-z)^{1/3}\ _{2}F_{1}\left(-\frac{1}{3},1,1+\frac{1}{3\gamma_{M}},\frac{z}{z-1}\right)\right|

(429)

and move the singular
1/\gamma_{M}
to the third argument only. We added such absolute values for the case of
z>1
. As we will show, the limit
\gamma_{M}\rightarrow 0
is purely real, such that we can drop the absolute value. Next, we employ

{}_{2}F_{1}(\tilde{a},\tilde{b},\tilde{c}+\lambda,\tilde{z})\xrightarrow{\lambda\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\Gamma(\tilde{c}+\lambda)}{\Gamma(\tilde{c}-\tilde{b}+\lambda)}\sum_{s=0}^{\infty}q_{s}(\tilde{z})(\tilde{b})_{s}\lambda^{-s-\tilde{b}},

(430)

where we identify
(\tilde{a},\tilde{b},\tilde{c},\lambda,\tilde{z})
as
(-1/3,1,1,1/(3\gamma_{M}),z/(z-1))
. The Pochhammer symbols
(\tilde{b})_{s}=\tilde{b}(\tilde{b}+1)\dots(\tilde{b}+s-1)
take the values
(\tilde{b})_{0}=1
and
(\tilde{b})_{1}=1
, respectively. The coefficients
q_{s}(\tilde{z})
are defined from the generating function order-by-order

\left(\frac{\mathrm{e}^{t}-1}{t}\right)^{\tilde{b}-1}\mathrm{e}^{t(1-\tilde{c})}\left(1-z+z\mathrm{e}^{-t}\right)^{-\tilde{c}}=\sum_{s=0}^{\infty}q_{s}(\tilde{z})t^{s}.

(431)

At next-to-leading order we find

1+\frac{1}{2}(1+\tilde{b}-2\tilde{c}+2\tilde{a}\tilde{z})t+\mathcal{O}(t^{2})=q_{0}(\tilde{z})+q_{1}(\tilde{z})t+\mathcal{O}(t^{2}).

(432)

Comparing coefficient order-by-order yields
q_{0}(\tilde{z})=1
and
q_{1}(\tilde{z})=-1/3\tilde{z}
. With this we find at next-to-leading order

\frac{\Gamma(\tilde{c}+\lambda)}{\Gamma(\tilde{c}-\tilde{b}+\lambda)}\sum_{s=0}^{\infty}q_{s}(\tilde{z})(\tilde{b})_{s}\lambda^{-s-\tilde{b}}\simeq\frac{1}{\lambda}(\lambda-\frac{1}{3}\tilde{z})+\mathcal{O}(\lambda^{-2}),

(433)

which, after substituting into Eq. (
429
) and replacing
(\tilde{a},\tilde{b},\tilde{c},\lambda,\tilde{z})
with
(-1/3,1,1,1/(3\gamma_{M}),z/(z-1))
, yields

_{2}F_{1}(-\frac{1}{3},\frac{1}{3\gamma_{M}},1+\frac{1}{3\gamma_{M}},z)=(1-z)^{1/3}\left|1-\gamma_{M}\frac{z}{z-1}\right|+\mathcal{O}(\gamma_{M}^{2}),

(434)

The monochromatic behaviour persists until these
corrections become order unity.
Setting the NLO correction equal to a
fraction
\Delta={\cal O}(1)
,

\gamma_{M}\frac{z}{z-1}\simeq\Delta,

(435)

defines the transition time
\eta_{\rm trans}
. Using
z=\left({\eta_{\rm cut}}/{\eta_{\rm osc}}\right)^{3}
with
\eta_{\rm osc}
given by Eq. (
424
) and
\eta_{\rm osc}\simeq\left<\eta_{\rm eva}\right>
yields the transition scale

k_{\rm trans}\,\langle\eta_{\rm eva}\rangle\simeq\frac{\sqrt{12}\,\Delta}{\gamma_{M}},

(436)

which matches Eq. (
426
) for
\Delta\simeq 0.4
.

Appendix E
Green’s function for tensor modes

We derive the retarded Green’s function
G_{h}(x,\bar{x})
for the tensor
equation of motion

G_{h}^{\prime\prime}(x,\bar{x})+2\frac{\mathcal{H}}{k}\,G_{h}^{\prime}(x,\bar{x})+G_{h}(x,\bar{x})=\delta(x-\bar{x}),

(437)

cf. Eq. (
97
), across the
matter-to-radiation transition induced by PBH evaporation at

\eta_{\rm eva}\equiv\langle\eta_{\rm eva}\rangle
,

{\rm eMD}\;\longrightarrow\;{\rm RD}_{2}\,,

(438)

see Eq. (
118
).
We further define
x_{\rm eva}\equiv k\eta_{\rm eva}
for the
corresponding dimensionless time.
Primes denote derivatives with respect to
x\equiv k\eta
and

\bar{x}\equiv k\bar{\eta}
, and the retarded boundary condition

G_{h}(x,\bar{x})=0
for
x<\bar{x}
is imposed throughout.

E.1
Background

In the limit of a prolonged matter-dominated era,

\eta_{\rm eq}\ll\eta_{\rm eva}

(equivalently
N_{\rm MD}\gg 1
), the scale factor in
Eq. (
45
) simplifies to the piecewise form

\frac{a(\eta)}{a_{\rm eq}}\;\simeq\;\begin{cases}\left(\dfrac{\eta}{\eta_{\star}}\right)^{2},&\eta\leq\eta_{\rm eva},\\[10.0pt]
\dfrac{(2\eta-\eta_{\rm eva})\,\eta_{\rm eva}}{\eta_{\star}^{2}},&\eta>\eta_{\rm eva},\end{cases}

(439)

where
\eta_{\star}\equiv\eta_{\rm eq}/(\sqrt{2}-1)
.
In the same limit, the conformal Hubble parameter takes the form

\frac{\mathcal{H}}{k}\;\simeq\;\begin{cases}\dfrac{2}{x},&x\leq x_{\rm eva}\quad(\text{eMD}),\\[10.0pt]
\dfrac{1}{2x-x_{\rm eva}},&x>x_{\rm eva}\quad(\mathrm{RD}_{2}).\end{cases}

(440)

E.2
Homogeneous solutions within each phase

Within each constant-
\omega
phase the tensor Green’s function
is given by Eq. (
98
),

G_{h}(x,\bar{x})=\frac{\pi}{2}\,\frac{\bar{x}^{\,b+3/2}}{x^{\,b+1/2}}\left[J_{b+1/2}(\bar{x})\,Y_{b+1/2}(x)-J_{b+1/2}(x)\,Y_{b+1/2}(\bar{x})\right]\Theta(x-\bar{x}),

(441)

where
b\equiv(1-3\omega)/(1+3\omega)
, and
J_{\alpha}
and
Y_{\alpha}

denote the Bessel functions of the first and second kind, respectively.
We now specialise to each phase in turn.

•

\rm RD_{1}
phase
(
\omega=1/3
,
b=0
).
Inserting
b=0
into Eq. (
441
)
the Green’s function reduces to

G_{h}^{\mathrm{RD_{1}}}(x,\bar{x})=\frac{\bar{x}}{x}\sin(x-\bar{x})\Theta(x-\bar{x}).

(442)

•

\rm eMD
phase
(
\omega=0
,
b=1
).
Inserting
b=1
into Eq. (
441
) and using the
identities
J_{3/2}(z)=\sqrt{2/(\pi z)}\,(\sin z/z-\cos z)
and

Y_{3/2}(z)=-\sqrt{2/(\pi z)}\,(\cos z/z+\sin z)
,
the Green’s function reduces to

G^{\rm(eMD)}_{h}(x,\bar{x})=\frac{\bar{x}}{x^{3}}\Bigl[(1+x\bar{x})\sin(x-\bar{x})+(\bar{x}-x)\cos(x-\bar{x})\Bigr]\Theta(x-\bar{x}).

(443)

•

\mathrm{RD}_{2}
phase
(
\omega=1/3
,
b=0
).
After evaporation the scale factor reads
a\propto\eta-\eta_{\rm eva}/2

(see footnote
7
and Eq. (
45
)),
so the conformal Hubble parameter is

\mathcal{H}/k=(x-x_{\rm eva}/2)^{-1}
.
The two independent homogeneous solutions are

u_{1}(x)=\frac{\sin(x-x_{\rm eva}/2)}{x-x_{\rm eva}/2},\qquad u_{2}(x)=\frac{\cos(x-x_{\rm eva}/2)}{x-x_{\rm eva}/2},

(444)

and imposing the retarded jump condition at
x=\bar{x}
yields

G^{\rm(RD_{2})}_{h}(x,\bar{x})=\frac{\bar{x}-x_{\rm eva}/2}{x-x_{\rm eva}/2}\,\sin(x-\bar{x})\,\Theta(x-\bar{x}),\qquad x>\bar{x}>x_{\rm eva}.

(445)

Compared to the standard RD result obtained from Eq. (
441
) for
b=0
in Eq. (
442
), the effect of the prior
\rm eMD
phase is a shift in conformal time,

\tilde{x}=x-\frac{x_{\mathrm{eva}}}{2},\qquad\tilde{\bar{x}}=\bar{x}-\frac{x_{\mathrm{eva}}}{2},

(446)

such that

G_{h}^{\mathrm{RD_{2}}}(x,\bar{x})\;\rightarrow\;\frac{\tilde{\bar{x}}}{\tilde{x}}\sin(\tilde{x}-\tilde{\bar{x}}).

(447)

E.3
Sourced in eMD, evaluated in

\mathrm{RD}_{2}

For the eMD contribution to the SIGW spectrum (Sec.
6
),
the relevant configuration has the scalar source acting during the
eMD phase and the tensor mode evaluated after evaporation,
i.e.
\bar{x}<x_{\rm eva}<x
. In this case
G_{h}
must be constructed
by matching
G^{\rm(eMD)}_{h}
to a linear combination of the
\mathrm{RD}_{2}

homogeneous solutions
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
at the transition
x=x_{\rm eva}
,

G^{\rm(eMD\to RD_{2})}_{h}(x,\bar{x})=\frac{A(\bar{x})\cos(x-x_{\rm eva})+B(\bar{x})\sin(x-x_{\rm eva})}{x-x_{\rm eva}/2},\qquad\bar{x}<x_{\rm eva}<x.

(448)

The matching coefficients
A(\bar{x})
and
B(\bar{x})
are fixed by
requiring continuity of
G_{h}
and
G_{h}^{\prime}
at
x=x_{\rm eva}
,
26
26

26
